Scaling limit of the range of tree-valued branching random walks in random environment
Thomas Duquesne, Robin Khanfir

Abstract
We study a branching random walk (BRW) taking its values in a random tree 𝐓 \mathbf{T} (seen as a family tree) with an infinite line of ancestors that is a variant of a supercritical Galton–Watson (GW) tree with offspring distribution ν \nu . The transition probabilities of the BRW are those of a critical biased random walk on 𝐓 \mathbf{T} : namely, the probability to move from x x to one of its k x k_{x} children is 1 / ( 𝚖 ν + k x ) 1/(\mathtt{m}_{\nu}+k_{x}) and the probability to move from x x to the direct parent of x x is 𝚖 ν / ( 𝚖 ν + k x ) \mathtt{m}_{\nu}/(\mathtt{m}_{\nu}+k_{x}) . Here 𝚖 ν \mathtt{m}_{\nu} stands for the mean of ν \nu . The BRW is indexed by a critical GW tree conditioned to have n n vertices and whose offspring distribution is in the domain of attraction of an α \alpha -stable law with α ∈ ( 1 , 2 ] \alpha\!\in\!(1,2] . We denote by ℛ n \mathcal{R}_{n} the range of the BRW, i.e., the set of all sites in 𝐓 \mathbf{T} visited by the BRW. Under a moment assumption for ν \nu , we prove that if we view ℛ n \mathcal{R}_{n} as a random subtree of 𝐓 \mathbf{T} equipped with its graph distance d 𝚐𝚛 d_{\mathtt{gr}} and with its occupation measure 𝚖 ( n ) 𝚘𝚌𝚌 \mathtt{m}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{\mathtt{occ}}} then there exists a scaling sequence s n → ∞ s_{n}\!\to\!\infty such that conditionally given the environment 𝐓 \mathbf{T} , the measured metric space ( ℛ n , s n − 1 d 𝚐𝚛 , 1 n 𝚖 𝚘𝚌𝚌 ( n ) ) (\mathcal{R}_{n},s_{n}^{-1}d_{\mathtt{gr}},\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{n}}\mathtt{m}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{\mathtt{occ}}}) weakly converges in the Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov sense to a random measured compact real tree introduced by Curien, Le Gall & Miermont in [ 15 ] called the Brownian cactus with α \alpha -stable branching mechanism. This work extends in random environment the result from D., K., Lin & Torri [ 20 ] which deals with the case where 𝐓 \mathbf{T} is a regular tree. Keywords Branching random walks ⋅ \cdot Galton–Watson tree ⋅ \cdot Random environment ⋅ \cdot Scaling limit ⋅ \cdot Superprocess ⋅ \cdot Brownian snake ⋅ \cdot Brownian cactus ⋅ \cdot Real tree Mathematics Subject Classification 60J80 ⋅ \cdot 60G50 ⋅ \cdot 60G52 ⋅ \cdot 60F17



1
Introduction

Branching random walks (BRW) represent a simple model to study a branching behavior coupled with some spatial displacements. As such, they drew a lot of interest and are naturally linked with various areas of research. Let us mention at least superprocesses, traveling wave solutions of semi-linear partial differential equations (FKPP), and some statistical mechanics models such as Mandelbrot cascades, the contact process, the Gaussian free field, or the voter model. We refer to Shi
[
47
]
for an overview of this topic and its connections. We also cite the study of extreme particles of real-valued BRWs by Biggins
[
7
]
and Bramson
[
12
,
11
]
, the works of Gouëzel, Hueter, Lalley, Sellke
[
34
,
26
,
28
,
33
]
about BRWs with values in hyperbolic spaces, and the study of BRWs on trees of
Benjamini & Müller
[
5
]
and Liggett
[
40
]
.

In contrast to those works where the indexing tree is infinite, D., K., Lin & Torri
[
20
]
consider a BRW that is indexed by a critical Galton–Watson (GW) tree conditioned to have
n
vertices. In
[
20
]
, the BRW moves like a nearest-neighbor null-recurrent random walk (RW) on a deterministic rooted regular tree: it jumps to the parent of the current position with probability
\frac{1}{2}
, or it chooses uniformly one of the sites above otherwise. With this setting, D., K., Lin & Torri
[
20
]
describe the asymptotic behavior of the range of the BRW (i.e.,the set of all visited sites) as
n\!\to\!\infty
by showing that its scaling limit is a variant of the Brownian cactus introduced by Curien, Le Gall & Miermont
[
15
]
. Here, we obtain a similar result when the environment is instead a random tree with infinite depth.

We now give an informal description of the environment and the transitions of the BRWs that we consider in this paper. We fix a probability distribution
\nu=(\nu(k))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
on
\mathbb{N}
that we assume to be supercritical with a finite moment of order
2
:

\mathtt{m}_{\nu}:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\nu(k)\in(1,\infty)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\sigma_{\nu}^{2}:=\frac{1}{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}(\mathtt{m}_{\nu}-1)}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k(k-1)\nu(k)\in(0,\infty).

(1.1)

We denote by
\mathbf{T}
the environment and by
\mathtt{o}\in\mathbf{T}
the starting point of the BRW. One can understand
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
as a GW tree with offspring distribution
\nu
that is modified so that the distinguished point
\mathtt{o}
has an infinite line of ancestors: informally,
the numbers of children
k_{x}(\mathbf{T})
of the vertices
x\in\mathbf{T}
are independent; outside of the ancestral line of
\mathtt{o}
, their common law is
\nu
; on the ancestral line of
\mathtt{o}
, their law is the biased probability
distribution
(k\nu(k)/\mathtt{m}_{\nu})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.
We say that
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
is an
infinite GW tree with offspring distribution
\nu
and we refer to Definition
2.16
for a more precise construction.
For all
x\in\mathbf{T}
, let
\overleftarrow{x}
stand for the parent of
x
(which is always well-defined since
\mathbf{T}
has an infinite line of ancestors).

The BRW on
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
we are interested in shares its spatial displacements with the so-called critical biased RW. For all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
, the
\lambda
-biased RW on
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
is the Markov chain whose transition probabilities are expressed by

p_{\mathbf{T},\lambda}(x,y)=\bm{1}_{\{\overleftarrow{y}=x\}}\frac{1}{\lambda+k_{x}(\mathbf{T})}+\bm{1}_{\{\overleftarrow{x}=y\}}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda+k_{x}(\mathbf{T})}

(1.2)

for all
x,y\in\mathbf{T}
.
This model has been introduced by Lyons
[
41
]
who also showed that the behavior of biased RWs is subject to a phase transition concerning the parameter
\lambda
: we refer to Section
2.3
for a more detailed account. We shall only consider the case
\lambda=\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
which is critical in the sense that a

\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RW on an ordinary GW tree with offspring distribution
\nu
is null-recurrent.

The present paper is related to earlier works about scaling limits for the range of tree-valued critical or near-critical biased RW. First, D.
[
19
]
studies near-critical biased RWs on regular trees and show that their rescaled ranges converge to a CRT coded by Brownian motions with drift.
Then, Peres & Zeitouni
[
45
]
prove that the process of the distances to the root of a critical biased RW in a supercritical GW tree without leaves is diffusive. Similarly, Dembo & Sun
[
16
]
treat the cases of critical biased RW on multi-type GW trees. Furthermore, Aïdékon & de Raphélis
[
2
]
strengthen Peres & Zeitouni’s result and show that the rescaled range of the same RW converges to a variant of the Brownian CRT. We also mention Chen & Miermont
[
14
]
who show, using previous results due to Bougerol & Jeulin
[
10
]
, that the scaling limits of Brownian bridges and loops in hyperbolic spaces are the Brownian CRT. Independently, Stewart proves in his Ph.D. thesis
[
49
]
that the rescaled simple RW bridges on a
\mathtt{b}
-regular tree (with
\mathtt{b}\geq 3
) converge to the Brownian CRT.

Now, let us discuss the assumptions we make on the genealogy of the BRWs, which is always supposed to be independent of the environment
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
. We fix another probability measure
\mu\!=\!(\mu(k))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
on
\mathbb{N}
. We denote by
\tau
a (rooted and ordered) GW tree with offspring distribution
\mu
that satisfies the following with
\alpha\!\in\!(1,2]
:

\begin{cases}(\mathrm{H}_{1}):&\;\mu(0)+\mu(1)<1\quad\textrm{and}\quad\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\mu(k)=1,\\
(\mathrm{H}_{2}):&\;\textrm{$\mu$ is aperiodic, {(namely, $\mu$ is not supported by a proper subgroup of $\mathbb{Z}$),}}\\
(\mathrm{H}_{3}):&\;\textrm{$\mu$ is in the domain of attraction of an $\alpha$-stable law. }\end{cases}

(1.3)

The assumption
(\mathrm{H}_{1})
implies that the number of vertices
\#\tau
of
\tau
is almost surely finite. The assumption
(\mathrm{H}_{2})
is convenient to work with because it implies that
{\bf P}(\#\tau\!=\!n)\!>\!0
for all integers
n
sufficiently large. We recall that
(\mathrm{H}_{3})
translates into the following assertion: if
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a RW on
\mathbb{Z}
starting at
V_{0}\!=\!0
and whose jump law is given by
{\bf P}(V_{1}\!=\!k)\!=\!\mu(k+1)
for all
k\!\in\!\{-1\}\!\cup\!\mathbb{N}
, then there exists a
\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}
-regularly varying sequence
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\tfrac{a_{n}}{n}V_{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\textrm{(law)}}X_{1}\;,\quad\textrm{where $\log{\bf E}[\exp(-\lambda X_{1})]=\lambda^{\alpha}$ for all $\lambda\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}$}\;.

(1.4)

We refer to Section
2.5
for more details on these assumptions.

We next define the BRW we consider as follows.
For all sufficiently large
n
, we denote by
\tau_{n}
a tree with the same law as a GW tree
\tau
with offspring distribution
\mu
under
{\bf P}(\,\cdot\,\big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\big)
. We denote by
\varnothing
the root of
\tau_{n}
and to each vertex
u\!\in\!\tau_{n}
, we associate a spatial position
Y^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!u}}\!\in\!\mathbf{T}
whose conditional joint law given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
and
\tau_{n}
is
defined by

{\bf P}\big(\,\forall u\in\tau_{n},\;Y^{(n)}_{u}\!=\!x_{u}\,\big|\,(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o}),\tau_{n}\,\big)={\bf 1}_{\{Y^{(n)}_{\varnothing}=\mathtt{o}\}}\!\!\!\prod_{u\in\tau_{n}\backslash\{\varnothing\}}\!\!\!\!p_{\mathbf{T},\mathtt{m}_{\nu}}(x_{\overleftarrow{u}},x_{u})

(1.5)

for all
x_{u}\!\in\!\mathbf{T}
and
u\!\in\!\tau_{n}
(here
\overleftarrow{u}
stands for the direct parent of
u
in
\tau_{n}
).

It is convenient to see
\tau_{n}
as a family tree whose ancestor corresponds to the root
\varnothing

and where siblings are ordered by birth-rank. The depth-first exploration of
\tau_{n}
is
the sequence of vertices
(u_{j})_{0\leq j<\#\tau}
that is defined recursively as follows:

u_{0}
is the root
\varnothing
and for all
j\!\in\!\{0,\ldots,\#\tau\!-\!2\}
, if
v
is the most recent ancestor of
u_{j}
having at least one unexplored child then
u_{j+1}
is the unexplored child of
v
with least birth-rank (see Section
2.1
for a more precise construction).

Our main result concerns a quenched limit of the following rescaled random pointed measured metric spaces

\bm{\tau}_{n}:=\big(\tau_{n},\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}d_{\mathtt{gr}},\varnothing,\tfrac{1}{n}\mathtt{m}_{n}\big)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\bm{\mathcal{R}}_{n}:=\big(\mathcal{R}_{n},\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}d_{\mathtt{gr}},\mathtt{o},\tfrac{1}{n}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{occ}}^{(n)}\big)

(1.6)

where
d_{\mathtt{gr}}
stands for the graph distance in
\tau_{n}
and in
\mathbf{T}
, where we recall from (
1.1
) the definition of
\sigma_{\nu}
and where we have set:

\mathcal{R}_{n}=\big\{Y_{v}\,;\,v\!\in\!\tau_{n}\big\},\quad\mathtt{m}_{n}\!=\!\sum_{v\in\tau_{n}}\delta_{v},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{occ}}^{(n)}=\sum_{v\in\tau_{n}}\delta_{Y^{(n)}_{v}}.

Let us first recall the scaling limit of
\bm{\tau}_{n}
;
\bm{\tau}_{n}
is encoded by its
height process

(H_{s}^{{}_{(n)}})_{s\in[0,n]}
, i.e., the continuous interpolation of the discrete-time process
H^{{}_{(n)}}_{j}\!=\!d_{\mathtt{gr}}(\varnothing,u_{j})
which is the height in
\tau_{n}
of the
j
-th vertex of

\tau_{n}
in the depth-first exploration.
Then, Theorem 3.1 in D.
[
18
]
asserts the following:

we assume that (
1.5
) holds and let
(a_{n})
be as in (
1.4
); then there is a nonnegative continuous process
H\!=\!(H_{s})_{s\in[0,1]}
, whose law only depends on
\alpha
and such that the convergence

\big(\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}H_{ns}^{(n)}\big)_{s\in[0,1]}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}(H_{s})_{s\in[0,1]}

(1.7)

holds weakly for the topology of uniform convergence
. Under the stronger assumption that the variance of
\mu
is finite, this result is due to Aldous
[
3
, Theorem 23]
. The process
H
is the normalized excursion of the
\alpha
-stable height process, which is a local-time function of an
\alpha
-stable spectrally positive Lévy process. In the case
\alpha\!=\!2
,
H
is more precisely the normalized Brownian excursion. We refer to Section
2.5
for precise definitions.

As in the discrete setting,
H
actually encodes a random pointed compact measured metric space that is defined as follows: we define a pseudometric
d_{H}
on
[0,1]
from
H
by setting

\forall 0\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq 1,\quad d_{H}(s_{1},s_{2})\!=\!d_{H}(s_{2},s_{1})\!=\!H_{s_{1}}+H_{s_{2}}-2\min_{s\in[s_{1},s_{2}]}H_{s}\,,

(1.8)

and we introduce the relation
\sim_{H}
on
[0,1]
by setting
s_{1}\sim_{H}s_{2}
if and only if
d_{H}(s_{1},s_{2})\!=\!0
;
clearly,
\sim_{H}
is an equivalence relation and the normalized
\alpha
-stable Lévy tree is taken as the quotient space
T_{H}\!=\![0,1]/\!\sim_{H}
, equipped with the distance induced by
d_{H}
that we keep denoting
d_{H}
. We denote by
p_{H}\!:\![0,1]\rightarrow T_{H}
the canonical projection. Note that
p_{H}
is continuous; therefore
T_{H}
is compact and connected. Moreover,
T_{H}
is a real tree, namely, a metric space such that all pairs of points are joined by a unique simple arc that turns out to be a geodesic (see Section
2.6
for more details). We set
\mathtt{r}_{H}\!:=\!p_{H}(0)
that is viewed as the root of
T_{H}
and we equip
T_{H}
with the measure
\mathtt{m}_{H}
that is the image of the Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]
via
p_{H}
, namely,
\int_{T_{H}}\!f\,d\mathtt{m}_{H}\!=\!\int_{0}^{1}\!f(p_{H}(s))\,ds
, for all
continuous
f\!:\!T_{H}\!\rightarrow\!\mathbb{R}
. The convergence (
1.7
)
then implies the following one:

\bm{\tau}_{n}=\big(\tau_{n},\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}d_{\mathtt{gr}},\varnothing,\tfrac{1}{n}\mathtt{m}_{n}\big)\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\;(T_{H},d_{H},\mathtt{r}_{H},\mathtt{m}_{H})\,.

(1.9)

Here the convergence holds weakly on the space
\mathbb{M}
of isometry classes of pointed measured compact metric spaces equipped with the Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov distance

{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}
that makes it a Polish space, as proved in Abraham, Delmas & Hoscheit in
[
1
]
, Theorem 2.5 (see Section
2.6
for precise definitions).

Then the limit of
\bm{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
is constructed as
follows: as proved in D. & Le Gall
[
22
]
(Lemma 6.4 p. 600)
conditionally given
H
, there exists a Hölder-continuous centered Gaussian process
\sigma\!\in\!T_{H}\!\longmapsto\!W_{\sigma}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}
whose covariance is characterized by

{\bf E}\big[\big|W_{\sigma_{1}}\!-W_{\sigma_{2}}\big|^{2}\big|\,H\big]\!=\!d_{H}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
, for all
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\!\in\!T_{H}
. Then, we set

\forall\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\!\in\!T_{H},\quad d_{H,W}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})=W_{\sigma_{1}}+W_{\sigma_{2}}-2\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\min_{\hskip 8.19447pt\sigma\in[\![\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}]\!]}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!W_{\sigma},

where
[\![\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}]\!]
is the unique geodesic that joins
\sigma_{1}
to

\sigma_{2}
in
T_{H}
.
As proved in D., K., Lin & Torri
[
20
]
,
d_{H,W}
is a pseudometric on
T_{H}
;
we then define the equivalence relation
\sim_{H,W}
on
T_{H}
by setting
\sigma_{1}\sim_{H,W}\sigma_{2}
if and only if

d_{H,W}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\!=\!0
and we denote by
T_{H,W}\!=\!T_{H}/\!\sim_{H,W}
the quotient metric space and we keep denoting by

d_{H,W}
the resulting metric; we denote by
\pi_{H,W}\!:\!T_{H}\!\rightarrow\!T_{H,W}
the canonical projection that is continuous. Thus
T_{H,W}
is compact and connected, and

(T_{H,W},d_{H,W})
is a real tree (see Section
2.8
for more details).
It turns out that this kind of spaces has been introduced in Curien, Le Gall & Miermont
[
15
]
(see also Le Gall
[
37
]
for a different purpose);
they coined the name Brownian cactus, so we call
(T_{H,W},d_{H,W})
the normalized Brownian cactus with
\alpha
-stable branching mechanism.
We next set
\mathtt{r}_{H,W}\!=\!\pi_{H,W}(\mathtt{r}_{H})
that is viewed as the root of
T_{H,W}

and we equip
T_{H,W}
with the measure
\mathtt{m}_{H,W}
that is the image
of
\mathtt{m}_{H}
via
\pi_{H,W}
: namely,
\int_{T_{H,W}}\!f\,d\mathtt{m}_{H,W}\!=\!\int_{T_{H}}\!f(\pi_{H,W}(\sigma))\,\mathtt{m}_{H}(d\sigma)
, for all continuous
f\!:\!T_{H,W}\!\rightarrow\!\mathbb{R}
. Our main result is the following limit theorem.

Theorem 1.1
.

We keep the above notation: recall that
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
is an infinite GW tree whose offspring distribution
\nu
is supercritical, that we denote by
\tau_{n}
is a GW tree with offspring distribution
\mu
conditioned to have
n
vertices for all sufficiently large
n
, and that
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
and
\tau_{n}
are independent. We assume that
\mu
satisfies (
1.3
) and that
\nu
satisfies (
1.1
). We furthermore assume that there exists
\mathtt{b}\!\in\!\big(\tfrac{2\alpha}{\alpha-1},\infty\big)
such that
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k^{1+2\mathtt{b}}\nu(k)\!<\!\infty
.
Recall from (
1.6
) the definition of the spaces
\bm{\tau}_{n}
and
\bm{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
.
We denote by
(T_{H},d_{H},\mathtt{r}_{h},\mathtt{m}_{H})
the normalized
\alpha
-stable Lévy tree and by

(T_{H,W},d_{H,W},\mathtt{r}_{H,W},\mathtt{m}_{H,W})
the normalized Brownian cactus with
\alpha
-stable branching mechanism as defined above. Recall that
\mathbb{M}
is the space
of isometry classes of pointed measured compact metric spaces equipped with the Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov distance.
Then,
conditionally given the environment

(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
, the following joint convergence

\big(\bm{\tau}_{n},\bm{\mathcal{R}}_{n}\big)\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\Big(\big(T_{H},d_{H},\mathtt{r}_{H},\mathtt{m}_{H}\big)\,;\,\big(T_{H,W},d_{H,W},\mathtt{r}_{H,W},\mathtt{m}_{H,W}\big)\Big)

(1.10)

holds weaky on
\mathbb{M}^{2}
equipped with the product topology.

The proof of Theorem
1.1
is done by proving an analog of (
1.7
) for the BRW. Namely, we introduce the discrete snake associated with the BRW
(Y^{(n)}_{u})_{u\in\tau_{n}}
: this process
(\widehat{W}^{(n)}_{s})_{s\in[0,n]}
is the continuous interpolation of the discrete-time process

\widehat{W}^{(n)}_{j}=\;\textrm{generation in $\mathbf{T}$ of $Y_{u_{j}}^{(n)}$ }

where
(u_{j})_{0\leq j<n}
is the depth-first exploration of
\tau_{n}
. Here, by convention, the generation of
\mathtt{o}
is taken equal to
0
, so the generation of
x
in
\mathbf{T}
is a possibly negative integer. Then the following is proved in Theorem
4.1
: conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
the following convergence

\big(\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}H_{ns}^{(n)},\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{W}_{ns}^{(n)}\big)_{s\in[0,1]}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}(H,\widehat{W})

(1.11)

holds weakly on the space
\mathbf{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{2})
of continuous functions equipped with the topology of uniform convergence. Here
H
is the
\alpha
-stable height process as introduced above and
\widehat{W}
is the (endpoint process of the) one-dimensional Brownian snake whose lifetime process is
H
: namely, it is continuous centered real Gaussian process
(\widehat{W}_{s})_{s\in[0,1]}
whose covariances are characterized by

{\bf E}\big[\big|\widehat{W}_{s_{1}}\!-\widehat{W}_{s_{2}}\big|^{2}\big|\,H\big]\!=\!d_{H}(s_{1},s_{2})
for all
s_{1},s_{2}\!\in\![0,1]
(see D. & Le Gall
[
22
, Lemma 6.4 p. 600]
). We refer to Section
2.7
for a precise definition.

To prove (
1.11
) and more generally Theorem
4.1
,
we rely on the so-called harmonic coordinates used by Peres & Zeitouni
[
45
]
. These are a family
(S_{x})_{x\in\mathbf{T}}
of real random variables (r.v.s) depending on the environment
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
which approximates the relative generation of
x\!\in\!\mathbf{T}
on the one hand, and on the other hand such that
(S_{X_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a martingale when
(X_{n})
is a
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RW on
\mathbf{T}
.
This tool allows us to adapt the method of Janson & Marckert
[
30
]
and Marzouk
[
44
]
: we obtain the tightness thanks to a maximal inequality for martingales, together with a control of the variations of the height process of the genealogy, and we check the convergence of the finite-dimensional marginals via a conditional central limit theorem.

Theorem
1.1
echoes Theorem 1.2 in D., K., Lin & Torri
[
20
]
. As in this article, our general idea
consists in deriving the metric convergence in Theorem
1.1
from the snake convergence (
1.11
) (more precisely from Theorem
4.1
)
thanks to general arguments that are in part discussed in

[
20
]
. Namely, we show that
\bm{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
is close in some sense to the discrete snake-tree coded by

\widehat{W}^{(n)}
: see Section
2.8
and in particular Lemma
2.49
for precise statements.

Organization of the paper

In Section
2
we present the main objects and tools that are involved in this article. Among others, this includes the formalism of trees with an infinite line of ancestors, some properties about biased RWs on trees, and definitions and limit theorems for snakes and the real trees they code. In Section
3
, we prove Theorem
3.9
, which a limit theorem for the snake associted with a BRW indexed by a forest of unconditioned GW trees. It is the main technical step of the artcile. Section
4
is devoted to the proof of Theorem
4.1
, which then completes the demonstration of Theorem
1.1

2
Tree-valued BRWs and snakes: definitions, previous results

In this section, we present the main objects and tools which are used in this article. Among others, this includes the formalism of trees with an infinite line of ancestors, some properties about biased RWs on trees, and definitions and limit theorems for snakes and the real trees they encode.

2.1
Rooted ordered trees and forests, coding functions, discrete snakes.

In this section, we recall the framework of (
rooted ordered trees
(within Ulam’s formalism) and their encoding functions:
Lukasiewicz paths
,
height
and
contour
processes. We then briefly recall the definition of Galton–Watson trees and forest (GW trees and forest) within this framework and finally we recall the definition of
discrete snakes
which encode real-valued branching walks.

First, let us briefly recall Ulam’s formalism on rooted ordered trees. We first introduce the set of finite words written with positive integers:

\mathbb{U}\!=\!\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{N}^{*})^{n}\;.

Here,
\mathbb{N}\!=\!\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
,
\mathbb{N}^{*}\!=\!\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}
and

(\mathbb{N}^{*})^{0}
stands for
\{\varnothing\}
. The set
\mathbb{U}
is totally ordered by the
lexicographical order

\leq_{\mathtt{lex}}
(the strict order is denoted by
<_{\mathtt{lex}}
).
It is convenient to think of
\mathbb{U}
as a family tree whose
\varnothing
is the ancestor.
Let
u\!=\!(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})\!\in\!\mathbb{U}\backslash\{\varnothing\}
.
We use the notation
|u|\!=\!n
for the
height
of
u
(with the convention
|\varnothing|\!=\!0
) and we
set
\overleftarrow{u}\!=\!(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n-1})
that is interpreted as the (direct)
parent of
u
. More generally, for all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we set
u_{|p}\!=\!(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n\wedge p})
and
u_{|0}\!=\!\varnothing
. Namely,
u_{|p}
is the
ancestor of
u
at the generation
p
and we observe that
u_{|p}\!=\!u
if
p\!\geq\!|u|
and that

u_{|p}\!=\!\overleftarrow{u}
if
p\!=\!|u|\!-\!1
.
Let
v\!=\!(k_{1},\ldots,k_{m})
, we denote by
u\ast v
the concatenated word
(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n},k_{1},\ldots,k_{m})
.
For all
u,v\!\in\!\mathbb{U}
, we then denote by
u\wedge v
the
most recent common ancestor
of
u
and
v
, which is defined by
u\wedge v\!=\!u_{|p}\!=\!v_{|p}
where
p\!=\!\max\{q\!\in\!\mathbb{N}:u_{|p}\!=\!v_{|p}\}
.

We can think of
\mathbb{U}
as a graph whose (undirected) edges are
\{u,\overleftarrow{u}\}
,
u\!\in\!\mathbb{U}\backslash\{\varnothing\}
. Then, for all
u,v\!\in\!\mathbb{U}
, we denote by

[\![u,v]\!]\!\subset\!\mathbb{U}
the shortest path in the graph
\mathbb{U}
joining
u
to
v
. We
also use the following notation

\,]\!]u,v]\!]\!=\![\![u,v]\!]\backslash\{u\}
,
[\![u,v[\![\,=\![\![u,v]\!]\backslash\{v\}
and
\,]\!]u,v[\![\,=\![\![u,v]\!]\backslash\{u,v\}
.
Note that for integers
p\!\leq\!q
, we also use the notation
[\![p,q]\!]
to denote the set of integers
[p,q]\cap\mathbb{Z}
(along with the other obvious pieces of notation
[\![p,q[\![\,
,
\,]\!]p,q]\!]
, and
\,]\!]p,q[\![\,
). We do not think that there is any conflict in the notation here, as the context always removes any ambiguity.

Definition 2.1
.

(Rooted ordered trees, subtrees, forests)

(\textbf{a})\,
A
rooted ordered tree
is a subset
t\!\subset\!\mathbb{U}
satisfying the following conditions:

(1):

\varnothing\!\in\!t
.
(2):
If
u\!\in\!t\backslash\{\varnothing\}

then
\overleftarrow{u}\!\in\!t
.
(3):
For all
u\!\in\!t
, there exists
k_{u}(t)\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\cup\!\{\infty\}
such that:
u\!\ast\!(j)\!\in\!t
if and only if
1\!\leq\!j\!\leq\!k_{u}(t)
.

Here,
k_{u}(t)
is interpreted as the
number of children of
u
and if

1\!\leq\!j\!\leq\!k_{u}(t)
, then
u\ast(j)
is the
j
-th child of
u
.

We denote by
\mathbb{T}
the set of rooted ordered trees.

(\textbf{b})\,

Let
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
and
u\!\in\!t
. Then

\theta_{u}t\!=\!\{v\!\in\!\mathbb{U}:u\ast v\!\in\!t\}
is the
subtree of
t
stemming from
u
.
Note that
\theta_{u}t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
.

(\textbf{c})\,
A
fores
t is a sequence of trees

(t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
. It is sometimes convenient to view it as a single tree
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
with
k_{\varnothing}(t)\!=\!\infty
such that

t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}}
is the subtree stemming from the
k
-th child of
t
:
t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}}\!=\!\theta_{(k)}t
.
\hfill\square

Definition 2.2
.

(
Lukasiewicz path, height process
)

(\textbf{a})\,
Let
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
be
finite
and let
(u_{l})_{0\leq l<\#t}
be the vertices of
t
listed in
increasing lexicographical order. By convenience we set
u_{\#t}\!=\!\varnothing

and we define
V(t)\!=\!(V_{l}(t))_{0\leq l\leq\#t}
and
H(t)\!=\!(H_{l}(t))_{0\leq l\leq\#t}
by setting
V_{0}(t)\!=\!0
,
H_{\#t}(t)\!=\!0
and for all
0\!\leq\!l\!<\!\#t
,

V_{l+1}(t)\!=\!V_{l}(t)+k_{u_{l}}(t)\!-\!1\quad\textrm{and}\quad H_{l}(t)\!=\!|u_{l}|.

(2.1)

V(t)
is the
Lukasiewicz path
of
t
and
H(t)
is the
height process
of
t
. We extend
H(t)
continuously on
[0,\#t]
as follows: for all integer
0\!\leq\!l\!<\!\#t
and all
s\!\in\![0,1]
, we set

H_{l+s}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}H_{l}(t)\!-\!\big(H_{l}(t)\!-\!|u_{l}\!\wedge\!u_{l+1}|\big)(2s\!\wedge\!1)+(2s\!-\!1)_{+}&\textrm{if $|u_{l+1}|\!\leq\!|u_{l}|$ }\\
H_{l}(t)+s&\textrm{if $|u_{l+1}|\!>\!|u_{l}|$.}\end{array}\right.

(2.2)

(see Remark
2.3

(\textbf{c})
for a motivation).

(\textbf{b})\,
Let
(t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
be a forest of finite trees and
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
its associated tree as in Definition
2.1

(\textbf{c})
. The Lukasiewicz path and the height process of the forest are denoted resp. by
V(t)
and
H(t)
and they are obtained by concatenating the
V(t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}})
and
H(t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}})
in the following way. We set

\sigma_{0}\!=\!0
and for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we set
\sigma_{k}\!=\#t^{{}_{(1)}}_{{}^{\,}}+\ldots+\#t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}}
. Moreover, for all
l\!\in\![\![\sigma_{k-1},\sigma_{k}[\![\,
and for all
s\!\in\![\sigma_{k-1},\sigma_{k}]
, we set

V_{l}(t)\!=\!V_{l-\sigma_{k-1}}(t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}})\!-\!(k\!-\!1)\quad\textrm{and}\quad H_{s}(t)\!=\!H_{s-\sigma_{k-1}}(t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}}).\qquad\square

(2.3)

Remark 2.3
.

(\textbf{a})\,
Let
t
be the tree associated with the forest of finite trees
(t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
as in Definition
2.1

(\textbf{c})
. Let
(u_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\}}
be the sequence of vertices of
t
in increasing lexicographical order:
u_{-1}\!=\!\varnothing\!<_{\mathtt{lex}}\!u_{0}\!<_{\mathtt{lex}}\!\ldots\!<_{\mathtt{lex}}\!u_{l}\!<_{\mathtt{lex}}u_{l+1}\!<_{\mathtt{lex}}\!\ldots
. Let
V(t)
and
H(t)
be as in Definition
2.2

(\textbf{b})
.
Then
V_{l+1}(t)\!-\!V_{l}(t)\!=\!k_{u_{l}}(t)\!-\!1
and
H_{l}(t)\!=\!|u_{l}|\!-\!1
for all
l\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
.
Then observe that
s\!\in\![\sigma_{k},\sigma_{k+1})
if and only if
\inf_{[\![0,\lfloor s\rfloor]\!]}V(t)\!=\!-k
.

(\textbf{b})\,
Observe that if
t
is a finite tree or the tree associated to a forest of finite trees, then we can recover
t
from
H(t)
or from
V(t)
. Moreover,
H(t)
is an adapted functional of
V(t)
, as shown by the following equation that holds for finite trees of forest of finite trees.

\forall l\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*},\quad H_{l}(t)=\#\big\{m\!\in\!\{0,\ldots,l\!-\!1\}:V_{m}(t)\!=\!\inf_{m\leq j\leq l}V_{j}(t)\big\}.

(2.4)

See Le Gall & Le Jan
[
38
]
for more details.

(\textbf{c})\,
Let
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
be a finite tree and let
(u_{l})_{0\leq l<\#t}
be the vertices of
t
listed in
increasing lexicographical order. Let
l,l^{\prime}
be integers such that
0\!\leq\!l\!\leq\!l^{\prime}\!<\!\#t
. Then the height of the most recent common ancestor of
u_{l}
and
u_{l^{\prime}}
may differ from
\inf_{j\in[\![l,l^{\prime}]\!]}H_{j}(t)
by
1
. The
specific continuous extension of
H(t)
implies however that
|u_{l}\wedge u_{l^{\prime}}\big|\!=\!\min_{s\in[l,l^{\prime}]}H_{s}(t)
.

Similarly, if
t
is the tree corresponding to a forest of finite trees and if
(u_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\}}
stands for its sequence of vertices listed in lexicographical order, then we also check that
|u_{l}\wedge u_{l^{\prime}}|-1\!=\!\min_{s\in[l,l^{\prime}]}H_{s}(t)
, for all integers
l^{\prime}\!\geq\!l\geq 0
.
\hfill\square

Definition 2.4
.

(
Contour process
)
(\textbf{a})\,
Let
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
, possibly infinite.
Let
(v_{k})_{0\leq k\leq 2(\#t-1)}
(with an obvious convention if
\#t\!=\!\infty
)
the sequence of vertices of
t
be defined recursively as follows.

We set
v_{0}\!=\!\varnothing
and
R_{k}\!=\!\{w\!\in\!t\backslash\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{k}\}:\overleftarrow{w}\!=\!v_{k}\}
; if
R_{k}
is empty, then we set

v_{k+1}\!=\!\overleftarrow{v}_{\!\!k}
; otherwise we take
v_{k+1}
as the
<_{\mathtt{lex}}
-least vertex of
R_{k}
.

We call
(v_{k})
the exploration of
t
in the
contour order
.

(\textbf{b})\,
Let
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
be finite. For all
k\!\in\![\![0,2(\#t\!-\!1)]\!]
we set
C_{k}(t)\!=\!|v_{k}|
and we conveniently set
C_{2\#t\!-\!1}(t)\!=\!C_{2\#t}(t)\!=\!0
. We next extends
C(t)
continuously on
[0,2\#t]
by setting for all
k\!\in\![\![0,2\#t\!-\!1]\!]
and all
s\!\in\![0,1]
:
C_{k+s}(t)\!=\!C_{k}(t)+s(C_{k+1}(t)\!-\!C_{k}(t))
.
The resulting continuous function
s\!\in\![0,2\#t]\!\mapsto\!C_{s}(t)
is called the
contour process
of
t
.

(\textbf{c})\,
Let
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
be the tree associated with the forest of finite trees

(t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
(Definition
2.1

(\textbf{c})
). The contour process of the forest is denoted by
C(t)
. It is obtained by concatenating the
C(t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}})
in the following way. Recall notation
\sigma_{k}
from Definition
2.2

(\textbf{b})
. Then for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, all
s\!\in\![2\sigma_{k-1},2\sigma_{k}]
, we set
C_{s}(t)\!=\!C_{s-2\sigma_{k-1}}(t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}})
.
\hfill\square

Remark 2.5
.

Let
t
be the tree associated with the forest of finite trees
(t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
as in Definition
2.1

(\textbf{c})
. Observe that the exploration of
t
in the contour order visits all the vertices of
t
. In what follows we denote by
(v_{k})_{l\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\}}
the sequence of vertices of
t
in the contour order starting at
v_{-1}\!=\!\varnothing
(then
v_{0}\!=\!(1)
, etc).

(a)
We first note that
C_{k}(t)\!=\!(|v_{k}|\!-\!1)_{+}
for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
.

(b)
As already mentioned,
t\!=\!\{v_{k};k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\}\}
. More precisely, during the exploration of
t
in the contour order, each edge is crossed exactly twice (upwards first and then downwards). Namely, for all
u\!\in\!t\backslash\{\varnothing\}
, there is a unique pair of integers
k,k^{\prime}
such that

k\!<\!k^{\prime}
and
(\overleftarrow{u},u)\!=\!(v_{k},v_{k+1})\!=\!(v_{k^{\prime}+1},v_{k^{\prime}})
.
It is also easy to check recursively
for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
that
\{v_{j};-1\!\leq\!j\!\leq\!k\}\cap\{v_{j};j\!\geq\!k\}\!=\![\![\varnothing,v_{k}]\!]
.

(c)
The contour process
C(t)
completely encodes
t
and it is closely related to the height process
H(t)
. Indeed, let
(u_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\}}
stands for the sequence of vertices of
t
listed in increasing lexicographical order as in Remark
2.3
. Then, we observe that

\forall l\!\in\!\mathbb{N},\quad K_{l}:=2l\!-\!H_{l}(t)\!=\!\inf\{k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}:v_{k}\!=\!u_{l}\}

(2.5)

which necessarily increases with
l
. Thus for all
l\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
,

C_{K_{l}}(t)\!=\!H_{l}(t)
and furthermore, for all
s\!\in\![K_{l},K_{l+1}]
, we get

C_{s}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(H_{l}(t)\!-\!s+K_{l})_{+}&\textrm{if $s\!\in\![K_{l},K_{l+1}\!-\!1)$ }\\
(H_{l+1}(t)\!-\!K_{l+1}+s)_{+}&\textrm{if $s\!\in\![K_{l+1}\!-\!1,K_{l+1}]$.}\end{array}\right.

(2.6)

( See D. & Le Gall
[
21
, Section 2.4]
for more details).

(d)
We can derive
H(t)
from
C(t)
by the time-change
\phi_{t}\!:\!\mathbb{R}_{+}\!\to\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
that is defined as follows:
we set
\phi_{t}(0)\!=\!0
and for all
l\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
and all
s\!\in\![0,1]
,

\phi_{t}(l+s)\!=\!K_{l}+(K_{l+1}\!-\!1\!-\!K_{l})(2s\!\wedge\!1)+(2s\!-\!1)_{+}.

(2.7)

Then,
\phi_{t}
is an increasing bijection from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
onto itself and for all
s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}

H_{s}(t)=C_{\phi_{t}(s)}(t)\;.

(2.8)

We easily check that for all
l\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
,

\max_{s\in[0,l]}\big|\tfrac{1}{2}\phi_{t}(s)\!-\!s\big|\leq 1+2\sup_{s\in[0,l]}H_{s}(t)\;.

(2.9)

When
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
is finite, then
H(t)
is also obtained from
C(t)
by a continuous increasing time-change

\phi_{t}
from
[0,\#t]
onto
[0,2\#t]
by (
2.7
): then (
2.8
) holds true for all
s\!\in\![0,\#t]
and (
2.9
) holds true for all integers
l\!\leq\!\#t
.

(e)
As in Remark
2.3

(\textbf{c})
, we observe that

(|v_{k}\wedge v_{k^{\prime}}|\!-\!1)_{+}\!=\!\min_{s\in[k,k^{\prime}]}C_{s}(t)
for all integers
k^{\prime}\!\geq\!k
.
If
t
is a finite tree, then
|v_{k}\wedge v_{k^{\prime}}|\!=\!\min_{s\in[k,k^{\prime}]}C_{s}(t)
is obtained instead, for all integers

0\!\leq\!k\!\leq\!k^{\prime}\!\leq\!2(\#t\!-\!1)
.
\hfill\square

GW trees and forests.
We next recall below a convenient recursive definition of Galton–Watson trees and forests (
GW trees
and
GW forests
, for short).
To that end, we equip
\mathbb{T}
with the sigma-field
\mathscr{F}(\mathbb{T})
generated by the sets
\{t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}\!:\!u\!\in\!t\}
,
u\!\in\!\mathbb{U}
.
Unless otherwise specified, all the r.v.s that are mentioned in this paper are defined on the same probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},{\bf P})
.

Definition 2.6
.

(
GW trees and forests
) Let
\mu\!=\!(\mu(k))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

be a probability measure on
\mathbb{N}
.

(\textbf{a})\,
A
GW tree with offspring distribution
\mu
(a
GW(
\mu
)-tree
, for short) is a random tree
\tau
that satisfies the following.

(1):

k_{\varnothing}(\tau)
has law
\mu
.
(2):
For all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
\mu(k)\!>\!0
, the subtrees
\theta_{(1)}\tau,\ldots,\theta_{(k)}\tau
under
{\bf P}(\,\cdot\,|k_{\varnothing}(\tau)\!=\!k)
are independent with the same law as
\tau
under
{\bf P}
.

(\textbf{b})\,
Let
(\tau^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
be i.i.d. GW(
\mu
)-trees. We generically denote by
\tau_{\infty}
the tree associated with the forest
(\tau^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
. We call
\tau_{\infty}
a GW(
\mu
)-forest.

(\textbf{c})\,
We say that
\tau
is a
grafted GW(
\mu
)-tree
if
k_{\varnothing}(\tau)\!=\!1
and if
\theta_{(1)}\tau
is a GW(
\mu
)-tree.
\hfill\square

Remark 2.7
.

(a)
We shall always consider GW(
\mu
)-trees with a non-trivial offspring distribution. Namely,
\mu(0)\!+\!\mu(1)\!<\!1
. Let us recall that a GW(
\mu
)-tree is a.s. finite if and only if

\mu
is critical or subcritical, namely if and only if
\sum_{k\geq 1}k\mu(k)\!\leq\!1
.

(b)
Let us suppose that
\mu
is non-trivial and let
\tau_{\infty}
be a GW(
\mu
)-forest.
Then its Lukasiewicz path
(V_{l}(\tau_{\infty}))_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
RWis a RW starting at
0
whose jump law is
\widetilde{\mu}(k)\!:=\!\mu(k+1)
,
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\}
(according to Spitzer’s terminology, it is a
left-continuous RW
). See Le Gall & Le Jan
[
38
]
for more details.
\hfill\square

Real valued branching walks and discrete snakes.
Here we consider a population represented by a rooted ordered tree
that moves in
\mathbb{R}
(a
\mathbb{R}
-valued branching walk
): each individual has a spatial position. We discuss
two ways to encode the spatial positions of the individuals of the population: namely, via the
contour snake
and
via the
height snake
that are related to the exploration of the family tree respectively in
contour order and in lexicographical order. Here,

\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})
stands for the space of the continuous functions from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
to
\mathbb{R}
equipped with the topology of the uniform convergence on all compact intervals.

Definition 2.8
.

(
Discrete snakes
)
(\textbf{a})\,

Let
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
be finite. Let
(v_{k})_{0\leq k\leq 2(\#t-1)}
be the vertices of
t
in contour order. It is convenient to set
v_{2\#t-1}\!=\!v_{2\#t}\!=\!\varnothing
.
Let
\mathtt{M}\!:\!u\!\in\!t\mapsto\!\mathtt{M}_{u}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}
be a
t
-indexed and
\mathbb{R}
-valued walk.
For all
k\!\in\![\![0,2\#t]\!]
and all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, we set

W_{\!k}(t;p)\!=\!\mathtt{M}_{(v_{k})_{|p}}
, where we recall that
(v_{k})_{|p}
is the ancestor of
v_{k}
at generation
p
(and that
(v_{k})_{|p}\!=\!v_{k}
if
p\!\geq\!|v_{k}|
).
We next set

\forall r\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+},\quad W_{\!k}(t;r)\,=W_{\!k}(t;\lfloor r\rfloor)+(r\!-\!\lfloor r\rfloor)\big(W_{\!k}(t;\lfloor r\rfloor\!+\!1)\!-\!W_{\!k}(t;\lfloor r\rfloor)\big).

We note that
W_{\!k}(t;\cdot\,)\!\in\!\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})
for all
k\!\in\![\![0,2\#t]\!]
. We recall the contour process
C_{\cdot}(t)
from Definition
2.4
.
Then the
contour snake
associated with the
\mathbb{R}
-valued branching walk
(\mathtt{M}_{u})_{u\in t}
is the
\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})
-valued continuous process
(W_{\!s}(t,\cdot))_{s\in[0,2\#t]}
given by

\forall s\!\in\![0,2\#t),\quad W_{\!s}(t\,;\cdot)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}W_{\!\lfloor s\rfloor+1}\big(t\,;\cdot\wedge C_{\!s}(t)\big)&\textrm{if $\;|v_{\lfloor s\rfloor+1}|\!=\!1+|v_{\lfloor s\rfloor}|$,}\\
W_{\!\lfloor s\rfloor}\big(t\,;\cdot\wedge C_{\!s}(t)\big)&\textrm{if $\;|v_{\lfloor s\rfloor}|\!=\!1+|v_{\lfloor s\rfloor+1}|$.}\end{array}\right.

We also set
\widehat{W}_{\!s}(t)\!=\!W_{\!s}(t;C_{\!s}(t))
,
s\!\in\![0,2\#t]
, which is
the
endpoint process of the contour snake
.

(\textbf{b})\,
Let
\phi_{t}\!:\![0,\#t]\!\to\![0,2\#t]
be the continuous increasing time-change such that
H_{s}(t)\!=\!C_{\phi_{t}(s)}(t)
as in Remark
2.5

(\textbf{d})
. Then we define the
height snake
as the
\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})
-valued continuous process
(W^{*}_{\!s}(t,\cdot))_{s\in[0,\#t]}
given by

\forall s\!\in\![0\,,\#t],\quad W^{*}_{s}(t\,;\cdot)=W_{\phi_{t}(s)}(t\,;\cdot)\;.

If
(u_{l})_{0\leq l<\#t}
stands for the vertices of
t
listed in lexicographical order, then
W^{*}_{l}(t;p)\!=\!\mathtt{M}_{(u_{l})_{|p}}
, for all
l\!\in\![\![0,\#t\!-\!1]\!]
and all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
.
We also set
\widehat{W}^{*}_{\!s}(t)\!=\!W^{*}_{\!s}(t;H_{\!s}(t))\!=\!\widehat{W}_{\phi_{t}(s)}(t)
,
s\!\in\![0,\#t]
, which is
the
endpoint process of the height snake
.

(\textbf{c})\,
Let
(t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
be a forest of finite trees
and let
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
be
its associated tree as in Definition
2.1

(\textbf{c})
. For all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, let
R^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!=\!(M^{{}_{(k)}}_{u})_{u\in t^{{(k)}}}
be a
\mathbb{R}
-valued
t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}}
-indexed branching walk.
The contour snake of the branching walks
(M^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is denoted
(W_{s}(t;\cdot))_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}

and is obtained by concatenating and interpolating the
W_{\cdot}(t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}};\cdot)
in the following way. Recall notation
\sigma_{k}
from Definition
2.2

(\textbf{b})
. Then for all
r,s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}

W_{\!s}(t;r)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}W_{s-2\sigma_{k-1}}(t^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}};r)&\textrm{if $\;s\!\in\![2\sigma_{k-1},2(\sigma_{k}\!-\!1)]$ ,}\\
M^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\varnothing}}+\frac{{}_{{}_{1}}}{{}^{{}^{2}}}(s+2\!-\!2\sigma_{k})\big(M^{{}_{(k+1)}}_{{}^{\varnothing}}\!\!-\!M^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\varnothing}}\big)&\textrm{if $s\!\in\![2(\sigma_{k}\!-\!1),2\sigma_{k}]$.}\end{array}\right.

(2.10)

Namely, if
s\!\in\![2(\sigma_{k}\!-\!1),2\sigma_{k}]
, then
W_{s}(t;\cdot)
is a constant function and its value is the affine interpolation between
M^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\varnothing}}
and
M^{{}_{(k+1)}}_{{}^{\varnothing}}
.
We also set
\widehat{W}_{\!s}(t)\!=\!W_{\!s}(t;C_{\!s}(t))
,
s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
, which is
the
endpoint process of the contour snake
.

(\textbf{d})\,
We keep the notation of
(\textbf{c})
and for all
s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we set

W^{*}_{s}(t\,;\cdot)\!=\!W_{\phi_{t}(s)}(t\,;\cdot)
, where

\phi_{t}
is the continuous increasing bijection from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
onto itself such that

H_{s}(t)\!=\!C_{\phi_{t}(s)}(t)
as in Remark
2.5

(\textbf{d})
.
The resulting
\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})
-valued continuous
process
(W^{*}_{s}(t\,;\cdot))_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is the
height snake
associated with the branching walks
(M^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
.
We also set
\widehat{W}^{*}_{\!s}(t)\!=\!W^{*}_{\!s}(t;H_{\!s}(t))\!=\!\widehat{W}_{\phi_{t}(s)}(t)
,
s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
, which is the
endpoint process of the height snake
.
\hfill\square

2.2
Trees with an infinite line of ancestors.

We extend Ulam’s formalism to define ordered trees that may have
an infinite line of ancestors. To that end, it is convenient to introduce
bilateral words
as follows.

Definition 2.9
.

(
Bilateral words, relative height, shifts
)

(\textbf{a})
A
bilateral word
is a sequence

u\!=\!(a_{k})_{k_{1}<k\leq k_{2}}
where
k_{1}\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}\!\cup\!\{\!-\infty\}
,
k_{2}\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
and
a_{k}\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
k_{1}\!<\!k\!\leq\!k_{2}
.
If
k_{1}\!\geq\!k_{2}
, then
u\!=\!\varnothing
. We denote by
\overline{\mathbb{U}}
the set of bilateral words.

(\textbf{b})
Let
u\!=\!(a_{k})_{k_{1}<k\leq k_{2}}\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{U}}
, as above. Then

\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}\!=\!k_{1},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|\!=\!k_{2}.

(2.11)

Here
\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|
is called the
relative height

of
u
and
\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}
its
depth
.

(\textbf{c})
Let
u\!=\!(a_{k})_{k_{1}<k\leq k_{2}}\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{U}}
as above.
Let
l\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
. We denote by
\varphi_{l}(u)
the
l
-shifted word
u
. Namely,

\varphi_{l}(u)=(a_{k+l})_{k_{1}-l<k\leq k_{2}-l},

(2.12)

Therefore,
\bm{|}\!\!|\varphi_{l}(u)\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}\!=\!\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}\!-\!l
,
\bm{|}\!\!|\varphi_{l}(u)\bm{|}\!\!|\!=\!\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|\!-\!l
. We adopt the convention
\varphi_{l}(\varnothing)\!=\!\varnothing
. Observe that the
\varphi_{l}\!:\!\overline{\mathbb{U}}\!\to\!\overline{\mathbb{U}}
are bijections such that
\varphi_{l}\!\circ\!\varphi_{l^{\prime}}\!=\!\varphi_{l+l^{\prime}}
,
l,l^{\prime}\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
. For all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
, we then set

\overline{\mathbb{U}}_{k}=\varphi_{-k}(\mathbb{U})\;.

Note that
\overline{\mathbb{U}}_{k}\backslash\{\varnothing\}
is the set of bilateral words
u
such that

\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}\!=\!k
. We shall also denote by
\overline{\mathbb{U}}_{-\infty}
the set of bilateral words with an infinite depth.
\hfill\square

Note that
\mathbb{U}\!\subset\!\overline{\mathbb{U}}
and if
u\!\in\!\mathbb{U}
, then
\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|\!=\!|u|
(the relative height in this case is the height of the word) and
\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}\!=\!0
. Note for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
that
\overline{\mathbb{U}}_{k}
is countable. However
\overline{\mathbb{U}}_{-\infty}
is not countable.

Let
u\!=\!(a_{k})_{k_{1}<k\leq k_{2}}\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{U}}
be distinct from
\varnothing
. We introduce the following notation.

\mathtt{end}(u)=a_{k_{2}},\quad\overleftarrow{u}\!=\!(a_{k})_{k_{1}<k\leq k_{2}-1}\quad u_{|\!|_{(l_{1},l_{2}]}}\!=\!(a_{k})_{k_{1}\vee l_{1}<k\leq k_{2}\wedge l_{2}},

(2.13)

for all
l_{1}\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}\!\cup\!\{\!-\infty\}
,
l_{2}\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
such that
l_{1}\!\leq\!l_{2}
.
We still call
\overleftarrow{u}
the (direct)
parent
of
u
. Note that if
\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}+1\!=\!\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|
, then
\overleftarrow{u}\!=\!\varnothing
.
Here, we insist on the following convention: viewed as a bilateral word,
\varnothing

has neither a relative height nor a depth. However, we set
\varnothing_{|\!|_{(l_{1},l_{2}]}}=\varnothing
by convention.

For all
u\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{U}}
, we next denote by
L_{u}
the
ancestral line of
u
and by
\preceq
the
genealogical order
, which is a partial order on
\overline{\mathbb{U}}
. Namely

L_{u}=\big\{u_{|\!|_{(-\infty,p]}}\,;\,p\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}\big\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\forall u,v\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{U}},\quad\Big(u\preceq v\Big)\Longleftrightarrow\Big(u\!\in\!L_{v}\Big)\;.

(2.14)

Definition 2.10
.

(
Bilateral trees
)

(\textbf{a})
A non-empty subset
T\!\subset\!\overline{\mathbb{U}}
is a
bilateral tree
if it satisfies the following conditions.

(\textbf{a1})

There is
k\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}\!\cup\!\{\!-\infty\}
such that
T\backslash\{\varnothing\}\!\subset\!\overline{\mathbb{U}}_{k}
.

(\textbf{a2})

For all
u\!\in\!T\backslash\{\varnothing\}
,
\overleftarrow{u}\!\in\!T
.

(\textbf{a3})

For all
u\!\in\!T
,

k_{u}(T)\!\!:=\!\#\{v\!\in\!T\!:\!\overleftarrow{v}\!=\!u\}\!<\!\infty

and
\{1,\ldots,k_{u}(T)\}\!=\!\{\mathtt{end}(v)\,;\,v\!\in\!T\!:\!\overleftarrow{v}\!=\!u\}
if
k_{u}(T)\!\geq\!1
.

(\textbf{a4})

For all
u,v\!\in\!T
, there is
l\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
such that
u_{\mathbf{|\!|}_{(-\infty,l]}}\!\!=\!v_{\mathbf{|\!|}_{(-\infty,l]}}
.

(\textbf{b})
We denote by
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
the set of all bilateral trees. For all
T\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}
distinct from
\{\varnothing\}
, we define the
depth
\bm{|}\!\!|T\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}
of
T
as the unique
k\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}\!\cup\!\{\!-\infty\}
such that
T\backslash\{\varnothing\}\!\subset\!\overline{\mathbb{U}}_{k}
.

(\textbf{c})
A
pointed bilateral tree
is a bilateral tree
T\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}
and a distinguished vertex
o\!\in\!T
that is interpreted as an origin when
T
is viewed as a space (or a distinguished individual when
T
is viewed as a family tree: in this case it is rather denoted by
\varrho
). Recall from (
2.14
) the notation
L_{o}
for the ancestral line of
o
. For all integers
p\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
, we shall use the following notation.

o(p)=o_{|\!|_{(-\infty,-p]}},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\partial L_{o}\!=\!\big\{u\!\in\!T\backslash L_{\mathtt{o}}:\overleftarrow{u}\!\in\!L_{\mathtt{o}}\big\}.

(2.15)

Here
\partial L_{o}
is called the
spine
of
(T,o)
. We denote by
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
the set of pointed bilateral trees.
\hfill\square

Remarks.

(a)
We view
T\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}
as a graph: its set of vertices is
T
and its edges (simple and non oriented) are the unordered pair
\{v,\overleftarrow{v}\}
,
v\!\in\!T\backslash\{\varnothing\}
.

(b)
Let
T\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}
be distinct from
\{\varnothing\}
. If
\bm{|}\!\!|T\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}\!\neq\!-\infty
, then
1+\bm{|}\!\!|T\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}\!=\!\min_{u\in T\backslash\{\varnothing\}}\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|
and Definition
2.10

(b)
implies that
\varnothing\!\in\!T
that is viewed as the root (or the ancestor) of
T
. However, if

\bm{|}\!\!|T\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}\!=\!-\infty
, then
\varnothing\!\notin\!T
and
T
has no proper ancestor but an infinite line of ancestors.
\hfill\square

As for rooted ordered trees, we introduce a formal definition of

subtree, most recent common ancestor and lexicographical order
as follows.

\bullet

Subtrees.
Let us first specify what we mean by concatenation in the context of bilateral words. For all

u\!=\!(a_{k})_{k_{1}<k\leq k_{2}}\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{U}}
and all
v\!=\!(b_{k})_{1\leq k\leq k_{3}}\!\in\!\mathbb{U}
.

u\ast v\!=\!(c_{l})_{k_{1}<k\leq k_{2}+k_{3}}\quad\textrm{where}\quad c_{k}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}a_{k}&\textrm{If $k_{1}\!<\!k\!\leq\!k_{2}$,}\\
b_{k-k_{2}}&\textrm{If $k_{2}\!<\!k\!\leq\!k_{2}+k_{3}$.}\end{array}\right.

(2.16)

The bilateral word
u\ast v
is the concatenation of a bilateral word on the left with
a null depth word
v
on the right. Note that
\bm{|}\!\!|u\ast v\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}\!=\!\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}
, that

\bm{|}\!\!|u\ast v\bm{|}\!\!|\!=\!\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|+\bm{|}\!\!|v\bm{|}\!\!|
and that
u\ast\varnothing\!=\!u
.
Then for all
T\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}
such that
u\in T
, the
subtree
\theta_{u}T
stemming from
u
is defined by

\theta_{u}T\!=\!\{v\!\in\!\mathbb{U}:u\ast v\!\in\!T\},

(2.17)

Note that either
\theta_{u}T\!=\!\{\varnothing\}
or
\bm{|}\!\!|\theta_{u}T\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}\!=\!0
, namely:
\theta_{u}T\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
.

\bullet

Most recent common ancestor.

Let
T\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}
. We define the
most recent common ancestor
(or more simply the common ancestor)
of
u,v\!\in\!T
by

u\!\wedge\!v\!:=\!u_{|\!|_{(-\infty,b(u,v)]}}\!\!=v_{|\!|_{(-\infty,b(u,v)]}}\;\,\textrm{where}\;\,b(u,v)\!:=\!\max\big\{l\!\leq\!\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|,\bm{|}\!\!|v\bm{|}\!\!|:\,u_{|\!|_{(-\infty,l]}}\!\!=\!v_{|\!|_{(-\infty,l]}}\big\},

(2.18)

which is well-defined thanks to Definition
2.10

(\textbf{a4})
.

\bullet

Lexicographical order.
Let
T\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}
. The vertices of
T
are totally ordered by the
lexicographical order

\leq_{T}
that is formally defined as follows: if
\varnothing\!\in\!T
, then
\varnothing
is the
\leq_{T}
-least element of
T
and
for all
u,v\!\in\!T\backslash\{\varnothing\}
,

u\leq_{T}v\quad\textrm{iff}\quad\bm{1}_{\{b(u,v)<\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|\}}\mathtt{end}(u_{|_{]-\infty,b(u,v)+1]}})\leq\bm{1}_{\{b(u,v)<\bm{|}\!\!|v\bm{|}\!\!|\}}\mathtt{end}(v_{|_{]-\infty,b(u,v)+1]}})\;.

(2.19)

Note that there are
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\!\in\!\mathbb{U}
such that
u\!=\!(u\wedge v)\ast u^{\prime}
and
v\!=\!(u\wedge v)\ast v^{\prime}
. Then, it holds that
u\leq_{T}v
if and only if
u^{\prime}\leq_{\mathtt{lex}}v^{\prime}
.
We denote by
<_{T}
the strict order associated with
\leq_{T}
.

Pointed labelled trees.
We view BRWs and more generally branching Markov chains as labelled pointed bilateral trees. Let
(E,d_{E})
be a Polish metric space that is the
space of labels, i.e., , the state space where Markovian spatial motion takes place. A
E
-labelled tree is given by a pointed tree
(T,o)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
and labels
z_{v}\!\in\!E
,
v\!\in\!T
that are interpreted as follows:
T
is a family tree of a population (that possibly goes back to an infinite line of ancestors) and

z_{v}\!\in\!E
is the spatial position of individual
v
.

We introduce the following notation
for the
space of pointed
E
-labelled trees
.

\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}(E)\!=\!\{\partial\}\!\cup\!\Big\{\Theta\!=\!\big(T,o\,;\mathbf{z}\!=\!(z_{v})_{v\in T}\big)\,;\,(T,o)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet},\,z_{v}\!\in\!E,v\!\in\!T\Big\}.

Here
\partial
stands for a cemetery point.

Truncation and local convergence.

We shall consider, the ergodicity of certain transformations on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
and
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
on the one hand, and
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
-valued r.v.s and various statements involving their conditional laws on the other hand. For this reason, we equip
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
with the Polish topology inherited from
E
and from the local convergence on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
. More precisely, we introduce the following truncation procedure.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\Theta\!=\!\big(T,\mathtt{o}\,;\mathbf{z}\!=\!(z_{v})_{v\in T}\big)\!\!\in\!\,\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
and
let
p\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
and
q\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
such that
p\!<\!q
. If
T\!=\!\{\varnothing\}
then we set
[\Theta]_{p}^{q}\!=\!\Theta
. When
T
is distinct from
\{\varnothing\}
, we use the convention that if
\mathtt{o}\!=\!\varnothing
then
\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\bm{|}\!\!|\!=\!\bm{|}\!\!|T\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}
. Then, if
q\!\leq\!\bm{|}\!\!|T\bm{|}\!\!|_{{}_{-}}
or if
p\!>\!\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\bm{|}\!\!|
, then we adopt the convention that
[\Theta]^{q}_{p}\!=\!\partial
. Otherwise, we define
[\Theta]_{p}^{q}
as the
E
-labelled tree
\big(T^{\prime},o^{\prime};\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\!=\!(z^{\prime}_{\!v^{\prime}})_{v^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}}\big)
given by

o^{\prime}\!=\!o_{|\!|_{(p,q]}},\quad T^{\prime}\!=\!\big\{v_{|\!|_{(p,q]}}\,;\;v\!\in\!T:v_{|\!|_{(-\infty,p]}}\!=\!\mathtt{o}_{|\!|_{(-\infty,p]}}\big\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad z^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}\!=\!z_{v},

where
v
is the vertex of
T
such that
\bm{|}\!\!|v\bm{|}\!\!|\!\leq\!q
,
v^{\prime}\!=\!v_{|\!|_{(p,q]}}
and
v_{|\!|_{(-\infty,p]}}\!=\!o_{|\!|_{(-\infty,p]}}
.
We adopt the convention that
[\partial]_{p}^{q}\!=\!\partial
and we call
[\Theta]_{p}^{q}
the
(p,q)
-truncation of
\Theta
.

If there is no label, we use the same notation
[(T,o)]_{p}^{q}
for the
(p,q)
-truncation of the pointed tree
(T,o)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
.
\hfill\square

Let us define local convergence on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
and
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}(E)
.
To that end, we first introduce for all
\Theta\!=\!(T,o;\mathbf{z})
,
\Theta^{\prime}\!=\!(T^{\prime},o^{\prime};\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
in

\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
,

\Delta_{E}(\Theta,\Theta^{\prime})\!=\!{\bf 1}_{\{(T,o)\neq(T^{\prime},o^{\prime})\}}+{\bf 1}_{\{(T,o)=(T^{\prime},o^{\prime})\}}\max_{v\in T}\big(1\!\wedge\!d_{E}(z_{v},z^{\prime}_{v})\big)

with the conventions
\Delta_{E}(\Theta,\partial)\!=\!1
and
\Delta_{E}(\partial,\partial)\!=\!0
.
We easily check that
\Delta_{E}
is a (non-separable) metric on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
. Then,
local convergence on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
is defined as follows.

Definition 2.12
.

For all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, let
\Theta_{n}\!=\!(T^{(n)},o^{(n)};\mathbf{z}^{(n)})\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
.

(\textbf{a})

(\Theta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

locally converges
to
\Theta\!=\!(T,o;\mathbf{z})\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
if for all
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,1)
and for all
q\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, there is
n_{q,\varepsilon}\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
such that for all integers
n\!\geq\!n_{q,\varepsilon}
,
\Delta_{E}\big([\Theta^{(n)}]_{-q}^{q},[\Theta]_{-q}^{q}\big)\!<\!\varepsilon
.

(\textbf{b})

The
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!\!
-valued sequence
\big((T^{(n)},o^{(n)})\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

locally converges
to

(T,o)
if for all
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,1)
and for all
q\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, there is
n_{q,\varepsilon}\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
such that for all integers
n\!\geq\!n_{q,\varepsilon}
,
[(T^{(n)},o^{(n)})]_{-q}^{q}\!=\![(T,o)]_{-q}^{q}
.
\hfill\square

Remark 2.13
.

Local convergence corresponds for instance to the following metric.

\forall\Theta,\Theta^{\prime}\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E),\quad{\bm{\delta}}^{E}_{\mathtt{loc}}\big(\Theta,\Theta^{\prime}\big)=\sum_{q\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-q-1}\Delta_{E}\big([\Theta]_{-q}^{q},[\Theta^{\prime}]_{-q}^{q}\big),

(2.20)

with
{\bm{\delta}}^{E}_{\mathtt{loc}}(\Theta,\partial)\!=\!1
and
{\bm{\delta}}^{E}_{\mathtt{loc}}(\partial,\partial)\!=\!0
. We easily check that
the topology of
(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E),{\bm{\delta}}^{E}_{\mathtt{loc}})
is Polish. Note that
\Theta\mapsto[\Theta]_{p}^{q}
is (by definition) continuous
and that
\lim_{p,q\to\infty}{\bm{\delta}}^{E}_{\mathtt{loc}}(\Theta,[\Theta]_{-p}^{q})\!=\!0
.

Similarly, local convergence on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
corresponds to a Polish topology. generated by the distance

{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathtt{loc}}\big((T,o),(T^{\prime},o^{\prime})\ \big)=\sum_{q\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-q-1}{\bf 1}_{\{[(T,o)]_{-q}^{q}\neq[(T^{\prime},o^{\prime})]_{-q}^{q}\}}
, for all
(T,o),(T^{\prime},o^{\prime})\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
, with the same conventions
{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathtt{loc}}((T,o),\partial)\!=\!1
and
{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathtt{loc}}(\partial,\partial)\!=\!0
.
Let us mention that in most parts of the paper, the space
E
of the labels is the space of pointed bilateral trees

\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
itself.
\hfill\square

Centering function, infinite GW trees.
We next introduce a fundamental function on

\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
that allow us to define dynamics: the centering function. Right after, we introduce infinite GW trees that are invariant under such transforms and whose law has nice related ergodic properties.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
(T,o)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
. When
\varnothing\!\in\!T
but
T\!\neq\!\{\varnothing\}
, we use the convention
\bm{|}\!\!|\varnothing\bm{|}\!\!|\!=\!\bm{|}\!\!|T\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}
. If
T\!=\!\{\varnothing\}
then we set
\mathtt{cent}(T,o)\!=\!(T,o)
, and otherwise, we set

\mathtt{cent}(T,o)\!=\!\big(\varphi_{\bm{|}\!\!|o\bm{|}\!\!|}(T),\varphi_{\bm{|}\!\!|o\bm{|}\!\!|}(o)\big)

(2.21)

The function
\mathtt{cent}\!:\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!\to\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
is called the
centering
function.
\hfill\square

Lemma 2.15
.

The centering application
\mathtt{cent}(\cdot)
is
locally continuous on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
.

Proof.
See D., K., Lin & Torri
[
20
, Lemma. 2.7]
.

\hfill\blacksquare

Definition 2.16
.

(
Infinite GW trees
) Let
\mathbf{r}\!=\!(r(j,k))_{k\geq j\geq 1}
be a probability measure
on
\{(j,k)\!\in\!(\mathbb{N}^{*})^{2}\!:\!j\!\leq\!k\}
.
Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{N}
whose mean
\mathtt{m}_{\mu}\!:=\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\mu(k)
is finite and positive.
Let
(\mathbf{T}\!,\mathtt{o})\!:\!\Omega\!\rightarrow\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
be a
Borel-measurable random pointed tree such that

\textrm{${\bf P}$-a.s.}\quad\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\bm{|}\!\!|\!=\!0\quad\textrm{and}\quad\bm{|}\!\!|\mathbf{T}\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}\!\!=\!-\infty\;.

Recall from (
2.14
) that the notation
L_{\mathtt{o}}\!=\!\{\mathtt{o}(p);p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\}
for the
the ancestral line of
\mathtt{o}
and recall from (
2.15
) the definition of the spine
\partial L_{\mathtt{o}}
of
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
.
Recall from (
2.13
) the notation
\mathtt{end}(\cdot)
.

(\textbf{a})
We say that
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
is an
infinite GW tree with offspring distribution
\mu
and dispatching measure
\mathbf{r}
if:

-

the
r.v.s
S\!:=\!\big(\mathtt{end}(\mathtt{o}(p)),k_{\mathtt{o}(p+1)}(\mathbf{T})\big)_{\!p\in\mathbb{N}}
are i.i.d. with law
\mathbf{r}
,

-

conditionally given
S
,
the subtrees
(\theta_{u}\mathbf{T}\,;\,u\!\in\!\partial L_{\mathtt{o}})
are independent GW(
\mu
)-trees (as in Definition
2.6

(\mathbf{a})
)

More specifically:

(\textbf{b})

(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
is an
infinite GW tree with offspring distribution
\mu
if

r(j,k)\!=\!\frac{\mu(k)}{\mathtt{m}_{\mu}}
, for all
k\!\geq\!j\!\geq\!1
.
\hfill\square

Many-to-one.
Infinite (pointed) GW trees are related to GW trees via the
many-to-one
principle that asserts
the following.

Let
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
be a GW(
\mu
)-tree
and let
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
be an infinite GW(
\mu
)-tree.
Then for all Borel-measurable functions
F\!:\!\mathbb{N}\times\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!\rightarrow\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

{\bf E}\Big[\sum_{v\in\mathbf{T}^{\prime}}F\big(|v|;\mathtt{cent}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime},v)\big)\Big]=\sum_{p\geq 0}\mathtt{m}_{\mu}^{p}\,{\bf E}\big[F(p\,;[(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})]^{\infty}_{-p})\big]\;,

(2.22)

where
\mathtt{m}_{\mu}\!=\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\mu(k)\!<\!\infty
and where
we recall from Definition
2.11
that
[(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})]^{\infty}_{-p}
is the tree
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})

truncated above
\mathtt{o}(p)
, a vertex which is situated at relative height
-p
.

2.3
Biased RWs on trees

In this section, we recall the definition of
biased RWs on trees
as introduced by R. Lyons
[
41
]
. This model depends on one parameter
\lambda
ranging in
(0,\infty)
and we focus on the critical parameter when the environment is a GW tree
\mathbf{T}
. Instead of viewing the RW as successive positions
X_{n}
in
\mathbf{T}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we view it as the
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
-valued sequence
\overline{X}_{n}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},X_{n})
. We then recall the definition of the law of an invariant GW tree, that is a law on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
invariant (up to centering)
for the biased RW
(\overline{X}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. We next recall ergodic properties of biased RW
on GW trees which have been originally investigated in Zeitouni & Peres
[
45
]
.

\bm{\lambda}
-biased RWs on trees.
We fix
\lambda\!\in\!(0,\infty)
and
T\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}
, a bilateral tree not reduced to
\{\varnothing\}
. The transition probabilities of a
\lambda
-biased RW on
T
are defined as follows:
p_{T,\lambda}(x,y)\!=\!0
if
x
and
y
are not adjacent and otherwise for all
x\!\in\!T\backslash\{\varnothing\}
,

p_{T,\lambda}(\overleftarrow{x},x)=\bm{1}_{\{\overleftarrow{x}\neq\varnothing\}}\frac{1}{\lambda+k_{\overleftarrow{x}}(T)}+\bm{1}_{\{\overleftarrow{x}=\varnothing\}}\frac{1}{k_{\varnothing}(T)}\quad\textrm{and}\quad p_{T,\lambda}(x,\overleftarrow{x})=\frac{\lambda}{\lambda+k_{x}(T)}

(2.23)

(we recall that
\varnothing\!\notin\!T
if
\bm{|}\!\!|T\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}\!=\!-\infty
).

A
\lambda
-RW on
T
is the RW associated with the conductances
C_{\!\{x,\overleftarrow{x}\}}\!=\!\lambda^{-\bm{|}\!\!|x\bm{|}\!\!|}
. As proved in R. Lyons
[
41
]
when
T\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
, there is a quantity
\mathtt{br}(T)\!\in\![0,\infty]
called the
branching number
such that the
\lambda
-biased RW is recurrent if
\lambda\!>\!\mathtt{br}(T)
, and transient if
\lambda\!<\!\mathtt{br}(T)
(see also Lyons & Peres
[
42
, Theorem 3.5]
for more details).

In this article,
we want to view
\lambda
-biased RWs on a tree
T\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}
as
a
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
-valued sequences of r.v.
\overline{X}_{n}\!=\!(T,X_{n})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
.

The only reason why we adopt this point of view is technical: we want to apply ergodic theory, to work with regular versions of conditional laws of the RW when
(T,o)
is random, etc.
The transition kernels of the Markov chain
(\overline{X}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
have to be modified accordingly as explained in the following definition.
To fit in our framework of labelled bilateral trees, we generically denote elements of
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
by
\overline{x}\!=\!(T,x)
,
\overline{x}^{\prime}\!=\!(T^{\prime},x^{\prime})
, etc and
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
-valued sequences by
\overline{x}_{n}\!=\!(T_{n},x_{n})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
(T,o)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
. Let
\lambda\!\in\!(0,\infty)
and let
p_{T,\lambda}(\cdot,\cdot)
be the transition probabilities of
\lambda
-RWs on
T
as defined in (
2.23
).
The transition kernel on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!

of the
\lambda
-biased RW is then defined for all
\overline{x}\!:=\!(T,x)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
by

\overline{\mathtt{p}}_{\lambda}(\overline{x},d\overline{x}^{\prime})=\sum_{y\in T}p_{T,\lambda}(x,y)\delta_{(T,y)}(d\overline{x}^{\prime})\,.

(2.24)

We then denote by
P_{T,o}
the law of the
\lambda
-biased RW
on
(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!)^{\mathbb{N}}
(that is
the space of the
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
-valued sequences equipped with the product topology, which makes it Polish) starting at
(T,o)
.
Note that
P_{T,o}
depends on
\lambda
although it does not appear in the notation.
\hfill\square

Remark 2.18
.

It is easy to check that

(T,o)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!\longmapsto P_{T,o}\quad\textrm{is continuous}

(2.25)

with respect to local convergence on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
and to the weak convergence on the space of probability measures on
(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!)^{\mathbb{N}}
. If
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
is a
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
-valued r.v., then
P_{\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o}}
stands for the (regular version of the) conditional law conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
of the
\lambda
-biased RW on
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
denoted by
\overline{X}_{n}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},X_{n})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, starting at
\overline{X}_{0}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
.
These technical points being cleared, when such a formal point of view is not essential to our arguments,
we rather speak of
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
as to the
\lambda
-biased RW on
\mathbf{T}
starting at
\mathtt{o}
.
\hfill\square

Let
\overline{X}_{n}\!=\!(T,X_{n})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, be a
\lambda
-biased RW whose initial value is
\overline{X}_{0}\!=\!(T,x)
. We use the following notation for the hitting times of
\overline{X}
:

\forall y\in T,\quad\mathtt{H}_{y}\!=\!\inf\{n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}:X_{n}\!=\!y\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathtt{H}^{+}_{y}\!=\!\inf\{n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}:X_{n}\!=\!y\},

(2.26)

with the convention that
\inf\emptyset\!=\!\infty
.
The next lemma recalls basic results
on hitting times and the Green function of
\lambda
-biased RWs.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
\lambda\!\in\!(1,\infty)
and let
\overline{x}\!=\!(T,x)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
, whose depth
\bm{|}\!\!|T\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}
is possibly infinite. Let
\overline{X}_{n}\!=\!(T,X_{n})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, be a
\lambda
-biased RW with initial state
(T,x)
. We assume that

\forall x\!\in\!T\backslash\{\varnothing\},\quad P_{(T,x)}\big(\mathtt{H}_{\overleftarrow{x}}\!<\!\infty\big)\!=\!1\;.

(2.27)

Let
\mathtt{o}\!\in\!T\backslash\{\varnothing\}
. Recall from (
2.14
) that
L_{\mathtt{o}}
stands for
the ancestral line of
\mathtt{o}
and let us denote by
\mathtt{o}(p)
the vertex of
L_{\mathtt{o}}
at distance
p
from
\mathtt{o}
, which makes sense if and only if
0\leq p\!\leq\!\bm{|}\!\!|o\bm{|}\!\!|-\bm{|}\!\!|T\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}
. Namely

\mathtt{o}(p)\!=\!\mathtt{o}_{|\!|_{\mathbf{(}-\infty\,,\,\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\bm{|}\!\!|-p\mathbf{]}}},\quad 0\!\leq\!p\!\leq\!\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\bm{|}\!\!|\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|T\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}

(2.28)

We also introduce the successive times of visit of
\overline{X}
in
L_{\mathtt{o}}
that are recursively defined for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
by
\mathbf{r}^{\mathtt{o}}_{k}\!=\!\inf\big\{n\!>\!\mathbf{r}^{\mathtt{o}}_{k-1}:X_{n}\!\in\!L_{\mathtt{o}}\big\}
,
with the convention that
\mathbf{r}^{\mathtt{o}}_{-1}\!=\!-1
and
\inf\emptyset\!=\!\infty
.
Then, the following holds true.

(i)

For all
x\!\in\!T
,
P_{(T,x)}
-a.s. for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{r}^{\mathtt{o}}_{k}\!<\!\infty
and
there exists a birth-and-death Markov chain
(Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
that takes its values in
\mathbb{N}\cap[0,\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\bm{|}\!\!|\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|T\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}]
and such that
\mathtt{o}(Z_{k})\!=\!X_{\mathbf{r}_{k}}
,
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
.
If
T
has a finite depth, then it is reflected at
\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\bm{|}\!\!|\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|T\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}
; otherwise, its transition probabilities are given for all
1\!\leq\!p\!<\!\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\bm{|}\!\!|\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|T\bm{|}\!\!|_{-}
by

\rho_{p,p+1}\!=\!\frac{\lambda}{\lambda+k_{\mathtt{o}(p)}(T)},\;\,\rho_{p,p-1}\!=\!\frac{1}{\lambda+k_{\mathtt{o}(p)}(T)},\;\,\rho_{p,p}\!=\!\frac{k_{\mathtt{o}(p)}(T)\!-\!1}{\lambda+k_{\mathtt{o}(p)}(T)}\;,

(2.29)

and
\rho_{0,0}\!=\!1\!-\!\rho_{0,1}\!=\!\frac{k_{\mathtt{o}}(T)}{\lambda+k_{\mathtt{o}}(T)}
.
Moreover, if
T
has an infinite depth, then
Z
is transient which implies that
\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\wedge\!X_{n}\bm{|}\!\!|\!\rightarrow\!-\infty
and that
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is transient.

(ii)

Let
x,y,z\!\in\!T
such that
z\preceq y
. Then

P_{(T\!\!,\,x)}\big(\mathtt{H}_{y}\!<\!\mathtt{H}_{z}\big)\!=\!\frac{(\lambda^{\bm{|}\!\!|x\wedge y\bm{|}\!\!|}\!-\!\lambda^{\bm{|}\!\!|z\bm{|}\!\!|})_{+}}{\lambda^{\bm{|}\!\!|y\bm{|}\!\!|}\!-\!\lambda^{\bm{|}\!\!|z\bm{|}\!\!|}}\;.

(2.30)

(iii)

We assume now that
T
has an infinite depth. For all
x,y\!\in\!T
, we first get

P_{(T\!\!,\,x)}\big(\mathtt{H}_{y}\!<\!\infty\big)\!=\!\lambda^{-(\bm{|}\!\!|y\bm{|}\!\!|-\bm{|}\!\!|x\wedge y\bm{|}\!\!|)}\quad\textrm{and}\quad P_{(T\!\!,\,x)}\big(\mathtt{H}^{+}_{x}\!<\!\infty\big)\!=\!\tfrac{1+k_{x}(T)}{\lambda+k_{x}(T)}\;.

(2.31)

We denote
the Green function by
G_{T}(x,y)\!=\!E_{(T,x)}\big[\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bf 1}_{\{X_{n}=y\}}\big]
. Then

G_{T}(x,y)=G_{T}(x\wedge y,y)=\tfrac{\lambda+k_{y}(T)}{\lambda-1}\,\lambda^{-(\bm{|}\!\!|y\bm{|}\!\!|-\bm{|}\!\!|x\wedge y\bm{|}\!\!|)}.

(2.32)

If
x\preceq y
, we also get
E_{(T,x)}\big[\sum_{0\leq n<\mathtt{H}_{\overleftarrow{x}}}{\bf 1}_{\{X_{n}=y\}}\big]\!=\!(\lambda\!+\!k_{y}(T))\lambda^{-(\bm{|}\!\!|y\bm{|}\!\!|-\bm{|}\!\!|x\bm{|}\!\!|+1)}
.

Proof.

We easily see that Assumption (
2.27
) implies that
\mathbf{r}^{\mathtt{o}}_{k}\!<\!\infty
a.s. for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
.
Markov property at
\mathbf{r}^{\mathtt{o}}_{k}
implies that
(Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a
\mathbb{N}
-valued birth-and-death Markov chain with transition probabilities as in (
2.29
). We use the previous result with
\mathtt{o}\!=\!y
: Gambler’s ruin for birth and death chains (see e.g. Durrett
[
23
, Theorem. 6.4.6 p. 249]
) implies the first equality in (
2.30
). When
T
has an infinite depth, it also implies that
\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\wedge\!X_{n}\bm{|}\!\!|\!\rightarrow\!-\infty

(the RW is transient) and the first equality in (
2.31
). To prove the second equality in (
2.31
),
we first deduce from (
2.27
) that
P_{(T\!\!,\,x)}(\mathtt{H}^{+}_{x}\!<\!\infty)\!=\!\frac{k_{x}(T)}{\lambda+k_{x}(T)}+\frac{\lambda}{\lambda+k_{x}(T)}P_{(T\!\!,\,\overleftarrow{x})}(\mathtt{H}_{x}\!<\!\infty)
and we use the first equality in (
2.31
).

We now compute the Green function when
T
has an infinite depth: by the strong Markov property at the first time of visit of
x\wedge y
, we get
G_{T}(x,y)\!=\!G_{T}(x\wedge y,y)
. So we only need to consider the cases where
x\!\preceq\!y
. By the Markov property, we observe that

G_{T}(x,y)\!=\!P_{T,x}(\mathtt{H}_{y}\!<\!\infty)G_{T}(y,y)
and that
E_{T,y}[\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\bf 1}_{\{X_{n}=y\}}|(X_{0},\ldots,X_{\mathtt{H}_{\overleftarrow{y}}})]\!=\!1+Z+G_{T}(\overleftarrow{y},y)
where
P_{T,y}(Z\!=\!j)\!=\!p(1\!-\!p)^{j}
,
j\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, with
p\!=\!p_{T,\lambda}(y,\overleftarrow{y})
. Thus,
G_{T}(x,y)\!=\!P_{T,x}(\mathtt{H}_{y}\!<\!\infty)\big(p^{-1}+G_{T}(\overleftarrow{y},y))
. We find the value of
G_{T}(\overleftarrow{y},y)
by taking
x\!=\!\overleftarrow{y}
and (
2.32
) follows.

We then prove the last point of
(iii)
by counting how many times the RW visits
y
before and after

\mathtt{H}_{\overleftarrow{x}}
to get
G_{T}(x,y)\!-\!G_{T}(\overleftarrow{x},y)\!=\!E_{(T,x)}\big[\sum_{0\leq n<\mathtt{H}_{\overleftarrow{x}}}{\bf 1}_{\{X_{n}=y\}}\big]
, by Markov, which
implies the desired result.
\hfill\blacksquare

Critical biased RWs on GW trees.
We restrict our attention to
critical
biased RWs on supercritical GW trees and variants of such trees. More precisely, let
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
be GW(
\nu
)-tree whose offspring distribution
\nu
is supercritical: namely
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}\!:=\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\nu(k)\in(1,\infty)
.
In Lyons
[
41
, Proposition 6.4]
) it is proved that

{\bf P}(\,\cdot\,|\,\#\mathbf{T}^{\prime}\!=\!\infty)
-a.s.
\mathtt{br}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})\!=\!\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
and and that
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
- biased RWs on
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
are null-recurrent. We shall refer to this case as to the
critical case
.
This lemma shows in particular that the estimates of Lemma
2.19
apply to critical biased RW on GW trees.
Recurrence here does not contradict Lemma
2.19

(i)
where the tree is supposed to be infinitely deep.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
\nu
be a supercritical offspring distribution whose mean is denoted by
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
. Let
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
be a GW(
\nu
)-tree (Definition
2.6
) or an infinite GW(
\nu
)-tree (Definition
2.16
).
Let
\overline{X}_{n}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},X_{n})
be a
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RW on
\mathbf{T}
.
Then,
{\bf P}
-a.s. for all
x\!\in\!\mathbf{T}\backslash\{\varnothing\}
,
P_{(\mathbf{T},x)}\big(\mathtt{H}_{\overleftarrow{x}}\!<\!\infty\big)\!=\!1
.

Proof.
By e.g. Lyons & Peres
[
42
, Theorem. 3.5]
), as already mentioned
if
\mathbf{T}
is a GW(
\nu
)-tree, then the
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RW is recurrent which implies
the lemma in this case.

Suppose that
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
is an infinite GW(
\nu
)-tree: conditionally given
\partial L_{\mathtt{o}}
, the subtrees
\theta_{y}\mathbf{T}
,
y\!\in\!\partial L_{\mathtt{o}}
, are i.i.d. GW(
\nu
)-trees and
{\bf P}
-a.s. for all
x\notin L_{\mathtt{o}}
,
P_{\mathbf{T},x}(\mathtt{H}_{\overleftarrow{x}}\!<\!\infty)\!=\!1
by the previous case. If
x\!\in\!L_{\mathtt{o}}
and
\mathtt{H}_{\overleftarrow{x}}\!=\!\infty
, then the previous cases imply that the
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RW has to visit infinitely many often
each vertex of the subtree above
x
because it is the union of a finite number of GW(
\nu
)-trees and of a finite line.
However, each time the RW returns to
x
, it has an independent possibility to jump to
\overleftarrow{x}
with positive probability
p_{T,\mathtt{m}_{\nu}}(x,\overleftarrow{x})
. Thus

{\bf P}
-a.s.
P_{\mathbf{T},x}(\mathtt{H}_{\overleftarrow{x}}\!=\!\infty)\!=\!0
,
which completes the proof of the lemma.
\hfill\blacksquare

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RW on a GW(
\nu
)-tree
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
. Then
the centered trees
\mathtt{cent}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime},X_{n})
weakly locally converge in
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
to a law which is recalled in the following definition, and which is in some sense invariant and reversible for
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RWs as explained right after.

Definition 2.21
.

(Invariant GW trees)
Let
\nu
be a supercritical offspring distribution whose mean is denoted by

\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
.
Let
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
be
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
-valued r.v. It is distributed as an

invariant GW(
\nu
)-tree
if for all bounded Borel measurable function
F
on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
,

{\bf E}\big[F\big(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o}\big)\big]={\bf E}\Big[\tfrac{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}+k_{\mathtt{o}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})}{2\mathtt{m}_{\nu}}F\big(\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathtt{o}^{\prime}\big)\Big],

(2.33)

where
(\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathtt{o}^{\prime})
is distributed as an infinite GW(
\nu
)-tree as in Definition
2.16
.
\hfill\square

Let
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
be an invariant GW(
\nu
)-tree and let
\overline{X}_{n}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},X_{n})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, be a
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RW on
\mathbf{T}
starting at
\overline{X}_{0}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
.
As proved in Zeitouni & Peres
[
45
, Lemma 2]
, the law of invariant GW(
\nu
)-trees is invariant and reversible in the following sense:

(\overline{X}_{0},\overline{X}_{1})\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{=}\big(\mathtt{cent}(\overline{X}_{1}),\varphi_{\bm{|}\!\!|X_{1}\bm{|}\!\!|}(\overline{X}_{0})\big)

(2.34)

where we recall
\mathtt{cent}(\cdot)\!:\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!\to\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
from Definition
2.14
.
This allows us to extend this Markov chain into a
stationnary process

(\overline{X}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
such that for all
n_{0}\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
:

\big(\varphi_{\bm{|}\!\!|X_{n_{0}}\bm{|}\!\!|}(\overline{X}_{n_{0}+n})\big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{=}\big(\varphi_{\bm{|}\!\!|X_{n_{0}}\bm{|}\!\!|}(\overline{X}_{n_{0}-n})\big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{=}(\overline{X}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}.

(2.35)

Moreover, this process is ergodic as stated in the following proposition.

Proposition 2.22
.

Let
\nu
be a supercritical offspring distribution whose mean is denoted by
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
and let
\overline{\mathtt{p}}_{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}}
be as in Definition
2.17
. Let
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
be an invariant GW(
\nu
)-tree as in Definition
2.21
. Let
\overline{X}_{n}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},X_{n})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
,
be a
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RW on
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
starting at
\overline{X}_{0}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
.
Denote by
\Pi_{\nu}
the law of
(\overline{X}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
on

(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!)^{\mathbb{N}}
.
Then,
\vartheta\!:\!(\overline{x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\!\mapsto\!(\varphi_{\bm{|}\!\!|x_{1}\bm{|}\!\!|}(\overline{x}_{n+1}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is
\Pi_{\nu}
-ergodic, where
\overline{x}_{n}\!=\!(T_{n},x_{n})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, stands for the canonical sequence on
(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!)^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.
See Dembo & Sun
[
16
, Theorem 1.4 and Section 2.1]
. In their notation, here,
A
is the single-entry matrix
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
, which is of course irreductible, and
\rho=\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
.
\hfill\blacksquare

We shall apply Proposition
2.22
in the following specific way.

Corollary 2.23
.

We keep the assumptions and the notation of Proposition
2.22
. Let
F\!:\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!\to\mathbb{R}
be
a measurable function
such that
{\bf E}[|F(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})|]\!<\!\infty
and such that
F(T,\mathtt{o})\!=\!F(\mathtt{cent}(T,o))
for all
(T,o)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
. Then

\textrm{${\bf P}$-a.s.\penalty\ }\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{0\leq k<n}F\big(\mathbf{T},X_{k}\big)={\bf E}\big[F(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})\big]\;.

(2.36)

Moreover, if
F
is nonnegative and if there is
\mathtt{b}\!\in\!(1,\infty)
such that
{\bf E}[F(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})^{\mathtt{b}}]\!<\!\infty
, then

\textrm{${\bf P}$-a.s.\penalty\ }\quad\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{0\leq k<n}E_{\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o}}\big[F\big(\mathbf{T},X_{k}\big)\big]<\infty\;.

(2.37)

Proof.
Denote by
\overline{x}_{\cdot}\!=\!(\overline{x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the canonical
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
-sequence. We write
G(\overline{x}_{\cdot})\!=\!F(\mathtt{cent}(\overline{x}_{0}))
. Observe that

F\big(\mathbf{T},X_{k}\big)\!=\!(G\circ\vartheta^{\circ k})((\overline{X}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}})
. It first implies that

(F\big(\mathbf{T},X_{k}\big))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
under
{\bf P}
has the same law as
(G\circ\vartheta^{\circ k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
under

\Pi_{\nu}
. By Birkhoff ergodic theorem (see e.g. Krengel
[
32
, Theorem 2.3 p. 9]
)
\Pi_{\nu}
-a.s.
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{0\leq k<n}G\circ\vartheta^{\circ k}\!=\!\int\!G\,d\Pi_{\nu}\!=\!{\bf E}\big[F(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})\big]
, hence (
2.36
).

We next suppose
F
(and thus
G
) nonnegative and such that

{\bf E}[F(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})^{\mathtt{b}}]\!=\!\int G^{\mathtt{b}}d\Pi_{\nu}\!<\!\infty
, and we proceed to the proof of (
2.37
). To that end,
we use Wiener
L^{\mathtt{b}}
-bound for the maximal function
S^{*}\!\!:=\!\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{0\leq k<n}G\circ\vartheta^{\circ k}
: this theorem asserts that
\int(S^{*})^{\mathtt{b}}d\Pi_{\nu}\leq\big(\tfrac{\mathtt{b}}{\mathtt{b}-1}\big)^{\mathtt{b}}\int G^{\mathtt{b}}d\Pi_{\nu}

(see Krengel
[
32
, Theorem 6.3 p. 52]
). Thus,

{\bf E}[S^{*}(\overline{X}_{\cdot})^{\mathtt{b}}]\!<\!\infty
, which implies in particular that
S^{*}(\overline{X}_{\cdot})
is
{\bf P}
-integrable. Then, for any
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{G}\!\subset\!\mathscr{F}
, we
{\bf P}
-a.s. get

{\bf E}\big[S^{*}(\overline{X}_{\cdot})|\mathcal{G}]\!<\!\infty
since
S^{*}
is nonnegative.
Next, observe that for all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
{\bf P}
-a.s.
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{0\leq k<n}F(\mathbf{T},X_{k})\!\leq\!S^{*}(\overline{X}_{\cdot})
,
Therefore, we
{\bf P}
-a.s. get

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{0\leq k<n}{\bf E}[F(\mathbf{T},X_{k})|\mathcal{G}]\!\leq\!{\bf E}\big[S^{*}(\overline{X}_{\cdot})|\mathcal{G}]\!<\!\infty
, which entails (
2.37
) when
\mathcal{G}
is the
\sigma
-field generated by
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
since, in this case,
{\bf E}[F(\mathbf{T},X_{k})|\mathcal{G}]\!=\!E_{\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o}}\big[F\big(\mathbf{T},X_{k}\big)\big]
by definition of
P_{\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o}}
as explained in Remark
2.18
.
\hfill\blacksquare

2.4
Tree-valued BRWs

In this section, we introduce BRWs with values in trees and more specifically RWs that are
indexed by GW trees
