whose offspring distribution is critical
(or a variant of such trees) and that
take their values in an invariant GW tree
whose offspring distribution
is supercritical.
Formally, these BRWs are viewed as trees that are labeled by pointed trees, i.e., ,
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet})
-valued r.v. After some background on such branching Markov chains, we extend the
centering
function to

\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet})
.

Branching Markov chains.
We first set some notation for branching Markov chains indexed by bilateral trees and that take their values in a complete and separable metric space
(E,d_{E})
.
We denote by
(\mathtt{q}(y,dy^{\prime}))_{y\in E}
a Borel measurable transition kernel on
E
: for all
y\!\in\!E
,
\mathtt{q}(y,dy^{\prime})
is a Borel probability measure on
E
and for all Borel sets
B
, the function
y\!\in\!E\mapsto\mathtt{q}(y,B)
is measurable.
We equip
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
with the Polish topology of local convergence and the associated Borel
\sigma
-field.
For all pointed tree
(t,u)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!\!
, we briefly recall the definition of the law
Q^{{}_{y}}_{{}^{\mathtt{q},(t,u)}}
on

\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
of the
t
-indexed
\mathtt{q}
-branching Markov chain
such that the spatial position of
u
is
y
.

To that end, we introduce the following notation. We equip
t
with its
graph distance

d_{\mathtt{gr}}
; for all
v,w\!\in\!t
, we denote by
[\![v,w]\!]
the geodesic path joining
v
to
w
; if
w\!\neq\!v
, then we denote by

\overleftarrow{w}^{v}
the unique
v^{\prime}\!\in\![\![v,w]\!]
such that
d_{\mathtt{gr}}(v^{\prime},w)\!=\!1
. Namely

[\![v,w]\!]\!=\!\!\big\{v^{\prime}\!\!\in\!t:d_{\mathtt{gr}}(v,v^{\prime})\!+\!d_{\mathtt{gr}}(v^{\prime},w)\!=\!d_{\mathtt{gr}}(v,w)\big\},\quad\overleftarrow{w}^{v}\!\!\in\![\![v,w]\!]\;\,\textrm{and}\;\,d_{\mathtt{gr}}\big(w,\overleftarrow{w}^{v}\big)\!=\!1.

(2.38)

We easily see that
Q^{{}_{y}}_{{}^{\mathtt{q},(t,u)}}
is characterized by the following properties.

(i)

Recall that
\partial
is a cemetery point in
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
. We
first agree on the convention
Q^{{}_{y}}_{{}^{\mathtt{q},\partial}}\!=\!\delta_{\partial}
.

(ii)

If
(t,u)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
is finite, then
Q^{{}_{y}}_{{}^{\mathtt{q},(t,u)}}
is the law of the
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
-valued r.v.
\bm{\Theta}\!:=\!\big(t,u;(Y_{v})_{v\in t}\big)
, where the joint law of the
Y_{v}
,
v\!\in\!t
, on
E^{t}
is

\delta_{y}(dy_{u})\!\!\!\prod_{v\in t\backslash\{u\}}\!\!\!\mathtt{q}(y_{\overleftarrow{v}^{u}},dy_{v}).

(2.39)

(iii)

Let
(t,u)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
be finite. If
\bm{\Theta}
has law

Q^{{}_{y}}_{{}^{\mathtt{q},(t,u)}}
, then for all
p,q\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
such that
p\!\leq\!\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|\!\leq\!q
,

[\bm{\Theta}]_{p}^{q}
has law
Q^{{y}}_{{}^{\mathtt{q},[(t,u)]_{p}^{q}}}
.

Indeed
, when
(t,u)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
is infinite,
Q^{{}_{y}}_{{}^{\mathtt{q},(t,u)}}
is defined thanks to
(iii)
and to
a simple projective argument. Moreover, in general
for all
p,q\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
, such that
p\!<\!\bm{|}\!\!|u\bm{|}\!\!|\!<\!q
,

\Big(\bm{\Theta}\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{=}Q^{{}_{y}}_{{}^{\mathtt{q},(t,u)}}\Big)\Longrightarrow\Big([\bm{\Theta}]_{p}^{q}\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{=}Q^{y}_{{}^{\mathtt{q},[(t,u)]_{p}^{q}}}\Big)\;.

(2.40)

It is easy to check for all Borel subsets
A
of

\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
that

\big(y,(t,u)\big)\!\in\!E\!\times\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!\longmapsto Q^{{}_{y}}_{{}^{\mathtt{q},(t,u)}}(A)\;\,\textrm{is Borel measurable.}

(2.41)

Moreover, let us equip the space of Borel probability measures on
E
and
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
with the (Polish) topology of weak convergence. If we assume that
y\!\in\!E\mapsto\mathtt{q}(y,\cdot)
is weakly continuous, then it is also easy to check that

\big(y,(t,u)\big)\in E\times\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!\longmapsto Q^{{}_{y}}_{{}^{\mathtt{q},(t,u)}}\;\,\textrm{is weakly continuous.}

(2.42)

In (
2.41
) and (
2.42
),
E\!\times\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
is equipped with the product topology.

Tree-valued branching walks.
Here we fix a
genealogical tree

(t,\varrho)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
and a pointed tree

(T,o)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
that is viewed as an
environment
: here, each individual
v\!\in\!t
has a position
y_{v}\!\in\!T
. To fit into our framework of labelled pointed trees, we view the
t
-indexed and
T
-valued branching walk as the
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
-labelled pointed tree

\Theta\!=\!\big(t,\varrho\,;\,\overline{y}_{v}\!:=\!(T,y_{v}),v\!\in\!t\big)\in\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}).

Note that
\Theta
is not a generic element of
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet})
because all the
\overline{y}_{v}
have the same base-tree
T
. We extend the definition (
2.21
) of the centering of pointed trees to
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet})
by setting

\mathtt{cent}(\Theta)\!=\!\Big(\varphi_{\bm{|}\!\!|\varrho\bm{|}\!\!|}(t),\varphi_{\bm{|}\!\!|\varrho\bm{|}\!\!|}(\varrho);\varphi_{\bm{|}\!\!|y_{\varrho}\bm{|}\!\!|}(\overline{y}_{\varphi_{-\bm{|}\!\!|\varrho\bm{|}\!\!|}(v)}),v\!\in\!\varphi_{\bm{|}\!\!|\varrho\bm{|}\!\!|}(t)\Big),

(2.43)

using the same conventions. We observe here that spatial marks (that are pointed trees) are shifted too. By convention we set
\mathtt{cent}(\partial)\!=\!\partial
.

Lemma 2.24
.

The function
\mathtt{cent}(\cdot)
is
locally continuous on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet})
.

Proof.
This is easily derived from Lemma
2.15
: we leave the details to the reader.
\hfill\blacksquare

Definition 2.25
.

(
Biased BRWs
) We next fix
\lambda\!\in\!(0,\infty)
, and

(t,\varrho),(T,o)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
.
We recall from (
2.23
) the definition of the transition probabilities
p_{T,\lambda}(\cdot,\cdot)
of the

\lambda
-biased RW on
T
and we recall from (
2.24
) in Definition
2.17
the related transition
kernel
\overline{\mathtt{p}}_{\lambda}(\overline{y},d\overline{y}^{\prime})
on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
. A
t
-indexed,

T
-valued
\lambda
-biased BRW with initial position
o
is a
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet})
-valued
r.v.
\bm{\Theta}\!=\!\big(t,\varrho\,;\overline{Y}_{\!\!v}\!=\!(T,Y_{\!v}),v\!\in\!t\big)
whose law is

Q_{{}^{\overline{\mathtt{p}}_{{}^{\!\lambda}}\!,(t,\varrho)}}^{{}_{(T,o)}}
, which implies that

Y_{\!\varrho}\!=\!o
.

\bullet
When
t
is finite and
\varrho\!=\!\varnothing
then for all
y_{v}\!\in\!T
,
v\!\in\!t
,

{\bf P}\big(\forall v\!\in\!t,\;Y_{\!v}\!=\!y_{v}\big)\!=\!{\bf 1}_{\{y_{\varrho}=o\}}\prod_{v\in t\backslash\{\varnothing\}}p_{T,\lambda}(y_{\overleftarrow{v}},y_{v})\;.

(2.44)

\bullet
When there is no ambiguity on the parameter
\lambda
, we simply write

Q_{{}^{t,\varrho}}^{{}_{T,o}}
instead of
Q_{{}^{\overline{\mathtt{p}}_{{}^{\!\lambda}}\!,(t,\varrho)}}^{{}_{(T,o)}}
.
\hfill\square

Remark 2.26
.

We easily check that
\overline{y}\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\mapsto\overline{\mathtt{p}}_{\lambda}(\overline{y},d\overline{y}^{\prime})
is weakly continuous. Thus by (
2.42
),

\big((t,\varrho),(T,o)\big)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!\times\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\longmapsto Q_{{}^{\overline{\mathtt{p}}_{{}^{\!\lambda}}\!,(t,\varrho)}}^{{}_{(T,o)}}

is also weakly continuous on the space of Borel probability measures on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet})
.
\hfill\square

2.5
Stable continuous height processes.

In this section, we recall (mostly from
[
21
]
and
[
22
]
) various results concerning stable height processes and stable spectrally positive Lévy processes that are used throughout the paper.

{\bm{\alpha}}
-stable spectrally positive Lévy processes, related height processes.

We fix
\alpha\in(1,2]
. We denote by
X\!=\!(X_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
an
\alpha
-stable and spectrally positive Lévy process
. Its law is characterized by its Laplace exponent

\forall s,\lambda\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+},\quad{\bf E}\big[e^{-\lambda X_{s}}\big]=e^{s\lambda^{\alpha}}\;.

(2.45)

Note that
X_{s}
is integrable
and that
{\bf E}[X_{s}]\!=\!0
, which easily implies that
X
oscillates when
s\to\infty
.
Moreover,
{\bf P}
-a.s. the path
X
has
infinite variation sample paths
(for more details, see Bertoin
[
6
]
Chapters VII and VIII).

In the more general context of spectrally positive Lévy processes, it has been proved in Le Gall & Le Jan
[
38
]
and in D. & Le Gall
[
21
, Chapter 1]
that there exists a continuous process
H\!=\!(H_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
such that for any
s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the following limit holds in
{\bf P}
-probability:

H_{s}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{s}{\bf 1}_{\{X_{r}<\varepsilon+\inf_{[r,s]}X\}}\,dr\;.

(2.46)

The process
H\!=\!(H_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is called the
\alpha
-stable height process
. It is an adapted functional of

X
and we shall sometimes use the notation
H_{s}\!=\!H_{s}(X)
to indicate that. The
\alpha
-stable height process
provides a way to explore the genealogy of an
\alpha
-stable CSBP. We refer to Le Gall & Le Jan
[
38
]
for a careful explanation of (
2.46
) in the discrete setting. Compare with (
2.4
) in Remark
2.3
in the discrete setting.

In Section
3.4
, to prove the quenched convergence of the finite-dimensional marginals
of snakes processes coding the range of BRWs, we shall need the following lemma. Its proof, which is technical, is postponed in Appendix.

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
\alpha\!\in\!(1,2]
and let
H
be the height process associated with the
\alpha
-stable spectrally positive Lévy process
X
as above. Let
s_{1},s_{2}
be two real numbers such that
0\!<\!s_{1}\!<\!s_{2}
. Then
{\bf P}
-almost surely, it holds that
H_{s_{1}}\wedge H_{s_{2}}\!>\!\min_{[s_{1},s_{2}]}H
.

Proof.
See Section
A
.
\hfill\blacksquare

Excursions of the
{\bm{\alpha}}
-stable height process.
For all
s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we set
I_{s}\!=\!\inf_{r\in[0,s]}X_{r}
, the
infimum process of
X
, which has continuous sample paths since
X
is spectrally positive.
Recall that
X
has infinite variation sample paths. Basic results on fluctuation theory (see e.g. Bertoin
[
6
]
Chapter VI.1 and VII.1) entail that
X\!-\!I
is a strong Markov process in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and that
0
is regular for

(0,\infty)
and recurrent with respect to this Markov process. Moreover,
-I
is a local time at
0
for
X\!-\!I
(see Theorem VII.1
[
6
]
). We denote by
\mathbf{N}
the corresponding

excursion measure
of
X\!-\!I
above
0
and denote by
(l_{j},r_{j})
,
j\!\in\!\mathcal{I}
, the excursion intervals of
X\!-\!I
above
0
and by
X^{j}_{\cdot}\!=\!X_{(l_{j}+\cdot)\wedge r_{j}}-I_{l_{j}}
,
j\!\in\!\mathcal{I}
, the corresponding excursions.
Then, the point measure
\sum_{j\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(-I_{l_{j}},X^{j})}
is a
Poisson point measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})
with intensity

dx\otimes\mathbf{N}
. Here
\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})
stands for the space of càdlàg functions from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
to
\mathbb{R}
equipped with Skorokhod’s topology.
Now, observe that (
2.46
) implies that the value of
H_{s}
only depends on the excursion of
X\!-\!I
straddling
s
and that

\big\{s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}:X_{s}\!>\!I_{s}\big\}\!=\!\bigcup_{{}^{j\in\mathcal{I}}}(l_{j},r_{j})=\big\{s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}:H_{s}\!>\!0\big\}\;.

(2.47)

This allows defining
H
under
\mathbf{N}
as
H(X)
when
X
is under the excursion measure
\mathbf{N}
.
More precisely the processes
H^{j}\!:=\!H_{(l_{j}+\cdot)\wedge r_{j}}\!=\!H(X^{j})
,
j\!\in\!\mathcal{I}
, are
the excursions of
H
above
0
, and the point measure

\sum_{j\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(-I_{l_{j}},H^{j})}

(2.48)

is distributed under
{\bf P}
as a Poisson point measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})
with intensity
dx\otimes\mathbf{N}(dH)
, (with a slight abuse of notation for using
\mathbf{N}(dH)
as the ‘distribution’ of
H(X)
under the excursion measure

\mathbf{N}(dX)
). Recall here
\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})
is the space of continuous functions from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
to
\mathbb{R}
equipped with the topology of uniform convergence on every compact interval. We refer to the comments in D. & Le Gall
[
21
, Section 3.2 ]
for more details.

Under
\mathbf{N}
,
X
and
H
have the same lifetime
\zeta
: namely, we have
\mathbf{N}
-a.e.
\zeta\!<\!\infty
,
X_{s}\!=\!X_{0}\!=\!H_{0}\!=\!H_{s}\!=\!0
for all
s\!\in\![\zeta,\infty)
and
H_{s}
and
X_{s}\!>\!0
for all
s\!\in\!(0,\zeta)
.
Basic results of fluctuation theory (see Bertoin
[
6
]
, Chapter VII) also entail that

\mathbf{N}\big[1\!-\!e^{-\lambda\zeta}\big]\!=\!\lambda^{1/\alpha}
,
\lambda\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Therefore
\mathbf{N}(\zeta\!>\!r)\!=\!c_{\alpha}r^{-\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{\alpha}}}
where
c_{\alpha}=1/\Gamma_{\!\mathtt{e}}\big(\frac{{}_{\alpha-1}}{{}^{\alpha}}\big)
and where

\Gamma_{\!\mathtt{e}}
stands for Euler’s Gamma function.

Remark 2.28
.

Denote by
(\mathbf{g}_{1},\mathbf{d}_{1})
the first excursion interval of
H
(or
X\!-\!I
) above
0
that is longer than
1
.
Namely, for all
s\!\in\!(0,\infty)
, introduce
r(s)\!=\!\inf\{r\!\in\!(s,\infty):H_{r}\!=\!0\}
. Then

\mathbf{g}_{1}\!=\!\inf\{s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}:r(s)\!-\!s\!>\!1\}
and
\mathbf{d}_{1}\!=\!r(\mathbf{g}_{1})
. Then, standard results on Poisson point processes imply that

\big(X_{(\mathbf{g}_{1}+\cdot)\wedge\mathbf{d}_{1}},H_{(\mathbf{g}_{1}+\cdot)\wedge\mathbf{d}_{1}}\big)\quad\textrm{under ${\bf P}$}\quad\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{=}(X,H)\quad\textrm{under $\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,|\,\zeta\!>\!1)$.}\qquad\square

(2.49)

Let us next define the law of the
normalized
\alpha
-stable height process

\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,|\,\zeta\!=\!1)
.
To that end, recall that the
\alpha
-stable spectrally positive Lévy process

X
enjoys the following
scaling property
: for all
r\!\in\!(0,\infty)
, under
{\bf P}
the rescaled process

(r^{-1/\alpha}X_{rs})_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
has the same law as
X
. We easily derive from (
2.46
) that
H
also enjoys a scaling property: namely, under
{\bf P}
,

(r^{-(\alpha-1)/\alpha}H_{rs}\big)_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
and
H
have the same law. Therefore,

\big(r^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}X_{rs},r^{-\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}}\!H_{rs}\big)_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\;\textrm{under}\;\,r^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\mathbf{N}\quad\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{=}\quad(X,H)\;\textrm{under}\,\;\mathbf{N}\;.

(2.50)

(with a slight abuse of the word ‘law’). This scaling property allows us to define a regular version of the excursion conditioned to last
r
. Namely, there exists a family of laws on
\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})
denoted by

\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,|\,\zeta\!=\!r)
,
r\!\in\!(0,\infty)
, such that

r\mapsto\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,|\,\zeta\!=\!r)
is weakly continuous on

\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})
,
such that
\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,|\,\zeta\!=\!r)
-a.s.
\zeta\!=\!r
and such that
\mathbf{N}\!=\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,|\,\zeta\!=\!r)\,\mathbf{N}(\zeta\!\in\!dr)
.
We call
\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,|\,\zeta\!=\!1)
the
normalized law of the

\alpha
-stable spectrally positive Lévy process above its infimum
(or of
the
\alpha
-stable height process
). By (
2.50
), we see that

\mathbf{N}\big[F(X,H)\big]=\alpha^{-1}c_{\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\!dr\,r^{-1-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\,\mathbf{N}\Big[F\Big(\big(r^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\!X_{s/r},r^{\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}}\!H_{s/r}\big)_{\!s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\Big)\big|\zeta\!=\!1\Big]\;.

(2.51)

Remark 2.29
.

Let us fix
s\!\in\!(0,1)
. Then the laws of
(H_{\cdot\wedge s},X_{\cdot\wedge s})
under
\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,|\zeta\!>\!1)
and
\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,|\zeta\!=\!1)
are absolutely continuous.
More precisely, there exists a continuous function
(s,x)\!\in\!(0,1)\!\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto D_{s}(x)
such that for all measurable nonnegative functions
F
,

\mathbf{N}\Big[F\big(H_{\cdot\wedge s},X_{\cdot\wedge s}\big)\big|\zeta\!=\!1\Big]=\mathbf{N}\Big[F\big(H_{\cdot\wedge s},X_{\cdot\wedge s}\big)D_{s}(X_{s})\big|\zeta\!>\!1\Big].

(2.52)

See Kortchemski
[
31
]
for more details. A discrete version of this identity is used in the proof of our limit theorem.
\hfill\square

Limit theorems.
We next recall mostly from D. & Le Gall
[
21
, Chapter 2]
, D.
[
18
]
and Kortchemski
[
31
]
the convergence of the rescaled contour and height processes of GW forests whose offspring distribution belongs to the domain of attraction of an
\alpha
-stable law.
More precisely, we fix
\alpha\!\in\!(1,2]
. Then, all the genealogical trees that we consider are (variants of) GW trees whose offspring distribution
\mu
is fixed and satisfies (
1.3
).
We denote by
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
a RW whose jump law is
\widetilde{\mu}(k)\!=\!\mu(k+1)
,
k\!\in\!\{-1\}\!\cup\!\mathbb{N}
.
By definition, Assumption (H3) in (
1.3
) implies that there exists a
(0,\infty)
-valued sequence
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
tending to
\infty
such that

\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{n}\underset{n\to\infty}{\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}}X_{1}\;.

(2.53)

where
(X_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
stands for the
\alpha
-stable spectrally positive Lévy process whose Laplace exponent is

\lambda^{\alpha}
. Here, the sequence
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is
\tfrac{1}{\alpha}
-regularly varying, i.e., there is
a slowly varying function
L\!:\!(0,\infty)\!\rightarrow\!(0,\infty)
at
\infty
such that

b_{n}=n^{1/\alpha}L(n)\;.

(2.54)

By e.g. Bingham, Goldies & Teugels
[
9
, Theorem 8.3.1]
, Assumption (
\textrm{H}_{3}
) in (
1.3
) implies that there exists
a slowly varying function
L^{*}\!:\!(0,\infty)\!\rightarrow\!(0,\infty)
at
\infty
such that

\mu([n,\infty))\!\sim\!n^{-\alpha}L^{*}(n)\;\,\textrm{if}\;\alpha\!\in\!(1,2)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\sum_{0\leq k\leq n}\!\!\!\!k^{2}\mu(k)\!-\!1\,\sim\,2L^{*}(n)\;\textrm{if}\;\alpha\!=\!2.

(2.55)

\sum_{0\leq k\leq n}k^{2}\mu(k)-1
is ultimately positive by (
\textrm{H}_{1}
) in (
1.3
).
By e.g. Bingham, Goldies & Teugels
[
9
, Theorem 8.1.6]
,

\psi_{\mu}(s)\underset{0}{\sim}c_{\alpha}^{\prime}s^{\alpha}L^{*}\!\big(\tfrac{1}{s}\big)\;\,\textrm{where}\;\,\psi_{\mu}(s)\!=\!g_{\mu}(1\!-\!s)\!-\!(1\!-\!s)\;\textrm{and}\;g_{\mu}(s)\!=\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}s^{k}\mu(k),\,s\!\in\![0,1].

(2.56)

where
c_{\alpha}^{\prime}\!=\!\frac{\alpha-1}{\Gamma_{\!\mathtt{e}}(2-\alpha)}
if
\alpha\!<\!2
and
c_{2}^{\prime}\!=\!1
.
Then
L
and
L^{*}
are related as follows.

nL(n)^{\alpha}\sim b_{n}^{\alpha}\sim nL^{*}(b_{n})\sim nL^{*}\big(n^{1/\alpha}L(n)\big)\;.

(2.57)

Theorem
2.30
below gathers several limiting results
for GW trees and their coding processes that are used in our proofs.
To that end, let us first introduce the following notation.
Let
\tau_{\infty}
be a GW(
\mu
)-forest as in Definition
2.6

(\textbf{b})
which is associated with the sequence of i.i.d. GW(
\mu
)-trees
(\tau^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
as in Definition
2.1

(\textbf{c})
. Recall that
V(\tau_{\infty})
,
H(\tau_{\infty})
and
C(\tau_{\infty})
stands for resp. its Lukasiewicz path, its height process and its contour process.
For all integers
n
, we then set

\kappa_{n}=\inf\{k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}:\#\tau^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!\geq\!n\big\},\quad\mathfrak{g}_{n}\!=\!\sum_{1\leq k<\kappa_{n}}\!\!\#\tau^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathfrak{d}_{n}\!=\!\mathfrak{g}_{n}+\#\tau^{{}_{(\kappa_{n})}}_{{}^{\!}}

(2.58)

with the convention that
\mathfrak{g}_{n}\!=\!0
if
\kappa_{n}\!=\!1
.
Namely,
\mathfrak{g}_{n}
is the time when
H(\tau_{\infty})
begins its first excursion above
0
lasting at least
n
units of time.
Note that
\tau^{{}_{(\kappa_{n})}}_{{}^{\!}}

has the same law as a single GW(
\mu
)-tree
\tau
under

{\bf P}(\,\cdot\,|\#\tau\!\geq\!n)
.

Theorem 2.30
.

Let
\alpha\!\in\!(1,2]
and let
\mu
satisfy (
1.3
).
Let
\tau_{\infty}
be a GW(
\mu
)-forest and let
\tau
be a GW(
\mu
)-tree (Definition
2.6
). Then for all (sufficiently large)
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we denote by
\tau_{\geq n}
a tree distributed as
\tau
under
{\bf P}(\,\cdot\,|\,\#\tau\!\geq\!n)
and by
\tau_{n}
a tree distributed as
\tau
under
{\bf P}(\,\cdot\,|\,\#\tau\!=\!n)
.
Recall
\mathfrak{g}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{n}
from (
2.58
) and recall
\mathbf{g}_{1}
and
\mathbf{d}_{1}
from Remark
2.28
.
Let
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
be as in (
2.53
). For all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we set

a_{n}=n/b_{n},\quad n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}\;.

(2.59)

Then, the following holds true.

(i)

The joint convergence

\mathscr{Q}_{n}=\Big(\big(\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{\lfloor n\cdot\rfloor}(\tau_{\infty}),\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}H_{n\cdot}(\tau_{\infty}),\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}C_{2n\cdot}(\tau_{\infty}),\tfrac{1}{n}\mathfrak{g}_{n},\tfrac{1}{n}\mathfrak{d}_{n}\Big)\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\big(X,H,H,\mathbf{g}_{1},\mathbf{d}_{1}\big)

(2.60)

holds weakly on
\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})\!\times\!\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})^{2}\!\times\!(0,\infty)^{2}
equipped with the product topology.

(ii)

For all integers
n\!\geq\!k\geq 0
, there exists a function
D^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{k}}\!:\!\{-1\}\!\cup\!\mathbb{N}\!\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for all measurable nonnegative function
F

{\bf E}\big[F(V_{\cdot\wedge\,k}(\tau_{n}))\big]={\bf E}\big[F(V_{\cdot\wedge\,k}(\tau_{\geq n}))D^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{k}}\big(V_{k}(\tau_{\geq n})\big)\big].

(2.61)

(iii)

We recall from Remark
2.29
the definition of the continuous function
(s,x)\!\in\!(0,1)\!\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto D_{s}(x)
. We fix
(s,x,y)\!\in\!(0,1)\!\times(0,\infty)^{2}
with
x\!\leq\!y
. Let
s_{n}\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, be such that
s_{n}/n\!\to\!s
. Then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\max_{p\in[\![\lfloor b_{n}x\rfloor,\lfloor b_{n}y\rfloor]\!]}\big|D^{{}_{(n)}}_{{s_{n}}}(p)\!-\!D_{s}(p/b_{n})\big|\!=\!0\;.

(2.62)

Moreover, the r.v.
\big(D^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{s_{n}}}(V_{s_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n}))\big)_{\!n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
are uniformly integrable:

\lim_{c\to\infty}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}{\bf E}\Big[D^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{s_{n}}}(V_{s_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n})){\bf 1}_{\big\{D^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{s_{n}}}(V_{s_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n}))\geq c\big\}}\Big]=0\;.

(2.63)

(iv)

The joint convergence

\mathscr{Q}_{n}^{\prime}=\Big(\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{\lfloor n\cdot\rfloor}(\tau_{n}),\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}H_{n\cdot}(\tau_{n}),\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}C_{2n\cdot}(\tau_{n})\Big)\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\big(X^{\prime},H^{\prime},H^{\prime}\big)

(2.64)

holds weakly on
\mathbf{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})\times\mathbf{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R})^{2}
equipped with the product topology. Here

(X^{\prime},H^{\prime})
has the same law as
(X,H(X))
under
\mathbf{N}(dX\,|\zeta\!=\!1)
.

Proof.
In
(i)
, for a proof of the joint convergence of the Lukasiewicz path, the height process and the contour process: see D. & Le Gall
[
21
, Thm 2.3.2 and Corollary 2.5.1]
. The joint convergence with
(\mathfrak{g}_{n}/n,\mathfrak{d}_{n}/n)
follows from arguments in Le Gall
[
36
, Thm 5.1]
.
For
(ii)
see Le Gall & Miermont
[
39
, Lemma 10]
or Kortchemski
[
31
, Lemma 2]
.
For
(iii)
, we refer to the arguments in Kortchemski
[
31
, Section 3.2]
. For
(iv)
: see D.
[
18
]
.

\hfill\blacksquare

We shall use the previous Theorem to derive limits of coding processes of
\tau_{n}
from limits of coding processes of
\tau_{\geq n}
. More precisely, we shall use the following lemma, whose proof is given in Appendix.

Lemma 2.31
.

We keep the assumptions and notation of Theorem
2.30
. Let
\mathscr{G}\!\subset\!\mathscr{F}
be
\sigma
-field. Let
E
and
E^{\prime}
be Polish spaces equipped with their respective Borel
\sigma
-field. The product spaces below
are equipped with product topology. For all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, let
r_{n}\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, let
U_{n}
be a
E
-valued r.v. and let
Z_{n}\!:\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})\!\times\!E\!\to E^{\prime}
be a measurable function. We assume for all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}

that
\tau_{\geq n}
and
\tau_{n}
are independent from
(\mathscr{G},U_{n})
, that

r_{n}/n\!\to\!r\!\in\!(0,1)
and that conditionally given
\mathscr{G}
, the joint convergence

M_{\geq n}:=\Big(\big(\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{(ns)\wedge r_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n})\big)_{\!s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\,,\,Z_{n}\big(V_{\cdot\wedge r_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n}),U_{n}\big)\Big)\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\big(X^{{}_{(r)}}_{{}^{\!}},Z\big)

(2.65)

holds weakly on
\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})\!\times\!E^{\prime}
. Here we furthermore assume that

X^{{}_{(r)}}_{{}^{\!}}
has the same law as
X_{\cdot\wedge r}
under
\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,\big|\,\zeta\!>\!1)
and that

(X^{{}_{(r)}}_{{}^{\!}},Z)
is independent from
\mathscr{G}
. Then, conditionally given
\mathscr{G}
, the joint convergence

M_{n}:=\Big(\big(\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{(ns)\wedge r_{n}}(\tau_{n})\big)_{\!s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\,,\,Z_{n}\big(V_{\cdot\wedge r_{n}}(\tau_{n}),U_{n}\big)\Big)\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\big(Y^{{}_{(r)}}_{{}^{\!}},Z^{\prime})

(2.66)

holds weakly on
\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})\!\times\!E^{\prime}
where the r.v.
(Y^{{}_{(r)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!\!,Z^{\prime})
is independent from
\mathscr{G}

and such that

{\bf E}[F(Y^{{}_{(r)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!\!,Z^{\prime})]\!=\!{\bf E}[F(X^{{}_{(r)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!\!,Z)D_{r}(X^{{}_{(r)}}_{{}^{r}})]
for all bounded measurable functions
F
on

\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})\!\times\!E^{\prime}
.

Proof.
See Section
B
.
\hfill\blacksquare

We complete this section by recalling several standard estimates on the distribution of
\#\tau
when
\tau
is a GW(
\mu
)-tree. Let
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be the RW as in (
2.53
). By standard results on GW trees and RWs,

\#\tau\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{=}T_{-1}\quad\textrm{where}\quad T_{-p}\!:=\!\inf\big\{n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}:V_{n}\!=\!-p\big\},\quad p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}.

(2.67)

Note that
T_{-p}
is a.s. finite by (
\textrm{H}_{1}
) in (
1.3
). By the strong Markov property,
(T_{-p})_{p\in\mathbb{N}}
is a RW.

Lemma 2.32
.

We assume that
\mu
satisfies (
1.3
). Recall from (
2.54
) the definition of the slowly varying function
L\!:\!(0,\infty)\!\to\!(0,\infty)
.
For all
s\!\in\![0,1]
, we set
\varphi_{\mu}(s)\!=\!{\bf E}[s^{\#\tau}]
. Then, there is a constant

c_{\alpha}\!\in\!(0,\infty)
that only depends on
\alpha
, such that

n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L(n){\bf P}\big(\#\tau\!\geq\!n\big)\to c_{\alpha}\quad\textrm{and}\quad 1\!-\!\varphi_{\mu}(e^{-\lambda})\sim_{0^{+}}c_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\!\!\texttt{e}}\big(1\!-\!\tfrac{1}{\alpha}\big)\lambda^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda}\big)^{-1}

(2.68)

(here
\Gamma_{\!\!\texttt{e}}
is Euler’s gamma function). Furthermore,
n^{1+\frac{1}{\alpha}}L(n){\bf P}\big(\#\tau\!=\!n\big)\to\alpha^{-1}c_{\alpha}
.

Proof.
The first limit in (
2.68
) implies the second one by Karamata’s Abelian Theorem for Laplace transforms
see e.g. Bingham, Goldies & Teugels
[
9
, Theorem 1.7.6]
. By Kemperman’s identity (see e.g.
[
9
, Theorem 8.9.15]
), we first get

{\bf P}(T_{-1}\!=\!n)\!=\!\frac{1}{n}{\bf P}(V_{n}\!=\!-1)
. By Gnedenko’s local limit theorem
(see e.g.
[
9
, Theorem 8.4.1]
), we next get
{\bf P}(V_{n}\!=\!-1)\sim\textrm{Cst}/b_{n}
. Thus

{\bf P}(\#\tau\!=\!n)\sim\textrm{Cst}/(nb_{n})
. By Karamata’s Theorem for tails (see e.g.
[
9
, Proposition 1.5.10]
), we get
{\bf P}(\#\tau\!\geq\!n)\sim\alpha\textrm{Cst}/b_{n}
, which entails the lemma.
\hfill\blacksquare

2.6
Real trees, convergences of metric spaces and pseudometrics.

In this section, we first recall the definition of a specific class of metric spaces called real trees. They appear as limits of rescaled discrete trees. Here the limit holds in the sense of
Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov (GHP for short). GHP-convergence actually concerns isometry classes of pointed measured compact metric spaces
and, although it provides a neat and intrinsic framework for formulating
convergences of renormalized random graphs, it is more convenient to proceed with a certain type of encoding of these spaces which provides pseudometrics viewed as
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
-indexed processes.
We briefly recall from D., K., Lin & Torri
[
20
]

the results on the space of pseudometrics that are used in our proofs.

Real trees.
Informally, real trees are obtained by glueing compact intervals without creating cycles. More precisely, they are defined as follows.

Definition 2.33
.

(
Real trees
)
Let
(T,d)
be a metric space; it is a
real tree
if and only if the following holds true.

(a)
For any
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\!\in\!T
, there is a unique isometry

f:[0,d(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})]\!\rightarrow\!T
such
that
f(0)\!=\!\sigma_{1}
and
f(d(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}))\!=\!\sigma_{2}
. Then, we set

[\![\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}]\!]\!:=\!f([0,d(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})])
.

(b)
For any continuous injective function

g:[0,1]\!\rightarrow\!T
, such that
g(0)=\sigma_{1}
and
g(1)=\sigma_{2}
,
g([0,1])\!=\![\![\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}]\!]
.
\hfill\square

Among connected metric spaces, real trees are characterized by the so-called
four points inequality
. Namely, if
(T,d)
is a connected metric space, then
(T,d)
is a real tree if and only if for any
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4}\in T
,

d(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})+d(\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4})\leq\big(d(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{3})+d(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{4})\big)\vee\big(d(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{4})+d(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})\big).

(2.69)

We refer to Evans
[
25
]
or to Dress, Moulton & Terhalle
[
17
]
for a detailed account on this property.
In this paper, we shall only consider
compact pointed measured real trees
:
namely, compact real trees
(T,d)
equipped with a distinguished point
r\!\in\!T
that is viewed as a
root
, and with a finite measure
\mu
defined on the Borel
\sigma
-field of
T
.

Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov metric.
In this paper, we prove that rescaled specific random graph trees equipped with a root and (variants of) the counting measure converge to random compact pointed measured real trees. The convergence of these metric spaces holds weakly in the Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov sense as recalled here.

Let
(E_{1},d_{1},r_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(E_{2},d_{2},r_{2},\mu_{2})
be two pointed measured compact metric spaces: here
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are finite measures on the respective Borel
\sigma
-fields of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, and
r_{1}\!\in\!E_{1}
and
r_{2}\!\in\!E_{2}
are distinguished points. The pointed
Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov distance
(the
GHP-distance
for short) between
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
is defined by

{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}(E_{1},E_{2})\!=\!\inf\!\Big\{d_{E}^{\textrm{Haus}}\!\big(\phi_{1}(E_{1}),\phi_{2}(E_{2})\big)\\
+d_{E}(\phi_{1}(r_{1}),\phi_{2}(r_{2}))+d_{E}^{\textrm{Prok}}\big(\mu_{1}\!\circ\!\phi^{-1}_{1}\!\!,\mu_{2}\!\circ\!\phi^{-1}_{2}\big)\Big\}.

(2.70)

Here, the infimum is taken over all Polish spaces
(E,d_{E})
and all isometric embeddings
\phi_{i}:E_{i}\hookrightarrow E
,
i\!\in\!\{1,2\}
;

d_{E}^{\textrm{Haus}}
stands for the Hausdorff distance on the space of compact subsets of
E
(namely,

d_{E}^{\textrm{Haus}}(K_{1},K_{2})\!=\!\inf\{\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty):K_{1}\!\subset\!K_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}\;\textrm{and}\;K_{2}\!\subset\!K_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}\}
, where
A^{(\varepsilon)}\!=\!\{y\!\in\!E:d_{E}(y,A)\!\leq\!\varepsilon\}
for all non-empty

A\!\subset\!E
);

d_{E}^{\textrm{Prok}}
stands for the Prokhorov distance on the space of finite Borel measures on
E
(namely,

d_{E}^{\textrm{Prok}}(\mu,\nu)\!=\!\inf\{\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty)\!:\!\mu(K)\!\leq\!\nu(K^{(\varepsilon)})+\varepsilon\;\textrm{and}\;\nu(K)\!\leq\!\mu(K^{(\varepsilon)})+\varepsilon,\;\forall K\!\subset\!E\;\textrm{compact}\}
);
for all

i\!\in\!\{1,2\}
,
\mu_{i}\!\circ\!\phi^{-1}_{i}
stands for the pushforward measure of
\mu_{i}
via
\phi_{i}
.

We next recall from Theorem 2.5 in Abraham, Delmas & Hoscheit
[
1
]

the following assertions:
{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}
is a pseudometric (i.e., , it is symmetric and it satisfies the triangle inequality) and
{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}(E_{1},E_{2})\!=\!0
if and only if
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are
isometric
, namely if and only if there exists a bijective isometry
\phi\!:\!E_{1}\!\rightarrow\!E_{2}
such that
\phi(r_{1})\!=\!r_{2}
and such that
\mu_{2}\!=\!\mu_{1}\circ\phi^{-1}
.
Then denote by
\mathbb{M}

the space of isometry classes of pointed measured compact metric spaces.

Theorem 2.5 in Abraham, Delmas & Hoscheit
[
1
]
asserts that

(\mathbb{M},{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}})
is a complete and separable metric space.

(2.71)

It is easy to prove that the four points inequality (
2.69
) is preserved under GHP-convergence. Therefore,
the measure preserving pointed isometry classes of compact real trees form a
{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}
-closed subspace of
\mathbb{M}
.

Limits of continuous pseudometrics.
We introduce here the space of pseudometrics as continuous functions on real intervals, which is an alternative point of view to the intrinsic approach of GHP convergence of metric spaces: this allows a more concrete manipulation of metric spaces and it makes possible to use tools of weak convergence of
processes.

Definition 2.34
.

Let
\zeta\!\in\!(0,\infty)
. We denote by
\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R})
the space of continuous
functions from
[0,\zeta]^{2}
to
\mathbb{R}
that is a Banach space when equipped with
uniform norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert
.

(a)
We denote by
\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
the subspace of
\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R})
of
continuous pseudometrics
on
[0,\zeta]
. Namely, the functions

d(\cdot,\cdot)\!\in\!\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R})
such that for all
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\!\in\![0,\zeta]
,

d(s_{1},s_{2})\!\geq\!0,\;\,d(s_{1},s_{1})\!=\!0,\;\,d(s_{1},s_{2})\!=\!d(s_{2},s_{1})\;\,\mathrm{and}\;\,d(s_{1},s_{3})\!\leq\!d(s_{1},s_{2})+d(s_{2},s_{3}).

(b)
We also denote by
\mathbf{MT}([0,\zeta])
the space of continuous real tree pseudometrics.
Namely, the functions
d(\cdot,\cdot)\!\in\!\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
such that for all
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4}\!\in\![0,\zeta]
,

d(s_{1},s_{2})+d(s_{3},s_{4})\leq\max\big(d(s_{1},s_{3})+d(s_{2},s_{4})\,;\,d(s_{1},s_{4})+d(s_{2},s_{3})\big)\;.

(2.72)

We easily check that
\mathbf{MT}([0,\zeta])
and
\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
are closed subsets of

(\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R}),\lVert\cdot\rVert)
.
\hfill\square

Let
d\!\in\!\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
. It induces a connected compact pointed measured metric space as follows:
for all
s_{1},s_{2}\!\in\![0,\zeta]
, set
s_{1}\sim_{d}s_{2}
if and only if
d(s_{1},s_{2})\!=\!0
. Clearly,
\sim_{d}
is an equivalence relation. We then define the quotient space:

E_{d}=[0,\zeta]/\!\!\sim_{d},\quad\mathtt{proj}_{d}:[0,\zeta]\!\rightarrow\!E_{d},\;\textrm{the canonical projection},\;r_{d}=\mathtt{proj}_{d}(0){.}

(2.73)

We keep denoting
d
the metric induced by
d
on
E_{d}
. Since
d
is continuous on
[0,\zeta]^{2}
,
\mathtt{proj}_{d}
is continuous and
(E_{d},d,r_{d})
is a pointed compact and connected metric space.
We also equip
E_{d}
with the pushforward measure
\mu_{d}
of the Lebesgue measure on
[0,\zeta]
via the canonical projection: namely, for all nonnegative measurable functions
f
on
E_{d}
,

\int_{E_{d}}\!\!\!f(x)\,\mu_{d}(dx)\!=\!\int_{0}^{\zeta}\!\!f(\mathtt{proj}_{d}(s))\,ds\;.

(2.74)

Note that
\mu_{d}
is a finite measure with total mass
\zeta
. We next recall the following result from D., K., Lin & Torri
[
20
]
.

Proposition 2.35
.

Let
d,d^{\prime}\!\in\!\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
and let
E_{d}
and
E_{d^{\prime}}
be the related
pointed measured compact metric spaces as defined by (
2.73
) and (
2.74
). Then

{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}(E_{d},E_{d^{\prime}})\leq\tfrac{3}{2}\lVert d\!-\!d^{\prime}\rVert\;.

(2.75)

Proof.
See D., K., Lin & Torri
[
20
, Proposition 4.14]
.
\hfill\blacksquare

Example 2.36
.

(
Real trees coded by continuous functions
) Let
\zeta\!\in\!(0,\infty)
and
let
h\!:\![0,\zeta]\!\rightarrow\!\mathbb{R}
be continuous. For all
s_{1},s_{2}\!\in\![0,\zeta]
, we set

m_{h}(s_{1},s_{2})=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\inf_{\,\hskip 16.38895pts\in[s_{1}\wedge s_{2},s_{1}\vee s_{2}]}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!h(s)\qquad\textrm{and}\quad d_{h}(s_{1},s_{2})=h(s_{1})+h(s_{2})-2m_{h}(s_{1},s_{2})\;.

(2.76)

We easily check that
d_{h}\!\in\!\mathbf{MT}([0,\zeta])
and to simplify we denote by
(T_{h},d_{h},r_{h},\mu_{h})

the induced metric space
(E_{d_{h}},d_{h},r_{d_{h}},\mu_{d_{h}})
as defined in (
2.73
)
and (
2.74
);
we also denote by
p_{h}\!:\![0,\zeta]\!\rightarrow\!T_{h}
the canonical projection (instead of
\mathtt{proj}_{d_{h}}
);
(T_{h},d_{h},r_{h},\mu_{h})
is a pointed measured compact real tree that is the
tree coded by the function
h
.
Let
h^{\prime}\!:\![0,\zeta]\!\rightarrow\!\mathbb{R}
be continuous. Then observe that

\forall s_{1},s_{2},\!\in\![0,\zeta],\quad\big|d_{h}(s_{1},s_{2})\!-\!d_{h^{\prime}}(s_{1},s_{2})\big|\leq 4\sup_{s\in[0,\zeta]}|h(s)\!-\!h^{\prime}(s)|\;,

(2.77)

which shows the continuity in
\mathbf{MT}([0,\zeta])
of the application
h\mapsto d_{h}
and therefore the continuity of
h\mapsto T_{h}
with respect to GHP-convergence by Proposition
2.35
.
\hfill\square

2.7
Continuous snakes and related metrics.

Here we recall the definition of Brownian snakes that have been introduced by J-F. Le Gall in
[
35
]
. They are continuous analogs of discrete snakes introduced in Definition
2.8
. One-dimensional Brownian snakes play a key role in our result: they encode a random metric space called the
Brownian cactus
(see Curien, Le Gall & Miermont
[
15
]
) which is the limit of the rescaled range of the tree-valued BRWs studied in this article.
In this section, we recall the definition of the Brownian cactus with
\alpha
-stable branching mechanism, along with technical results on limits of snake-pseudometrics, some come from D., K., Lin & Torri
[
20
]
,
others are new.

General definition of snakes.
Let us first introduce some notation and conventions on spaces of continuous functions.
If
(E,d_{E})
is a complete and separable metric space (a Polish space), then
\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},E)
stands for the space of continuous functions from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
to
E
that is also Polish when equipped with the topology of uniform convergence on all compact intervals, which is metrized e.g. by
\delta(f,g)

\!=\!

\sum_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

2^{-N}\min(1,\sup_{s\in[0,N]}

d_{E}(f(s),g(s)))
,
f,g\!\in\!\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},E)
.

The Euclidian space
\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
q\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}\!
, is equipped with its
canonical basis denoted by
(\mathtt{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathtt{e_{q}})
, with its canonical scalar product denoted by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and with its associated Euclidian norm denoted by
\lvert\,\cdot\,\rvert
.
When it is convenient,
we shorten the
\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}^{q})
notation to
\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}}
and to simplify notation, we set
\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{\!+}}\!=\!\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
.

Definition 2.37
.

(
Continuous snakes)

Let
(E,d_{E})
be complete and separable.
We equip the set

\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{\!+}}\times\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})
with the product topology, which is Polish.
Then
(h(s),w_{s}(\cdot))_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\!\!\in\!\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{\!+}}\times\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})
is a
snake
if the following conditions are satisfied.

(a)

For all
s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
and for all
r\!\in\![h(s),\infty)
,
w_{s}(r)\!=\!w_{s}(h(s))\!=:\!\widehat{w}_{s}
.

(b)

For all
s_{1},s_{2}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

w_{s_{1}}(r)\!=\!w_{s_{2}}(r)
for all
r\!\in\![0,m_{h}(s_{1},s_{2})]
where we have set

m_{h}(s_{1},s_{2})\!=\!\inf_{s\in[s_{1}\wedge s_{2},s_{1}\vee s_{2}]}h(s)
.

\bullet
We refer to
(b)
as to the
snake property
. The function
h
is called the
lifetime process
of the snake and the function
(\widehat{w}_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
the
endpoint process
of the snake.

\bullet
We denote by
\bm{\Sigma}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
the space of snakes of
\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{\!+}}\!\times\!\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})
. For all

\zeta\!\in\!(0,\infty)
, we denote by
\bm{\Sigma}_{q}([0,\zeta])
the snake with duration
\zeta
: namely snakes
(h,w)\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that
h(s)\!=\!0
for all
s\!\in\![\zeta,\infty)
.

\bullet
To simplify notation, we also set
\bm{\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\!:=\!\bm{\Sigma}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and

\bm{\Sigma}([0,\zeta])\!:=\!\bm{\Sigma}_{1}([0,\zeta])
.
\hfill\square

Remark 2.38
.

(a)
We easily check that
\bm{\Sigma}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is a closed subset of
\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{\!+}}\!\times\!\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})
.

(b)
If
(h,w)\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, then note that
\widehat{w}\!\in\!\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}}
and observe that
(h,w)\mapsto\widehat{w}
is continuous from the Polish spaces
\bm{\Sigma}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
to
\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}}
.

(c)
Let
(h,w)\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and let
s,r\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
. If
r\!\leq\!m_{h}(0,s)
, the snake property implies that
w_{s}(r)\!=\!w_{0}(r)
and if
r\!\geq\!m_{h}(0,s)
,
w_{s}(r)\!=\!\widehat{w}_{\alpha_{r,s}}

where
\alpha_{r,s}\!=\!\sup\{s^{\prime}\!\in\![0,s]:h(s^{\prime})\!\leq\!r\wedge h(s)\}
by the snake property again.
Thus, there is a function
G
from
\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{\!+}}\!\times\!(\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})^{2}
to
\bm{\Sigma}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that
G(h,w_{0},\widehat{w})\!=\!(h,w)
for all
(h,w)\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. Note that actually
G
can be chosen to be Borel-measurable (this fact is not needed in what follows).

(d)
Let
(M_{u})_{u\in t}
be a
\mathbb{R}
-valued branching walk and let
W
stand for its associated discrete contour snake as in Definition
2.8
. Then observe that
(C(t),W_{\cdot}(t;\cdot))\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. Therefore, the contour process
C(t)
of
t
is the lifetime process of
W_{\cdot}(t;\cdot)
.

(e)
Let
(h,w)\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
N\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
. We define
R_{N}(h,w)\!=\!(h^{\prime},w^{\prime})
as follows:
h^{\prime}(s)\!=\!h(s)
and
w_{s}^{\prime}\!=\!w_{s}
for all
s\!\in\![0,N]
,
h^{\prime}(s)\!=\!h(2N\!-\!s)
and
w_{s}^{\prime}\!=\!w_{2N\!-\!s}
for all
s\!\in\![N,2N]
, and
h^{\prime}(s)\!=\!(2N\!+\!1\!-\!s)_{+}h(0)
and
w_{s}^{\prime}\!=\!w_{0}(\cdot\wedge h^{\prime}(s))
for all
s\!\in\![2N,\infty)
. Thus,
R_{N}(h,w)\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}_{q}([0,2N\!+\!1])
,
\widehat{w}^{\prime}_{s}\!=\!\widehat{w}_{s}
if
s\!\in\![0,N]
,
\widehat{w}^{\prime}_{s}\!=\!\widehat{w}_{2N\!-\!s}
if
s\!\in\![N,2N]
and
\widehat{w}^{\prime}_{s}\!=\!w_{0}((2N\!+\!1\!-\!s)_{+}\,h(0))
if
s\!>\!2N
.

(f)
Let
(h,w)\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. We set
\underline{h}(s)\!=\!h(s)\!-\!m_{h}(0,s)
and
\underline{w}_{s}(r)\!=\!w_{s}(r\!+\!m_{h}(0,s))\!-\!w_{0}(m_{h}(0,s))
,
s,r\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and we observe that
(\underline{h},\underline{w})\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and that
\widehat{\underline{w}}_{s}\!=\!\widehat{w}_{s}\!-\!w_{0}(m_{h}(0,s))
,
s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

\hfill\square

Finite-dimensional marginal laws of random snakes.
Here we consider Brownian snakes. To define their finite-dimensional marginals, it is convenient to introduce the following.
For all
w_{0},w_{1}\!\in\!\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}}
and all
m\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we define
w_{2}\!=\!w_{0}\!\oplus_{m}\!w_{1}\!\in\!\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}}
by setting
w_{2}(s)\!=\!w_{0}(s)
if
s\!\in\![0,m]
and
w_{2}(s)\!=\!w_{0}(m)+w_{1}(s\!-\!m)\!-\!w_{1}(0)
if
s\!\in\![m,\infty)
. We easily check that

(w_{0},w_{1},m,r)\!\in\!(\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})^{2}\!\times\!\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\longmapsto(w_{0}\!\oplus_{m}\!w_{1})(\cdot\wedge r)\!\in\!\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}}\quad\textrm{is continuous.}

(2.78)

Let
h\!\in\!\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{\!+}}
, let
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, let
s_{0}\!=\!0\!\leq\!s_{1}\!<\!\ldots\!<\!s_{p}
be real numbers
and let
w_{0},\ldots,w_{p}\!\in\!\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}}
. We define
F_{p}(h;(w_{j})_{0\leq j\leq p}\,;(s_{j})_{1\leq j\leq p})\!=\!(w^{\prime}_{j})_{0\leq j\leq p}\!\in\!(\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})^{p+1}
by setting

w^{\prime}_{0}\!=\!w_{0}(\cdot\wedge h(0))\quad\textrm{and}\quad w^{\prime}_{j+1}\!=\!\big(w^{\prime}_{j}\oplus_{m_{h}(s_{j},s_{j+1})}w_{j+1}\big)(\cdot\wedge h(s_{j+1})),\quad 0\leq j\!<\!p\;,

(2.79)

where we recall that
m_{h}(s_{j},s_{j+1})\!=\!\inf_{s\in[s_{j},s_{j+1}]}h(s)
. By (
2.78
)
and the continuity of the function
(h,(s_{j})_{0\leq j\leq p})\!\mapsto\!(h(0),(h(s_{j+1}),m_{h}(s_{j},s_{j+1}))_{0\leq j<p})
, we see that

F_{p}:\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{\!+}}\!\times\!(\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})^{p+1}\!\!\times\!\mathbb{R}_{{}^{+}}^{{}_{\downarrow p}}\longmapsto(\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})^{p+1}\quad\textrm{is continuous,}

(2.80)

where
\mathbb{R}_{{}^{+}}^{{}_{\downarrow p}}\!=\!\{(s_{j})_{1\leq j\leq p}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}^{p}:s_{1}\!<\!\ldots\!<\!s_{p}\}
. The functions
(F_{p})_{p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}

provide a convenient way of expressing the finite-dimensional marginal laws
of Brownian snakes. Recall that unless otherwise specified, all the r.v.s which are considered in this article
are defined on the same probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},{\bf P})
.

Definition 2.39
.

(Brownian snakes)

A
q
-dimensional Brownian snake is a r.v.
(H,W)\!:\!\Omega\!\rightarrow\!\bm{\Sigma}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
that is measurable with respect to
\mathscr{F}
and to the Borel
\sigma
-field of
\bm{\Sigma}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, such that for all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and all real numbers

0\!\leq\!s_{1}\!<\!\ldots\!<\!s_{p}
,

\big(W_{0},W_{\!s_{1}},\ldots,W_{\!s_{p}}\big)\;\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{=}\;F_{p}\big(H;(B^{(j)})_{0\leq j\leq p};(s_{j})_{1\leq j\leq p}\big)\;,

(2.81)

where
B^{(0)}\!=\!W_{0}
, where
(B^{(j)})_{1\leq j\leq p}
are independent standard
q
-dimensional Brownian motions that are moreover independent from
W_{0}
and
H
.
\hfill\square

Remark 2.40
.

(a)
First, it is easy to check that the laws provided by the left member in (
2.81
) actually form
a consistent family of finite-dimensional laws (so that the previous definition makes sense).
Next, since
\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})
is Polish, standard results ensure the existence of a regular version of the conditional law of
W
given
(W_{0},H)
, i.e., , a measurable transition kernel
P_{w_{0},h}(dW)
from
\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}}\!\times\!\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{\!+}}
to
\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})
such that
{\bf E}[G(W_{0},H){\bf 1}_{\{W\in A\}}]\!=\!{\bf E}[G(W_{0},H)P_{W_{0},H}(A)]
for all Borel subsets
A
of
\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})
and all nonnegative measurable functions
G
.

More precisely, the continuity (
2.80
) of
F_{p}
entails the following. Denote by
\Pi^{w_{0},h}_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{p}}
the law of
F_{p}(h;(B^{(j)})_{0\leq j\leq p};(s_{j})_{1\leq j\leq p})
where
B^{(0)}\!=\!w_{0}
and the
(B^{(j)})_{1\leq j\leq p}
are independent standard
q
-dimensional Brownian motions.
Then, there exists a Borel subset
B
of
\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}}\!\times\!\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{\!+}}
such that
{\bf P}((W_{0},H)\!\in\!B)\!=\!1

and such that for all
(w_{0},h)\!\in\!B
, for all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
and for all real numbers
0\!\leq\!

s_{1}

\!<\!

\ldots

\!<\!

s_{p}
,

\Pi^{w_{0},h}_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{p}}
is the law of
(W_{0},W_{s_{1}},\ldots,W_{s_{p}})
under
P_{w_{0},h}(dW)
.

(2.82)

(b)
The recursive construction (
2.79
) entails that conditionally given
(W_{0},H)
,
W
is a (time inhomogeneous) Markov process. More precisely, keeping notation from
(a)
, for all
(w_{0},h)\!\in\!B
we check that
W
under
P_{\!w_{0},h}
is an inhomogeneous Markov process with transition probabilities
P_{\!w_{0},h}(w_{s^{\prime}}\!\in\!dw^{\prime}\,|\,w_{s}\!=\!w)\!:=\!\Pi^{w,h(\cdot\,+s)}_{s^{\prime}-s}(dw^{\prime})
.
\hfill\square

Let us mention the following: given a continuous lifetime process
H
and an initial path
W_{0}
,
there is not necessarily a continuous snake

(H,W)
. However, when
H
stands for the
\alpha
-stable height process, there is a
continuous version of the Brownian snake as shown in the following proposition, which is a direct consequence of a result in D. & Le Gall
[
21
]
.

Proposition 2.41
.

Let
\alpha\!\in\!(1,2]
. Let
(H_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
be the
\alpha
-stable height process as defined by (
2.46
) (resp. under the normalized excursion law
\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,|\,\zeta\!>\!1)
or
\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,|\,\zeta\!=\!1)
). Then there exists a continuous one-dimensional Brownian snake
W
whose lifetime process is
H
which is called the
one-dimensional Brownian snake with
\alpha
-stable branching mechanism
.

Proof.
See
[
21
, Proposition 4.4.1]
when
H
is under
{\bf P}
. Standard arguments entail the result for
H
under
\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,|\,\zeta\!>\!1)
, then we use (
2.52
) to derive it under
\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,|\,\zeta\!=\!1)
. See
[
20
, Lemma 4.30]
for a more explicit discussion of the same ideas.
\hfill\blacksquare

Snake-pseudometrics and snake-trees.
Snakes encode the metric of the rescaled range of the BRWs that we consider here.

Definition 2.42
.

(
Snake-pseudometrics
) Let
\zeta\!\in\!(0,\infty)
and let
(h,w)\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}([0,\zeta])
. Recall from (
2.76
) the definition of
m_{h}(\cdot,\cdot)
and recall that
\widehat{w}_{s}\!=\!w_{s}(h(s))
,
s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
For all
s_{1},s_{2}\!\in\![0,\zeta]
, we set

\displaystyle M_{h,w}(s_{1},s_{2})\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\min\!\Big(\!\min\!\big\{w_{s_{1}}(r);r\!\geq\!m_{h}(s_{1},s_{2})\big\},\min\!\big\{w_{s_{2}}(r);r\!\geq\!m_{h}(s_{1},s_{2})\big\}\!\Big)

(2.83)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\textrm{and}\quad d_{h,w}(s_{1},s_{2})\!=\!\widehat{w}_{s_{1}}+\widehat{w}_{s_{2}}-2M_{h,w}(s_{1},s_{2})\;.

We call
d_{h,w}
the
snake-pseudometric associated with
(h,w)
(see Lemma
2.43
below).
\hfill\square

Lemma 2.43
.

Let
\zeta\!\in\!(0,\infty)
, let
(h,w)\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}([0,\zeta])
and let
d_{h,w}
be as in Definition
2.42
. Then,
d_{h,w}
is a continuous pseudometric on
[0,\zeta]
satisfying (
2.72
): namely
d_{h,w}\!\in\!\mathbf{MT}([0,\zeta])
.

Proof.
See
[
20
, Lemma 4.22]
.
\hfill\blacksquare

Definition 2.44
.

(
Snake-trees,
{\bm{\alpha}}
-stable Brownian cactus
) Let
\zeta\!\in\!(0,\infty)
, let
(h,w)\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}([0,\zeta])
and
d_{h,w}\!\in\!\mathbf{MT}([0,\zeta])
be as in Definition
2.42
.

(\mathbf{a})
We denote by

(T_{h,w},d_{h,w},r_{h,w},\mu_{h,w})
the pointed measured compact metric space induced by the pseudometric
d_{h,w}
as defined in (
2.73
). We call it the
snake-tree
associated with
(h,w)
. To simplify, we denote by
p_{h,w}:[0,\zeta]\!\rightarrow\!T_{h,w}
the canonical projection.

(\mathbf{b})
Let
\alpha\!\in\!(1,2]
and let
(H,W)
be a one-dimensional Brownian snake with
\alpha
-stable branching mechanism as defined in Proposition
2.41
. Then the random real tree

(T_{H,W},d_{H,W},r_{H,W},\mu_{H,W})
is called a
Brownian cactus with
\alpha
-stable branching mechanism
.
\hfill\square

As already mentioned, the Brownian Cactus has been introduced in Curien, Le Gall & Miermont
[
15
]
to study planar maps: roughly speaking the Brownian cactus corresponds to the case of a quadratic branching mechanism
\alpha\!=\!2
. In this case, they prove that a.s. its upper-local density for typical points is
4
(Proposition 5.1
[
15
]
p. 364) and that its Hausdorff dimension is
4
(Corollary 5.3
[
15
]
p. 365). See also Le Gall
[
37
]
where the level sets of the Brownian cactus are studied to derive results on the Brownian maps.
In D., K., Lin & Torri
[
20
, Lemma 4.34]
, it is proved that the Brownian cactus with
\alpha
-stable mechanism is a.s. a continuum real tree (i.e., ,
\mu_{H,W}
is diffuse and supported by the set of leaves of
T_{H,W}
) and that it is a binary real tree with a (necessarily countable) dense set of branch points: namely, a.s. for all

x\!\in\!T_{H,W}
,
T_{H,W}\backslash\{x\}
has one, two, or three connected components and the set of the
x
where
T_{H,W}\backslash\{x\}
has three connected components is dense in
T_{H,W}
.

Limits of snakes and snake-pseudometrics.
We first present a general result showing that convergence of snakes boils down to the convergence of the corresponding endpoint processes. This is a slight extension of the homeomorphism theorem of Marckert & Mokkadem
[
43
, Theorem 2.1]
. Then we recall from D., K., Lin & Torri
[
20
]
a result showing that the convergence of snakes implies the convergence of snake-pseudometrics, which leads to a pratical criterion to prove weak convergence of random snake-trees.

If
(h^{{}_{\!(n)}}\!\!,w^{{}_{\!(n)}})\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, is such that
(h^{{}_{\!(n)}}\!\!,\widehat{w}^{{}_{\!(n)}})\!\rightarrow\!(h,f)
in
\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{\!+}}\!\times\!\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}}
, then
f
is not necessarily the endpoint process of a continuous snake and the
w^{{}_{\!(n)}}
do not necessarily converge in the space
\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})
.
In this direction, we however
prove the following result.

Proposition 2.45
.

Let
(h^{{}_{\!(n)}}\!\!,w^{{}_{\!(n)}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of

q
-dimensional continuous snakes. We assume that there exists a
q
-dimensional continuous snake
(h,w)
such that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}h^{{}_{\!(n)}}\!\!=\!h
in
\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{\!+}}
, such that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}w_{{}^{0}}^{{}_{(n)}}\!=\!w_{0}
and such that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\widehat{w}^{{}_{(n)}}\!\!=\!\widehat{w}
in
\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}}
. Then
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}w^{{}_{(n)}}\!\!=\!w
in
\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})
.

Proof.
When
h^{{}_{\!(n)}}(0)\!=\!0
and
(h^{{}_{\!(n)}}\!\!,w^{{}_{\!(n)}})\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}_{q}([0,1])
for all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, the result follows from a direct application of Marckert & Mokkadem
[
43
, Theorem 2.1]
. By time-scaling, this still holds when there is some
N\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
such that
h^{{}_{\!(n)}}(0)\!=\!0
and
(h^{{}_{\!(n)}}\!\!,w^{{}_{\!(n)}})\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}_{q}([0,2N\!+\!1])
for all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
. Now, we claim that the desired result holds when
h^{{}_{\!(n)}}(0)\!=\!0
for all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
. Indeed, using Remark
2.38

(e)
, the previous particular case implies that
\lim_{n\to\infty}R_{N}(h^{{}_{\!(n)}}\!\!,w^{{}_{\!(n)}})\!=\!R_{N}(h,w)
in
\bm{\Sigma}_{q}([0,2N\!+\!1])
for any
N\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, so
w^{{}_{(n)}}\!\!\to\!w
uniformly on
[0,N]
. Now, we prove the desired result without any additional assumptions thanks to Remark
2.38

(f)
. We easily see that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\underline{h}^{{}_{\!(n)}}\!=\!\underline{h}
in
\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{\!+}}
and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\widehat{\underline{w}}^{{}_{(n)}}\!=\!\widehat{\underline{w}}
in
\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}}
, so
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\underline{w}^{{}_{(n)}}\!=\!\underline{w}
in
\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{q}})
. Then, we observe that

\forall s,r\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\quad w_{s}(r)=w_{0}\big(r\wedge m_{h}(0,s)\big)+\underline{w}_{s}\big((r-m_{h}(0,s))_{+}\big),

which completes the proof.
\hfill\blacksquare

The following lemma recalled from D., K., Lin & Torri
[
20
]
. It shows that the convergence of one-dimensional snakes implies the convergence of the associated pseudometrics. Recall that
\bm{\Sigma}([0,\zeta])
stands for the set of one-dimensional snakes. For all
(h,w)\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}([0,\zeta])
, recall from Example
2.36
the definition of the real-tree pseudometric
d_{h}
and recall from Definition
2.42
the snake-pseudometric
d_{h,w}
. Recall that
\lVert\cdot\rVert
stands for the uniform norm on

\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R})
.

Lemma 2.46
.

Let
\zeta\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We recall that
\bm{\Sigma}([0,\zeta])
stands for the set of one-dimensional snakes.
For all
(h,w)\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}([0,\zeta])
, we recall from Example
2.36
the definition of the real-tree pseudometric
d_{h}
and we recall from Definition
2.42
the snake-pseudometric
d_{h,w}
.
We denote by
\lVert\cdot\rVert
the supremum norm on
\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R})
.
For all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, let
(h^{{}_{\!(n)}}\!\!,w^{{}_{\!(n)}})\!\in\!\bm{\Sigma}([0,\zeta])
. We suppose that
(h^{{}_{\!(n)}}\!\!,w^{{}_{\!(n)}})\!\to\!(h,w)
in
\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{\!+}}\!\times\!\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta],\mathbf{C}^{{}_{0}}_{{}^{1}})
. Then,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lVert d_{h^{(n)}}\!-\!d_{h}\rVert\!=\!\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lVert d_{h^{(n)},w^{(n)}}\!-\!d_{h,w}\rVert\!=\!0
.

Proof.
See D., K., Lin & Torri
[
20
, Lemma 4.20]
.
\hfill\blacksquare

2.8
Tree-valued branching walks and snake-pseudometrics

We fix an environment
(T,o)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
.
We then fix a finite tree
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
and a branching walk

\Theta\!:=\!(t,\varnothing;\overline{Y}_{u}\!=\!(T,Y_{u}),u\!\in\!t)
such that:
(a)

Y_{\varnothing}\!=\!o
;
(b)
if
v,v^{\prime}\!\in\!t
are neighbors, so are
Y_{v}
and
Y_{v^{\prime}}
in
T
.
In this section, we consider the range
R\!=\!\{Y_{u};u\!\in\!t\}
that is a graph subtree of
T
and we explain that
R
is close in some sense to the snake-tree associated with the contour snake of the relative heights of the branching walk, i.e., , the contour snake associated with the
\mathbb{R}
-branching walk
(\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u}\bm{|}\!\!|)_{u\in t}
.

To that end, we denote by
(\widetilde{t},d_{\widetilde{t}})

the real tree obtained by joining adjacent vertices of
t

by a
segment isometric to
[0,1]
. We assume that
t\!\subset\!\widetilde{t}
.
Thus
d_{\mathtt{gr}}(u,v)\!=\!d_{\widetilde{t}}(u,v)
for all
u,v\!\in\!t
, where
d_{\mathtt{gr}}
stands for the graph distance in
t
. For all
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\!\in\!\widetilde{t}
, we denote by
[\![\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}]\!]_{\widetilde{t}}
the geodesic path joining
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
in
\widetilde{t}
.

We then introduce the
contour exploration of
\widetilde{t}
as follows. We denote by
(v_{k})_{0\leq k\leq 2(\#t-1)}
the vertices of
t
listed in the contour order.
We easily check that there exists a continuous function
\widetilde{v}\!:\![0,2(\#t\!-\!1)]\!\rightarrow\!\widetilde{t}
such that for all
0\!\leq\!k\!<\!2(\#t\!-\!1)
and for all
s\!\in\![0,1]
,

\widetilde{v}(k+s)\!\in\![\![v_{k},v_{k+1}]\!]_{\widetilde{t}}\quad\textrm{is such that}\quad d_{\widetilde{t}}(v_{k},\widetilde{v}(k+s))\!=\!s.

(2.84)

It is convenient to set
\widetilde{v}(s)\!=\!\varnothing
for all
s\!\in\![2(\#t\!-\!1),2\#t]
.
The continuous path
(\widetilde{v}(s))_{s\in[0,2\#t]}
is the contour exploration of
\widetilde{t}
. Namely for all
s,s^{\prime}\!\in\![0,2\#t]
,

d_{\widetilde{t}}\big((\widetilde{v}(s),(\widetilde{v}(s^{\prime})\big)=d_{C(t)}(s,s^{\prime})

where
d_{C(t)}
is the real tree distance associated with the contour function
(C_{s}(t))_{s\in[0,2\#t]}
as introduced in Example
2.36
.

Remark 2.47
.

It is easy to see that the tree
(T_{C(t)},d_{C(t)},\mathtt{r}_{C(t)},\mathtt{m}_{C(t)})
coded by
C_{\cdot}(t)
as introduced in Example
2.36
is isometric to

(\widetilde{t},d_{\widetilde{t}},\varnothing,\widetilde{\mathtt{m}})
where
\widetilde{\mathtt{m}}
is the occupation measure of the exploration process
\widetilde{v}
. As a consequence of D., K., Lin & Torri
[
20
, Lemma 5.2]
, we get that
d^{\widetilde{t}}_{\mathtt{Prok}}\big(\mathtt{m},\tfrac{{}_{1}}{{}^{2}}\widetilde{\mathtt{m}}\big)\!\leq\!2
where
\mathtt{m}\!=\!\sum_{v\in t}\delta_{v}
, which implies that
{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}\big((t,d_{\mathtt{gr}},\varnothing,\mathtt{m}),(\widetilde{t},d_{\widetilde{t}},\varnothing,\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{2}}\widetilde{\mathtt{m}}\big)\big)\!\leq\!3
.
\hfill\square

Similarly, we denote by
(\widetilde{T},d_{\widetilde{T}})

the real tree obtained by joining adjacent vertices of
T

by a segment isometric to
[0,1]
and we extend relative heights on
\widetilde{T}
by setting

\forall x\!\in\!T,\quad\forall\gamma\in[\![\overleftarrow{x},x]\!]_{\widetilde{T}},\quad\bm{|}\!\!|\gamma\bm{|}\!\!|\!=\!\bm{|}\!\!|\overleftarrow{x}\bm{|}\!\!|+d_{\widetilde{T}}(\overleftarrow{x},\gamma)\;.

For all
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\!\in\!\widetilde{T}
, we also denote by
\gamma\wedge\gamma^{\prime}
the most recent common ancestor of
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
that is defined as the unique point of
[\![\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}]\!]_{\widetilde{T}}
with minimal relative height.
We finally define a continuous function
\widetilde{Y}\!:\!\widetilde{t}\!\to\!\widetilde{T}
that extends
Y
as follows:
for all
\sigma\!\in\!\widetilde{t}
there exist two neighboring vertices
v,v^{\prime}\!\in\!t
such that
\sigma\!\in\![\![v,v^{\prime}]\!]_{\widetilde{t}}
and we define

\widetilde{Y}_{\sigma}
as the only point of
[\![Y_{v},Y_{v^{\prime}}]\!]_{\widetilde{T}}
such that

d_{\widetilde{T}}(Y_{v},\widetilde{Y}_{\sigma})\!=\!d_{\widetilde{t}}(v,\sigma)
.

(2.85)

We denote by
\widetilde{R}
the range of
\widetilde{Y}
and we denote by
\widetilde{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathtt{occ}}
the occupation measure on
\widetilde{R}
yielded by the contour exploration of
\widetilde{t}
. Namely,

\widetilde{R}\!=\!\big\{\widetilde{Y}_{\sigma}\,;\,\sigma\!\in\!\widetilde{t}\big\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\int_{\widetilde{R}}\!f(\gamma)\,\widetilde{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathtt{occ}}(d\gamma)\,=\,\int_{0}^{2\#t}\!\!\!\!\!f\big(\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(s)}\big)\,ds.

(2.86)

We also denote by
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{occ}}
the occuptation measure of
Y
on
R\!=\!\{Y_{u}\,;\,u\!\in\!t\}
:

\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{occ}}=\!=\!\sum_{u\in t}\delta_{Y_{u}}
. We note that
d^{\widetilde{T}}_{\mathtt{Haus}}\big(\widetilde{R},R\big)\!\leq\!1
. Then we recall from D. , K. , Lin & Torri
[
20
, Lemma 5.2]
that

d^{\widetilde{T}}_{\mathtt{Prok}}\big(\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{occ}},\tfrac{{}_{1}}{{}^{2}}\widetilde{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathtt{occ}}\big)\!\leq\!2\quad\textrm{and thus}\quad{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}\big((R,d_{\mathtt{gr}},o,\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{occ}}),(\widetilde{R},d_{\widetilde{T}},o,\frac{{}_{{}_{1}}}{{}^{{}^{2}}}\widetilde{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathtt{occ}})\big)\!\leq\!3.

(2.87)

We remind that
d^{\widetilde{T}}_{\mathtt{Haus}}
and
d^{\widetilde{T}}_{\mathtt{Prok}}
stand respectively
for the Hausdorff distance on compact subsets of
\widetilde{T}
and for the Prokhorov distance on the space of finite Borel measures on of
\widetilde{T}
.

We next introduce the
range pseudometric

d_{\widetilde{R}}(\cdot,\cdot)\!\in\!\mathbf{M}([0,2\#t])
by setting for all
s,s^{\prime}\!\in\![0,2\#t]

d_{\widetilde{R}}(s,s^{\prime})=d_{\widetilde{T}}\big(\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(s)},\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(s^{\prime})}\big)=\bm{|}\!\!|\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(s)}\bm{|}\!\!|+\bm{|}\!\!|\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(s^{\prime})}\bm{|}\!\!|-2\bm{|}\!\!|\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(s)}\wedge\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(s^{\prime})}\bm{|}\!\!|.

(2.88)

We observe that
d_{\widetilde{R}}
is a real tree pseudometric and that

\big(\widetilde{R},d_{\widetilde{T}},o,\widetilde{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathtt{occ}}\big)\quad\textrm{is isometric to}\quad\big(E_{d_{\widetilde{R}}},d_{\widetilde{R}},\mathtt{r}_{d_{\widetilde{R}}},\mathtt{m}_{d_{\widetilde{R}}}\big)

(2.89)

where
E_{d_{\widetilde{R}}}
is the real tree yielded by
d_{\widetilde{R}}
as explained in (
2.73
) and (
2.74
).

Remark 2.48
.

The previous constructions on the real trees
\widetilde{T}
and
\widetilde{t}
only depend on the branching walk
\Theta\!=\!(t,\varnothing;\overline{Y}_{u}\!=\!(T,Y_{u}),u\!\in\!t)
locally in a continuous way. Thus
\Theta\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet})\!\mapsto\!d_{\widetilde{R}}(\cdot,\cdot)\!\in\!\mathbf{M}([0,2\#t])
is continuous.
\hfill\square

We now compare
d_{\widetilde{R}}
with the snake-pseudometric associated with the relative heights of the branching walk.
We next denote by
(W_{s}(t;\cdot))_{s\in[0,2\#t]}
the contour snake associated with the
\mathbb{R}
-valued branching walk
(\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u}\bm{|}\!\!|)_{u\in t}
(see Definition
2.8
). We denote by
(\widehat{W}_{s}(t))_{s\in[0,2\#t]}
its associated endpoint process
and we recall that the corresponding lifetime process is the contour process
(C_{s}(t))_{s\in[0,2\#]}
. We then easily check for all
s\!\in\![0,2\#t]
and
r\!\in\![0,C_{s}(t)]
that

W_{s}(t;r)=\bm{|}\!\!|\widetilde{Y}_{\sigma}\bm{|}\!\!|\quad\textrm{where $\sigma\!\in\![\![\varnothing,\widetilde{v}(s)]\!]_{\widetilde{t}}$ is such that $d_{\widetilde{t}}(\varnothing,\sigma)\!=\!r$. }

(2.90)

In particular, note that
\widehat{W}_{s}(t)\!=\!\bm{|}\!\!|\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(s)}\bm{|}\!\!|
.
We next recall from (
2.83
) in Definition

2.42
that
d_{C(t),W(t;\cdot)}
stands for the snake-pseudometric associated with
(C(t),W(t;\cdot))
.
As a consequence of (
2.90
), we see for all
s,s^{\prime}\!\in\![0,2\#t]
that

d_{C(t),W(t;\cdot)}(s,s^{\prime})=\bm{|}\!\!|\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(s)}\bm{|}\!\!|+\bm{|}\!\!|\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(s^{\prime})}\bm{|}\!\!|-2\min\big\{\,\bm{|}\!\!|\widetilde{Y}_{\sigma}\bm{|}\!\!|\,;\sigma\!\in\![\![\widetilde{v}(s),\widetilde{v}(s^{\prime})]\!]_{\widetilde{t}}\,\big\}.

(2.91)

We then observe that

0\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\big(d_{C(t),W(t;\cdot)}(s,s^{\prime})-d_{\widetilde{R}}(s,s^{\prime})\big)=\bm{|}\!\!|\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(s)}\wedge\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(s^{\prime})}\bm{|}\!\!|-\min\big\{\bm{|}\!\!|\widetilde{Y}_{\sigma}\bm{|}\!\!|\,;\sigma\!\in\![\![\widetilde{v}(s),\widetilde{v}(s^{\prime})]\!]_{\widetilde{t}}\big\}.

(2.92)

When
Y
is a BRW whose spatial motion is a biased RW, (
2.92
) implies that
d_{C(t),W(t;\cdot)}
and
d_{\widetilde{R}}
are close because for the difference in (
2.92
) to be large, two independent and large parts of the BRW have to coincide. More precisely, we conclude this section by proving the following lemma that shows (for fixed times) that the difference in (
2.92
) is exponentially small
when
Y
is a (sub)critical biased BRW.

Lemma 2.49
.

Let
\lambda\!\in\!(1,\infty)
and let
(T,o)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
be such that
T\neq\{\varnothing\}
. Let
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
be finite. Let
\bm{\Theta}\!=\!(t,\varnothing;\overline{Y}_{\!u}\!=\!(T,Y_{u}),u\!\in\!t)
be a
t
-indexed
T
-valued
\lambda
-biased BRW whose law is
Q^{T,o}_{t,\varnothing}
(Definition
2.25
). If Assumption (
2.27
) in Lemma
2.19
is verified, then for all
\xi\!\in\!(1,\infty)
,

{\bf P}\Big(\!\!\!\!\!\!\max_{\hskip 8.19447pts,s^{\prime}\in[0,2\#t]}\!\big|d_{C(t),W(t\,;\,\cdot)}(s,s^{\prime})\!-\!d_{\widetilde{R}}(s,s^{\prime})\big|\,>\,2\xi\Big)\leq\lambda^{2}(\#t)^{3}\,\lambda^{-\xi}.

(2.93)

Proof.
By (
2.92
) and the definition on
\widetilde{T}
of relative heights and of most recent common ancestors, we see that if there are
s_{1},s_{2}\!\in\![0,2\#t]
such that

d_{C(t),W(t;\cdot)}(s_{1},s_{2})\!-\!d_{\widetilde{R}}(s_{1},s_{2})\!>\!2\xi
, then there are
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\!\in\!t
such that

\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u^{\prime}}\wedge Y_{v^{\prime}}\bm{|}\!\!|\!-\!\min_{w\in[\![u^{\prime},v^{\prime}]\!]}\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{w}\bm{|}\!\!|\!>\!\xi\!-\!1\!>\!0
. Then, let us choose
w^{\prime}\!\in\![\![u^{\prime},v^{\prime}]\!]
such that
\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{w^{\prime}}\bm{|}\!\!|\!=\!\min_{w\in[\![u^{\prime},v^{\prime}]\!]}\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{w}\bm{|}\!\!|
and with the greatest height possible. Since
\min(\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{\!u^{\prime}}\bm{|}\!\!|,\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{\!v^{\prime}}\bm{|}\!\!|)\!\geq\!\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{\!u^{\prime}}\wedge Y_{\!v^{\prime}}\bm{|}\!\!|\!>\!\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{\!w^{\prime}}\bm{|}\!\!|
, we see that
w^{\prime}\!\notin\!\{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\}
. Therefore,
without loss of generality, we can assume that
w^{\prime}\!\in\![\![u^{\prime}\wedge v^{\prime},u^{\prime}[\![\,
and we can find
w\!\in\,]\!]u^{\prime}\wedge v^{\prime},u^{\prime}]\!]
such that
\overleftarrow{w}\!=\!w^{\prime}
. By definition of
w^{\prime}
and
w
, we observe that
\overleftarrow{Y_{w}}=Y_{w^{\prime}}
, that
Y_{u^{\prime}}\wedge Y_{v^{\prime}}\!\in\![\![Y_{w^{\prime}},Y_{v^{\prime}}]\!]
, and that
Y_{u^{\prime}}\wedge Y_{v^{\prime}}\!\in\![\![Y_{w},Y_{u^{\prime}}]\!]
. Thus, there are
u,v\!\in\!t
such that
v\wedge w\!\neq\!w
,
w\!\preceq\!u
and
Y_{u}\!=\!Y_{v}\!=\!Y_{u^{\prime}}\wedge Y_{v^{\prime}}
. Consequently, (
2.93
) is derived from the following bound that holds for all
u,v,w\!\in\!t
with
v\wedge w
and
w\!\neq\!w\preceq u
, and all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
,

{\bf P}\Big(\,Y_{u}=Y_{v}\,,\,\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{v}\bm{|}\!\!|-\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{w}\bm{|}\!\!|\geq p\,,\,\overleftarrow{Y_{w}}\notin\{Y_{a}:a\!\in\![\![w,u]\!]\}\,\Big)\leq\lambda^{-p}.

(2.94)

It remains to prove (
2.94
). To that end, recall from Definition
2.17
that
P_{T\!\!,\,x}
stands for the law of the
\lambda
-biased RW
\overline{X}_{n}\!=\!(T,X_{n})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
on
T
such that
X_{0}=x
. By reasoning conditionally given
(Y_{w},Y_{v})
, the very definition of BRWs implies that

{\bf P}\Big(\,Y_{u}=Y_{v}\,,\,\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{v}\bm{|}\!\!|-\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{w}\bm{|}\!\!|\geq p\,,\,\overleftarrow{Y_{w}}\notin\{Y_{a}:a\!\in\![\![w,u]\!]\}\,\Big)\\
={\bf E}\Big[\bm{1}_{\{\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{v}\bm{|}\!\!|-\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{w}\bm{|}\!\!|\geq p\}}P_{T,Y_{w}}\big(X_{|u|-|w|}=Y_{v}\,,\,\overleftarrow{Y_{w}}\notin\{X_{j}:j\leq|u|-|w|\}\,\big|\,Y_{w},Y_{v}\big)\Big].

For all
x,y\!\in\!T\backslash\{\varnothing\}
, we set
f(x,y)\!=\!P_{T\!\!,\,x}\big(\mathtt{H}_{y}\!<\!\mathtt{H}_{\overleftarrow{x}}\big)
, where
\mathtt{H}_{y}
is the hitting time as in (
2.26
). Then the previous identity yields that

{\bf P}\Big(Y_{u}=Y_{v}\,,\,\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{v}\bm{|}\!\!|-\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{w}\bm{|}\!\!|\geq p\,,\,\overleftarrow{Y_{w}}\notin\{Y_{a}:a\!\in\![\![w,u]\!]\}\Big)\ \leq\ {\bf E}\Big[\bm{1}_{\{\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{v}\bm{|}\!\!|-\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{w}\bm{|}\!\!|\geq p\}}f(Y_{w},Y_{v})\Big]

(2.95)

Thanks to Assumption (
2.27
), we can use (
2.30
) to get
f(x,y)\leq\lambda^{\bm{|}\!\!|x\bm{|}\!\!|-\bm{|}\!\!|y\bm{|}\!\!|}
for all
x,y\!\in\!T\backslash\{\varnothing\}
. Together with (
2.95
), this immediately entails (
2.94
), which completes the proof.
\hfill\blacksquare

3
Limit of the
\tau_{\infty}
-indexed BRW and its snakes

3.1
Harmonic coordinates and related estimates.

One key-ingredient of the proof in Peres & Zeitouni
[
45
]
of the quenched CLT for a critical biased RW

(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in an invariant
GW tree is the
harmonic coordinates
, which are
\mathbb{R}
-valued
measurable functions
(S_{x})_{x\in\mathbf{T}}
of the environment
\mathbf{T}
such that on one hand
(S_{X_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a martingale to which conditional CLT applies, and on the other hand such that
S_{X_{n}}\sim\mathtt{Cst}\bm{|}\!\!|X_{n}\bm{|}\!\!|
. This idea is actually adapted to BRWs to prove the main result of the paper.
In this section, we recall from Peres & Zeitouni
[
45
]

the construction of the harmonic coordinates of (variants of) the invariant GW(
\nu
)-tree
\mathbf{T}
(within our framework of ordered trees) and we prove several estimates that are used in our proofs.
The following lemma summarizes definitions, notation, and the main properties of harmonic coordinates. Its
proof is adapted from Peres & Zeitouni
[
45
]
and Dembo & Sun
[
16
]
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\nu
be a supercritical offspring distribution whose mean is denoted by
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
.
We assume that there exists
\mathtt{b}^{\prime}\!\in\!(1,\infty)
such that
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}(\mathtt{b}^{\prime})\!:=\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k^{\mathtt{b}^{\prime}}\nu(k)\!<\!\infty
.
For all rooted ordered tree
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
as in Definition
2.1
, we next set

\mathtt{U}(t)=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\;\mathtt{m}_{\nu}^{-n}\,\#\big\{u\!\in\!t:|u|\!=\!n\big\}\in[0,\infty]\;.

(3.1)

Here,
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
is either a GW(
\nu
)-tree as in Definition
2.6

(\textbf{a})
(in this case:
\mathtt{o}\!=\!\varnothing
), or an infinite GW(
\nu
)-tree as in Definition
2.16

(\textbf{b})
, or an invariant GW(
\nu
)-tree as in
Definition
2.21
.
For all
x\!\in\!\mathbf{T}
, we
recall from (
2.17
) that the subtree
\theta_{x}\mathbf{T}
stemming from
x
belongs to
\mathbb{T}
and we set
U_{x}=\mathtt{U}(\theta_{x}\mathbf{T})
. Then the following holds true.

(i)

{\bf P}
-a.s. for all
x\!\in\!\mathbf{T}
,

U_{x}\!=\!\lim_{n\to\infty}\;\mathtt{m}_{\nu}^{-n}\,\#\big\{y\!\in\!\mathbf{T}:x\preceq y,\,\bm{|}\!\!|y\bm{|}\!\!|\!=\!\bm{|}\!\!|x\bm{|}\!\!|\!+\!n\,\big\}\!\in\!(0,\infty)\quad\textrm{and}\quad U_{x}=\frac{1}{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\hskip 16.38895pty\in\mathbf{T}:\overleftarrow{y}=x}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!U_{y}.

(3.2)

(ii)

Suppose that
\mathtt{b}\!\in\!(1,\infty)
is such that
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k^{\mathtt{b}}\nu(k)\!<\!\infty
.
Then,

{\bf E}[U_{\mathtt{o}}^{\mathtt{b}}]\!<\!\infty
if
\mathbf{T}
is a GW(
\nu
)-tree or an infinite
GW(
\nu
)-tree. If
\mathbf{T}
is
an invariant GW(
\nu
)-tree, then
{\bf E}[U_{\mathtt{o}}^{\mathtt{b}-1}]\!<\!\infty
if
\mathtt{b}\!\in\!(2,\infty)
.

(iii)

The harmonic coordinates are the
\mathbb{R}
-valued r.v.
(S_{x})_{x\in\mathbf{T}}
defined by the following

S_{\mathtt{o}}\!=\!0\quad\textrm{and}\quad\forall x\!\in\!\mathbf{T}\backslash\{\varnothing\},\quad S_{x}\!-\!S_{\overleftarrow{x}}\!=\!U_{x},

(3.3)

on the event where (
3.2
) holds true and by
S_{x}\!=\!0
,
x\!\in\!\mathbf{T}
, on the
{\bf P}
-negligible complementary event.
Let
\overline{X}_{n}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},X_{n})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, be a
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RW such that
X_{0}\!=\!\mathtt{o}
. We denote by
\mathscr{F}_{\!n}
the
\sigma
-field generated by
(\overline{X}_{k})_{0\leq k\leq n}
. Then
(S_{X_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a
(\mathscr{F}_{\!n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
-martingale:

\textrm{${\bf P}$-a.s.\penalty\ for all $n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}$, }\quad{\bf E}\big[S_{X_{n+1}}\,\big|\,\mathscr{F}_{\!n}\big]=S_{X_{n}}

(3.4)

We keep the previous notation and for all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, we set
\Delta_{n}\!=\!S_{X_{n+1}}\!-\!S_{X_{n}}
and

\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\overline{X}_{n})\!=\!{\bf E}\big[|\Delta_{n}|^{\mathtt{b}}\big|\mathscr{F}_{\!n}\big]
. Then,

\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\overline{X}_{n})\leq\frac{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}^{\mathtt{b}}+\mathtt{m}_{\nu}}{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}\!+\!k_{X_{n}}\!(\mathbf{T})}U^{\mathtt{b}}_{X_{n}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\overline{X}_{n})\!=\!\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}\big(\mathtt{cent}(\overline{X}_{n})\big)\;.

(3.5)

If
\mathtt{b}\!\in\!(1,\infty)
is such that
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k^{\mathtt{b}}\nu(k)\!<\!\infty
and if
\mathbf{T}
is an invariant GW(
\nu
)-tree, then
{\bf E}[\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\overline{X}_{n})]\!=\!{\bf E}[\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\overline{X}_{0})]\!<\!\infty
.
In the specific case where
\mathtt{b}\!=\!2
, then

{\bf E}\big[\Phi_{2}(\overline{X}_{n})\big]\!=\!\frac{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}(2)\!-\!\mathtt{m}_{\nu}}{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}^{2}\!-\!\mathtt{m}_{\nu}}=:\sigma_{\nu}^{2}.

(3.6)

Proof.
We prove
(i)
. To that end, let us first consider the case where
\mathbf{T}
is GW(
\nu
)-tree as in Definition
2.6

(\textbf{a})
. By standard arguments about martingales, a
\mathtt{b}^{\prime}
-moments for
\nu
with
\mathtt{b}^{\prime}\!\in\!(1,\infty)
implies
that
{\bf P}
-a.s.
\mathtt{U}(\mathbf{T})\!=\!\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathtt{m}_{\nu}^{-n}\#\{x\!\in\!\mathbf{T}:|x|\!=\!n\}\!\in\!(0,\infty)
. Next observe that for all
x\!\in\!\mathbb{U}
such that
{\bf P}(x\!\in\!\mathbf{T})\!>\!0
, the subtree
\theta_{x}\mathbf{T}
under
{\bf P}(\,\cdot\,|\,x\!\in\!\mathbf{T})
has the same law as
\mathbf{T}
. Since
\mathbb{U}
is countable,
{\bf P}
-a.s. (
3.2
) holds true for all

x\!\in\!\mathbf{T}
.

Suppose next that
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
is an infinite GW(
\nu
)-tree or an invariant GW(
\nu
)-tree.
In these cases, conditionally given
\partial L_{\mathtt{o}}
, the subtrees
\theta_{y}\mathbf{T}
,
y\!\in\!\mathbf{T}\backslash L_{\mathtt{o}}
, are independent GW(
\nu
)-trees. Therefore
{\bf P}
-a.s. (
3.2
) holds true for all
x\!\in\!\mathbf{T}\backslash L_{\mathtt{o}}
. Now
note that (
3.2
) provides a unambiguous definition of the weight
U_{x}
for
x\!\in\!L_{\mathtt{o}}
, which completes the proof of
(i)
.

Let us prove
(ii)
. We first suppose that
\mathbf{T}
is a GW(
\nu
)-tree or an infinite GW(
\nu
)-tree: in both cases

\theta_{\mathtt{o}}\mathbf{T}
is GW(
\nu
)-tree and as proved in Dembo & Sun
[
16
, Lemma 6.2]
,

{\bf E}[U_{\mathtt{o}}^{\mathtt{b}}]\!<\!\infty
, which implies the first part of
(ii)
.

Next, assume that
\mathbf{T}
is an invariant GW(
\nu
)-tree and that
\mathtt{b}\!\in\!(2,\infty)
. By convexity of
x\mapsto x^{\mathtt{b}-1}
and the second equality in (
3.2
),
U_{\mathtt{o}}^{\mathtt{b}-1}\!\leq\!\mathtt{m}^{{}_{-(\mathtt{b}-1)}}_{{}^{\nu}}k_{\mathtt{o}}(\mathbf{T})^{\mathtt{b}-2}\sum_{x\in\mathbf{T}:\overleftarrow{x}=\mathtt{o}}U_{x}^{\mathtt{b}-1}
. By (
2.33
) in the definition of invariant GW(
\nu
)-trees, we easily get
{\bf E}[U^{\mathtt{b}-1}_{\mathtt{o}}|k_{\mathtt{o}}(\mathbf{T})]\!\leq\!\mathtt{m}^{{}_{-(\mathtt{b}-1)}}_{{}^{\nu}}{\bf E}\big[\mathtt{U}(\mathbf{T}_{0})^{\mathtt{b}-1}\big]\,k_{\mathtt{o}}(\mathbf{T})^{\mathtt{b}-1}
, where
\mathbf{T}_{0}
is a GW(
\nu
)-tree. Thus

{\bf E}[U^{\mathtt{b}-1}_{\!\mathtt{o}}]\!\leq\!\tfrac{1}{2}\mathtt{m}_{\nu}^{-\mathtt{b}}{\bf E}[\mathtt{U}(\mathbf{T}_{0})^{\mathtt{b}-1}]

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}(\mathtt{m}_{\nu}+k)k^{\mathtt{b}-1}\nu(k)\!<\!\infty
, which completes the proof of
(ii)
.

To prove
(iii)
, we observe that

\displaystyle{\bf E}\big[S_{X_{n+1}}\!\!\!-\!S_{X_{n}}\big|\mathscr{F}_{\!n}\big]\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!(S_{\overleftarrow{X}_{n}}\!\!\!-\!S_{X_{n}}){\bf E}\big[{\bf 1}_{\{X_{n+1}=\overleftarrow{X}_{n}\}}\big|\mathscr{F}_{\!n}\big]+\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\hskip 16.38895pty\in\mathbf{T}:\overleftarrow{y}=X_{n}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(S_{y}\!-\!S_{X_{n}}){\bf E}\big[{\bf 1}_{\{X_{n+1}=y\}}\big|\mathscr{F}_{\!n}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}U_{X_{n}}}{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}+k_{X_{n}}(\mathbf{T})}+\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\hskip 16.38895pty\in\mathbf{T}:\overleftarrow{y}=X_{n}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{U_{y}}{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}+k_{X_{n}}(\mathbf{T})}=0

by the second equality in (
3.2
). This completes the proof of
(iii)
.

Let us prove
(iv)
. We first observe that

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\overline{X}_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}\!+\!k_{X_{n}}\!(\mathbf{T})}\Big(\mathtt{m}_{\nu}U_{X_{n}}^{\mathtt{b}}+\sum_{y\in\mathbf{T}}U_{y}^{\mathtt{b}}{\bf 1}_{\{\overleftarrow{y}=X_{n}\}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}\!+\!k_{X_{n}}\!(\mathbf{T})}\Big(\mathtt{m}_{\nu}U_{X_{n}}^{\mathtt{b}}+\Big(\sum_{y\in\mathbf{T}}U_{y}{\bf 1}_{\{\overleftarrow{y}=X_{n}\}}\Big)^{\mathtt{b}}\Big)=\frac{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}^{\mathtt{b}}+\mathtt{m}_{\nu}}{\mathtt{m}_{\nu}\!+\!k_{X_{n}}\!(\mathbf{T})}U^{\mathtt{b}}_{X_{n}}

since
s^{\mathtt{b}}+r^{\mathtt{b}}\leq(s+r)^{\mathtt{b}}
for all
s,r\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
and by the second equality in (
3.2
). This proves the inequality in (
3.5
).
Then, recall from (
2.12
) the definition of the shifts
\varphi_{l}
,
l\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
, and
\mathtt{cent}(\cdot)
from Definition
2.14
and observe that for all
x\!\in\!\mathbf{T}
,
\mathtt{U}(\theta_{x}\mathbf{T})\!=\!\mathtt{U}(\theta_{\varphi_{l}(x)}\varphi_{l}(\mathbf{T}))
and that
k_{x}(\mathbf{T})\!=\!k_{\varphi_{l}(x)}(\varphi_{l}(\mathbf{T}))
, which easily implies the equality in (
3.5
).

Next we suppose that
\mathbf{T}
is invariant. By Proposition
2.22
, the law of
\overline{X}_{n}
is then invariant and

{\bf E}[\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\overline{X}_{n})]\!=\!{\bf E}[\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\overline{X}_{0})]
. By (
3.5
), we then get

{\bf E}\big[\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\overline{X}_{0})\big]\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathtt{m}_{\nu}^{\mathtt{b}-1}\!+1){\bf E}\big[\mathtt{U}(\mathbf{T}_{0})^{\mathtt{b}}\big]<\infty

where
\mathbf{T}_{0}
a GW(
\nu
)-tree, by (
2.33
) in the definition of invariant GW(
\nu
)-trees.
In the special case
\mathtt{b}\!=\!2
, we then get

\displaystyle{\bf E}\big[\Phi_{2}(\mathbf{T},\varrho)\big]

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2\mathtt{m}_{\nu}}{\bf E}\Big[\mathtt{m}_{\nu}\mathtt{U}(\mathbf{T}_{0})^{2}+\!\sum_{u\in\mathbf{T}_{0}}\!\mathtt{U}(\theta_{u}\mathbf{T}_{0})^{2}{\bf 1}_{\{\overleftarrow{u}=\varnothing\}}\Big]

\displaystyle\!=\!
