\pi_{k}:\big(\mathtt{u},(\mathtt{z}_{1}(s),\mathtt{z}_{2}(s),\mathtt{z}_{3}(s))_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\big)\!\in\!\mathbb{R}^{2}\!\times\!\mathbf{D}_{3}\mapsto\mathtt{z}_{k}(\cdot)\!\in\!\mathbf{D}_{1}
is continuous.

(iv)

Let
k\!\in\!\{1,2,3\}
. For all
\mathtt{Q}\!\in\!\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\!\times\!\mathbf{D}_{3})
, we set
\mathtt{Q}^{k}\!=\!\mathtt{Q}\circ\pi^{-1}_{k}\!\in\!\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbf{D}_{1})
. Then
\mathtt{Q}\mapsto\mathtt{Q}^{k}
is continuous.

Proof.
For
(i)
, see e.g. Jacod & Shiryaev
[
29
, Chapter VI, Proposition 2.2 (b) p. 338]
. For
(ii)
, see e.g.
[
29
, Chapter VI, Proposition 2.1 (b5), p. 337]
. The last two points are immediate.
\hfill\blacksquare

By (
3.13
), by the independence of
\tau_{\infty}
from
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
and by Proposition
3.18
, we see that conditionally given
\mathcal{G}
the laws of the processes

\big(\tfrac{1}{n}\mathfrak{g}_{n},\tfrac{1}{n}\mathfrak{d}_{n},\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{\lfloor n\cdot\rfloor}(\tau_{\infty}),\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}H_{n\cdot}(\tau_{\infty}),\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{*}_{\!n\cdot}(\tau_{\infty}))

are tight on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\!\times\!\mathbf{D}_{1}\!\times\!(\mathbf{C}^{0}_{1})^{2}
. By Lemma
3.20

(i)
, it implies that conditionally given
\mathcal{G}
the laws of the processes

Z^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!:=\!\big(\tfrac{1}{n}\mathfrak{g}_{n},\tfrac{1}{n}\mathfrak{d}_{n},\big(\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{\lfloor ns\rfloor}(\tau_{\infty}),\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}H_{ns}(\tau_{\infty}),\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{*}_{\!ns}(\tau_{\infty}))_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\big)
are tight on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\!\times\mathbf{D}_{3}
.

We next denote by

\omega\!\in\!\Omega\mapsto Q_{n}(\omega,\cdot)\!\in\!\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\!\times\!\mathbf{D}_{3})
a regular version of the law of
Z^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}
conditionally given
\mathcal{G}
. We denote by
P_{n}
the law of
\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{\lfloor n\cdot\rfloor}(\tau_{\infty})
under
{\bf P}
, we denote by
P
the law
X
under
{\bf P}
and we denote by
Q_{\infty}
the law of
\big(\mathbf{g}_{1},\mathbf{d}_{1},(X_{s},H_{s},\widehat{W}_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\big)
under
{\bf P}
.
We observe that for
{\bf P}
-almost all
\omega
,
Q^{1}_{n}(\omega,\cdot)\!=\!P_{n}(\cdot)
. This, combined with (
3.45
), entails that there exists
\Omega_{0}\!\in\!\mathcal{G}
such that
{\bf P}(\Omega_{0})\!=\!1
, such that for all

\omega\!\in\!\Omega_{0}
, it holds that the
(Q_{n}(\omega,\cdot))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are tight on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\!\times\!\mathbf{D}_{3}
, that

Q_{n}^{1}(\omega,\cdot)\!=\!P_{n}(\cdot)
, and that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\!\times\!\mathbf{D}_{3}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!e^{i\langle\xi,\mathtt{u}\rangle+i\langle\lambda_{1},\mathtt{z}(s_{1})\rangle+\ldots+i\langle\lambda_{p},\mathtt{z}(s_{p})\rangle}Q_{n}(\omega,d\mathtt{u}d\mathtt{z})\!=\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\!\times\!\mathbf{D}_{3}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!e^{i\langle\xi,\mathtt{u}\rangle+i\langle\lambda_{1},\mathtt{z}(s_{1})\rangle+\ldots+i\langle\lambda_{p},\mathtt{z}(s_{p})\rangle}Q_{\infty}(d\mathtt{u}d\mathtt{z}).

(3.46)

for all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, all
\xi\!\in\!\mathbb{Q}^{2}
, all
s_{1},\ldots,s_{p}\!\in\!\mathbb{Q}\cap\mathbb{R}_{+}
and all
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p}\!\in\!\mathbb{Q}^{3}
.

We then fix
\omega\!\in\!\Omega_{0}
and we denote by
Q(\omega,\cdot)\!\in\!\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\!\times\!\mathbf{D}_{3})

a weak limit-point of the
Q_{n}(\omega,\cdot)
(there is such one since the
Q_{n}(\omega,\cdot)

are tight on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\!\times\!\mathbf{D}_{3}
). Namely, there exists an increasing sequence of integers

(n_{k}(\omega))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\lim_{k\to\infty}Q_{n_{k}(\omega)}(\omega,\cdot)\!=\!Q(\omega,\cdot)
.
By Lemma
3.20

(iv)
,
Q^{1}_{n_{k}(\omega)}(\omega,\cdot)\!=\!P_{n_{k}(\omega)}\to Q^{1}(\omega,\cdot)\!=\!P
. Since
X
has no fixed time discontinuity, it implies for all
s_{1},\ldots,s_{p}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
that
Q(\omega,d\mathtt{u}d\mathtt{z})
-a.s.
\mathtt{z}(s_{j}-)\!=\!\mathtt{z}(s_{j})
,
1\!\leq\!j\!\leq\!p
. Then by Lemma
3.20

(ii)
, by weak-continuity of the Fourier transform of probability measures and by (
3.46
), we get

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\!\times\!\mathbf{D}_{3}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!e^{i\langle\xi,\mathtt{u}\rangle+i\langle\lambda_{1},\mathtt{z}(s_{1})\rangle+\ldots+i\langle\lambda_{p},\mathtt{z}(s_{p})\rangle}Q(\omega,d\mathtt{u}d\mathtt{z})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\!\times\!\mathbf{D}_{3}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!e^{i\langle\xi,\mathtt{u}\rangle+i\langle\lambda_{1},\mathtt{z}(s_{1})\rangle+\ldots+i\langle\lambda_{p},\mathtt{z}(s_{p})\rangle}Q_{\infty}(d\mathtt{u}d\mathtt{z})

for all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, all
\xi\!\in\!\mathbb{Q}^{2}
, all
s_{1},\ldots,s_{p}\!\in\!\mathbb{Q}\cap\mathbb{R}_{+}
and all
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p}\!\in\!\mathbb{Q}^{3}
. Thus, it also holds for all
\xi\!\in\!\mathbb{R}^{2}
, all
s_{1},\ldots,s_{p}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
and all
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}^{3}
, by the continuity of the paths and dominated convergence. Injectivity of the Fourier transform of probability measures then entails that

Q(\omega,\cdot)
and
Q_{\infty}(\cdot)
have the same finite-dimensional marginals, which implies that

Q(\omega,\cdot)\!=\!Q_{\infty}(\cdot)
. This proves that for all
\omega\!\in\!\Omega_{0}
,
Q_{\infty}(\cdot)
is the only limit point of the
Q_{n}(\omega,\cdot)
: namely,
Q_{n}(\omega,\cdot)\!\to\!Q_{\infty}(\cdot)
, which easily entails (
3.44
) by Lemma
3.20

(iii)
and the fact that the topology induced by Skorokhod topology on the set
\mathbf{C}^{0}_{1}
is that of uniform convergence on every compact interval. This completes the proof of Proposition
3.10
.
\hfill\blacksquare

Before proceeding to the proof of Proposition
3.19
, we need to introduce some notation and to prove auxiliary results on finite-dimensional marginals of height processes and height snakes.

Contour processes of real trees with a finite number of leaves.
Let us first recall from Example
2.36
the definition of the real tree
(T_{h},d_{h},\mathtt{r}_{h})
coded by a continuous function

h:[0,\zeta]\!\to\!\mathbb{R}
and recall that
p_{h}\!:\![0,\zeta]\!\to\!T_{h}
stands for the canonical projection.
We discuss here how to encode
the subtree
T^{\prime}\!:=\!\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq p}[\![\mathtt{r}_{h},p_{h}(s_{j})]\!]
that is spanned by the points
corresponding to the times
0\!\leq\!s_{1}\!<\!\ldots\!<\!s_{p}\!<\!\zeta
(here, recall from Definition
2.33
that for all
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\!\in\!T_{h}
,
[\![\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}]\!]
stands for the geodesic path joining
\sigma
to
\sigma^{\prime}
).
The real tree
T^{\prime}
can be viewed as a discrete rooted ordered
tree (its
skeleton
) equipped with ‘heights’ and if
h(s_{j})\wedge h(s_{j+1})\!>\!\min_{[s_{i},s_{j+1}]}h
,
T^{\prime}
has exactly
p
leaves (i.e., points
\sigma\!\in\!T^{\prime}\backslash\{\mathtt{r}_{h}\}
such that

T^{\prime}\backslash\{\sigma\}
is connected).
To encode
T^{\prime}
, we introduce its contour function which represents
the distance from the root of a particle visiting
T^{\prime}
at unit speed and respecting the contour order of its skeleton. The contour function of
T^{\prime}
belongs to the set
\mathscr{C}_{p}
of functions
\mathcal{C}\!\in\!\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that

(a)

\mathcal{C}_{0}\!=\!0
and
\zeta_{\mathcal{C}}\!=\!\sup\{s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}:\mathcal{C}_{s}\!>\!0\}\!<\!\infty
,

(b)

\mathcal{C}
is a broken line whose slopes are either equal to
1
or equal to
-1
, that has
p
local maxima.

Namely, there are
r_{0}\!=\!\rho_{0}\!=\!0\!<\!r_{1}\!<\!\rho_{1}\!<\!\ldots\!<\!r_{p-1}\!<\!\rho_{p-1}\!<\!r_{p}\!<\!\rho_{p}=\zeta_{\mathcal{C}}
such that for all
s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\mathcal{C}_{s}=\int_{0}^{s}\!\!\!{\bf 1}_{J_{+}}(r)\,dr\,-\!\!\int_{0}^{s}\!\!\!{\bf 1}_{J_{-}}(r)\,dr\quad\textrm{where}\;J_{+}\!=\!\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq p}[\rho_{j-1},r_{j}]\;\textrm{and}\;J_{-}\!=\![0,\zeta_{\mathcal{C}}]\backslash J_{+}.

(3.47)

For all
j\!\in\![\![1,p]\!]
, note that
r_{j}
(resp.
\rho_{j-1}
) is the time when the function
\mathcal{C}
reaches its
j
-th
local maximum (resp. its
j
-th local minimum). Then, observe that
\mathcal{C}_{\rho_{j-1}}\!=\!\min_{[r_{j-1},r_{j}]}\mathcal{C}
. It shall be convenient to define
\mathscr{C}_{0}
as the set reducing to the null function.

Let
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
. Note that there is a one-to-one correspondence
F_{p}
between the open subset
\mathcal{D}_{p}\!=\!\big\{(x_{j})_{1\leq j\leq 2p-1}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2p-1}:x_{2j-1}\!\wedge x_{2j+1}\!>\!x_{2j},\,1\!\leq\!j\!\leq\!p\!-\!1\big\}
of
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2p-1}
and
\mathscr{C}_{p}
, that is defined as follows:
\mathcal{C}\!=\!F_{p}((x_{j})_{1\leq j\leq 2p-1})
if

\forall j\!\in\![\![1,p]\!],\quad x_{2j-1}=\mathcal{C}_{r_{j}}\quad\textrm{ and }\quad x_{2j}\!=\!\min_{[r_{j},r_{j+1}]}\mathcal{C}\!=\!\mathcal{C}_{\rho_{j}}\>\textrm{ when $j<p$}.

(3.48)

We easily check that
F_{p}
and its inverse
F_{p}^{-1}
are continuous when
\mathscr{C}_{p}
is
equipped with the topology of the uniform convergence on every compact interval.

Decompositions at the first branching time.
Let
\mathcal{C}\!\in\!\mathscr{C}_{p}
with
p\!\geq\!2
. Let
\zeta_{\mathcal{C}}
, the
r_{j}
and the
\rho_{j}
be as in (
3.47
). We set

m(\mathcal{C})\!=\!\min_{[r_{1},r_{p}]}\mathcal{C}\;\,\,\textrm{and}\;\,\,q(\mathcal{C})\!=\!-2+\#\{s\!\in\![0,\zeta_{\mathcal{C}}]:\mathcal{C}_{s}=m(\mathcal{C})\},

(3.49)

and we list the elements of
\{s\!\in\![0,\zeta_{\mathcal{C}}]:\mathcal{C}_{s}=m(\mathcal{C})\}
in increasing order as
\gamma_{0}\!<\!\gamma_{1}\!<\!\ldots\!<\!\gamma_{q(\mathcal{C})+1}
. Note that
\gamma_{0}\!<\!r_{1}\!<\!\gamma_{1}\!\leq\!\gamma_{q(\mathcal{C})}\!<\!r_{p}\!<\!\gamma_{q(\mathcal{C})+1}
, and that the intervals
(\gamma_{k-1},\gamma_{k})
,
1\!\leq\!k\!\leq\!q(\mathcal{C})\!+\!1
, are the connected components of
\{s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}:\mathcal{C}_{s}\!>\!m(\mathcal{C})\}
. Since
p\!\geq\!2
, it holds that
q(\mathcal{C})\geq 1
. Then, for all
k\!\in\![\![1,q(\mathcal{C})]\!]
and all
s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we also set

\mathcal{C}_{s}[k]^{{}^{{}_{-}}}\!\!=\!\mathcal{C}_{(\gamma_{0}+s)\wedge\gamma_{k}}\!\!-m(\mathcal{C})\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathcal{C}_{s}[k]^{{}^{{}_{+}}}\!\!=\!\mathcal{C}_{(\gamma_{k}+s)\wedge\gamma_{q(\mathcal{C})+1}}\!\!-m(\mathcal{C}).

(3.50)

Now, recall
F_{p}
from (
3.48
). If we write
(x_{j})_{1\leq j<2p}\!:=\!F_{p}^{-1}(\mathcal{C})
, then we observe that

\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{-}}}\!\!=\!F_{p_{k}(\mathcal{C})}\big((x_{j}\!-\!m(\mathcal{C}))_{1\leq j<2p_{k}(\mathcal{C})}\big)\quad\!\textrm{and}\!\quad\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{+}}}\!\!=\!F_{p-p_{k}(\mathcal{C})}\big((x_{2p_{k}(\mathcal{C})+j}\!-\!m(\mathcal{C}))_{1\leq j<2p-2p_{k}(\mathcal{C})}\big),

(3.51)

where
p_{k}(\mathcal{C})=\#\{j\!\in\![\![1,p]\!]:r_{j}\!\leq\!\gamma_{k}\}
. Here, note that
1\!\leq\!p_{k}(\mathcal{C})\!<\!p
and
\rho_{p_{k}(\mathcal{C})}\!=\!\gamma_{k}
.

In the special case where
p\!=\!1
, we set
m(\mathcal{C})\!=\!\mathcal{C}_{r_{1}}\!=\!\max\mathcal{C}
,
q(\mathcal{C})\!=\!1
and
\mathcal{C}[1]^{{}^{{}_{-}}}\!\!=\!\mathcal{C}[1]^{{}^{{}_{+}}}\!
is the null function. For any
k\!\in\![\![1,q(\mathcal{C})]\!]
, we call the pair of functions
(\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{-}}}\!,\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{+}}}\!)
the
k
-th decomposition of
\mathcal{C}
at the first branching point
.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!\in\!\mathscr{C}_{p}
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, be such that
\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!\to\!\mathcal{C}
uniformly on every compact interval.
Then,
m(\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}})\!\!\to\!m(\mathcal{C})
, and there exists a sequence of integers
k_{n}\!\in\![\![1,q(\mathcal{C})]\!]
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, such that:

(a)

\mathcal{C}[k_{n}]^{{}^{{}_{-}}}\!,\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}[1]^{{}^{{}_{-}}}\!\!\in\!\mathscr{C}_{p_{k_{n}}(\mathcal{C})}
and
\mathcal{C}[k_{n}]^{{}^{{}_{+}}}\!,\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}[1]^{{}^{{}_{+}}}\!\!\in\!\mathscr{C}_{p-p_{k_{n}}(\mathcal{C})}
when
n
is large enough,

(b)

\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\![1]^{{}^{{}_{-}}}\!\!-\mathcal{C}[k_{n}]^{{}^{{}_{-}}}\!\!\to\!0
and
\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\![1]^{{}^{{}_{+}}}\!\!-\mathcal{C}[k_{n}]^{{}^{{}_{+}}}\!\!\to\!0
uniformly on every compact interval.

Proof.
Note that
F^{-1}_{p}\!(\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}})\!=:\!(x^{{}_{(n)}}_{j})_{1\leq j\leq 2p-1}\!\to\!(x_{j})_{1\leq j\leq 2p-1}\!:=\!F_{p}^{-1}(\mathcal{C})
. Thus,

m(\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}})\!=\!\min_{1\leq j\leq 2p-1}x^{{}_{(n)}}_{j}\to\min_{1\leq j\leq 2p-1}x_{j}\!=\!m(\mathcal{C})
. Let us set
p^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!=\!p_{1}(\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}})
to lighten the notation.
We construct the sequence
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
as follows: if
x_{2p^{(n)}}\!\neq\!m(\mathcal{C})
, then
we set

k_{n}=1
; if
x_{2p^{(n)}}\!=\!m(\mathcal{C})
then there is
k\!\in\![\![1,q(\mathcal{C})]\!]
such that

p^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!=\!p_{k}(\mathcal{C})
, and we set
k_{n}=k
. By definition, we have

x^{{}_{(n)}}_{2p^{(n)}}\!=\!m(\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}})
, so it follows that
|x_{2p^{(n)}}\!-\!m(\mathcal{C})|

converges to
0
. But since the latter sequence can only take on a finite number of values, it is
eventually stationary at
0
. When
n
is large enough, it thus holds
p_{1}(\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}})\!=\!p_{k_{n}}(\mathcal{C})
.
We complete the proof by (
3.51
) and by continuity of
F_{\!p_{k}(\mathcal{C})}
.
\hfill\blacksquare

Finite-dimensional marginals of height snakes.
We next use the decompositions at the first branching point to compute the finite-dimensional marginals of Brownian snakes and BRWs. More precisely, we fix
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\mathcal{C}\!\in\!\mathscr{C}_{p}
, and we denote by
(\mathtt{W}_{s}(\mathcal{C}))_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
the one-dimensional Brownian snake starting at
0
whose lifetime process is
\mathcal{C}
(see Definition
2.39
).
Then we introduce the following notation for all
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}
:

\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{C}\,;\,\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p})={\bf E}\Big[\prod_{1\leq j\leq p}e^{i\lambda_{j}\widehat{\mathtt{W}}_{r_{j}}(\mathcal{C})}\Big]

(3.52)

where
r_{j}
stands for the time when
\mathcal{C}
reaches its
j
-th local maximum.
As an easy consequence of the snake property, for all
k\!\in\![\![1,q(\mathcal{C})]\!]
with
q(\mathcal{C})
as in (
3.49
), we get

\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{C}\,;\,\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p})=e^{-\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{2}}m(\mathcal{C})\overline{\lambda}^{2}}\mathscr{L}\big(\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{-}}};\,(\lambda_{j})_{1\leq j\leq p_{k}(\mathcal{C})}\big)\,\mathscr{L}\big(\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{+}}};\,(\lambda_{j})_{p_{k}(\mathcal{C})+1\leq j\leq p}\big)\;.

(3.53)

where
(\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{-}}}\!,\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{+}}}\!)
is the
k
-th decomposition of
\mathcal{C}
at its first branching time as in (
3.50
) and (
3.51
),
where
m(\mathcal{C})\!=\!\min_{[r_{1},r_{p}]}\mathcal{C}
,
and where
\overline{\lambda}\!=\!\lambda_{1}+\ldots+\lambda_{p}
.

Let us introduce similar notation for
\mathbf{T}
-valued BRWs, where
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
stands here for an invariant GW tree whose offspring distribution
\nu
is supercritical. We denote by
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
its mean and we assume that there is
\mathtt{b}\!\in\!(2,\infty)
such that
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k^{\mathtt{b}}\nu(k)\!<\!\infty
.
To that end, we suppose that there exists
a\!\in\!(0,\infty)
and a discrete tree
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
such that

C_{s}(t)\!=\!a\,\mathcal{C}_{s/a},\quad s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}\;

(3.54)

where
C_{\cdot}(t)
stands for the contour function of the rooted ordered tree
t
as in Definition
2.4
(
b
).

We then fix
x\!\in\!\mathbf{T}
and we denote by
(Y_{u})_{u\in t}
a
t
-indexed and
\mathbf{T}
-valued
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased BRW starting at
Y_{\varnothing}
. Namely, conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},x)
, we
{\bf P}
-a.s. get

{\bf P}\big(\forall u\!\in\!t,Y_{u}\!=\!y_{u}|(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})\big)\!=\!{\bf 1}_{\{y_{\varnothing}=x\}}\prod_{u\in t\backslash\{\varnothing\}}p_{\mathbf{T},\mathtt{m}_{\nu}}(y_{\overleftarrow{u}},y_{u})
for all
y_{u}\!\in\!\mathbf{T}
,
u\!\in\!t
(recall from (
2.23
) the definition of the transition probabilities
p_{\mathbf{T},\mathtt{m}_{\nu}}(\cdot,\cdot)
of the
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RW on
\mathbf{T}
).
Here we recall from (
3.3
) that
(S_{x})_{x\in\mathbf{T}}
stand for the harmonic coordinates of
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
. Then, we introduce the following notation for all
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}
:

\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathbf{T},x}(a\,\mathcal{C}_{\frac{\cdot}{{}^{a}}}\,;\,\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p})={\bf E}\Big[\prod_{1\leq j\leq p}e^{i\lambda_{j}\big(S_{Y_{v(j)}}-S_{Y_{\varnothing}}\big)}\Big|(\mathbf{T},x)\Big]

(3.55)

where the vertex
v(j)\!\in\!t

is the
j
-th leaf of
t
in increasing contour order. As a consequence of the definition of BRWs, for all
k\!\in\![\![1,q(\mathcal{C})]\!]
with
q(\mathcal{C})
as in (
3.49
), we

{\bf P}
-a.s. get

\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathbf{T},x}(a\,\mathcal{C}_{\frac{\cdot}{{}^{a}}}\,;\,\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p})=E_{\mathbf{T},x}\Big[\,e^{i\overline{\lambda}\big(S_{X_{am(\mathcal{C})}}-S_{X_{0}}\big)}\\
\times\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathbf{T},X_{am(\mathcal{C})}}\big(a\,\mathcal{C}_{\frac{\cdot}{{}^{a}}}[k]^{{}^{{}_{-}}};\,(\lambda_{j})_{1\leq j\leq p_{k}(\mathcal{C})}\big)\,\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathbf{T},X_{am(\mathcal{C})}}\big(a\,\mathcal{C}_{\frac{\cdot}{{}^{a}}}[k]^{{}^{{}_{+}}};\,(\lambda_{j})_{p_{k}(\mathcal{C})+1\leq j\leq p}\big)\,\Big]\;,

(3.56)

where
\overline{X}_{n}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},X_{n})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, stands for a
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RW starting at
X_{0}\!=\!x
(and recall that
P_{\mathbf{T},x}
is the conditional law of
(\overline{X}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
given
(\mathbf{T},x)
).
Here we have set
\overline{\lambda}\!=\!\lambda_{1}+\ldots+\lambda_{p}
and we recall that
(\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{-}}}\!,\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{+}}}\!)
is the
k
-th decomposition of
\mathcal{C}
at its first branching time:
m(\mathcal{C})\!=\!\min_{[r_{1},r_{p}]}\mathcal{C}
.
Note that
am(\mathcal{C})
is the height of the
\leq_{\mathtt{lex}}
-smallest vertex
u\!\in\!t
such that
k_{u}(t)\!\neq\!1
(which is also the first time that this vertex is visited in increasing contour order).

One of the main steps of the proof of Proposition
3.19
is the following lemma, which relies on decompositions at the first branching point and on
the specific CLT in Proposition
3.7
.

Lemma 3.22
.

We keep the above assumptions and notation. We fix
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
. Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!\in\!\mathscr{C}_{p}
and
r_{n}\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
. Here,
\overline{X}_{n}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},X_{n})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, stands for a
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RW starting at
X_{0}\!=\!\mathtt{o}
. We assume the following.

(a)

\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!\!\to\mathcal{C}
uniformly on every compact interval.

(b)

For all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, there exist
a_{n}\!\in\!(0,\infty)
and
t_{n}\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
such that

C_{s}(t_{n})\!=\!a_{n}\,\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{s/a_{n}}
,
s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

(c)

There exists
r\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
r_{n}/a_{n}\!\to\!r
.

Then for all
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}
, it holds
{\bf P}
-a.s. that

\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathbf{T},X_{r_{n}}}(a_{n}\,\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{\frac{\cdot}{{}^{a_{n}}}}\,;\,\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\lambda_{1},\ldots,\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\lambda_{p})\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{C}\,;\,\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p}),

(3.57)

where we recall from (
3.6
) the definition of
\sigma_{\nu}
.

Proof.
We argue recursively on
p
. If
p\!=\!1
, then the trees
t_{n}
are lines and (
3.57
) is a direct consequence of Proposition
3.7
. We then assume that
p\!\geq\!2
and that the lemma holds true for all
p^{\prime}\!<\!p
. Lemma
3.21
yields that
m(\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}})\!\to\!m(\mathcal{C})
and gives a specific sequence
k_{n}\!\in\![\![1,q(\mathcal{C})]\!],n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
. We fix
k\!\in\![\![1,q(\mathcal{C})]\!]
which is a subsequential limit of
(k_{n})
, and denote by
(\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\![1]^{{}^{{}_{-}}}\!,\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\![1]^{{}^{{}_{+}}}\!)
and
(\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{-}}}\!,\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{+}}}\!)
resp. the first and the
k
-th decomposition at the first branching point of resp.
\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!
,
\mathcal{C}
.

Observe that

(r_{n}+a_{n}m(\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}))/a_{n}\!\to\!r+m(\mathcal{C})
. Also recall from Lemma
3.21
that along the subsequence of integers
n
such that
k_{n}=k
,
\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\![1]^{{}^{{}_{\pm}}}\!\!\to\!\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{\pm}}}\!

uniformly on every compact interval. For all such
n
large enough, the common number of local maxima of
\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\![1]^{{}^{{}_{\pm}}}\!
and
\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{\pm}}}\!
is smaller than
p
, so our recurrence assumption applies and we
{\bf P}
-a.s. get

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\,\mathbf{T},X_{\!r_{n}+a_{n}m(\mathcal{C}^{(n)})}}\!\Big(a_{n}\,\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{\frac{\cdot}{{}^{a_{n}}}}[1]^{{}^{{}_{-}}};\Big(\tfrac{\lambda_{j}}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\Big)_{\!1\leq j\leq p_{1}(\mathcal{C}^{(n)})}\Big)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\
k_{n}=k\end{subarray}]{}\mathscr{L}\big(\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{-}}};\,(\lambda_{j})_{1\leq j\leq p_{k}(\mathcal{C})}\big),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\,\mathbf{T},X_{\!r_{n}+a_{n}m(\mathcal{C}^{(n)})}}\!\Big(a_{n}\,\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{\frac{\cdot}{{}^{a_{n}}}}[1]^{{}^{{}_{+}}};\Big(\tfrac{\lambda_{j}}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\Big)_{\!p_{1}(\mathcal{C}^{(n)})+1\leq j\leq p}\Big)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\
k_{n}=k\end{subarray}]{}\mathscr{L}\big(\mathcal{C}[k]^{{}^{{}_{+}}};\,(\lambda_{j})_{p_{k}(\mathcal{C})+1\leq j\leq p}\big).

To simplify, we denote by
L_{n}
(resp.
L[k]
) the product of the left-hand (resp. right-hand) sides of the two previous convergences. Let us also denote by
\mathscr{F}_{n}
the
\sigma
-field generated by
(\overline{X}_{m})_{0\leq m\leq n}
. Then, we
{\bf P}
-a.s. get
\lim_{n\to\infty,k_{n}=k}{\bf E}\big[|L[k]\!-\!L_{n}|\,\big|\,\mathscr{F}_{r_{n}}\big]\!=\!0
by the technical Lemma
C.1
in Appendix.

Note that
L[k]
is not random. Then, combining the decomposition (
3.56
) with the Markov property for
\overline{X}
at time
r_{n}
implies
{\bf P}
-a.s. that

\Big|\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\,\mathbf{T},X_{r_{n}}}(a_{n}\,\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{\frac{\cdot}{{}^{a_{n}}}}\,;\,\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\lambda_{1},\ldots,\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\lambda_{p})-L[k]\,E_{\mathbf{T},X_{r_{n}}}\!\Big[\exp\Big(i\tfrac{\overline{\lambda}}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\big(S_{X^{\prime}_{a_{n}m(\mathcal{C}^{(n)})}}\!\!-\!S_{X^{\prime}_{0}}\big)\Big)\Big]\Big|

is smaller or equal to
{\bf E}\big[|L_{n}\!-\!L[k]|\,\big|\,\mathscr{F}_{r_{n}}\big]
, where
\overline{X}^{\prime}_{n}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},X_{n}^{\prime})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, stands here for an auxiliary
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RW. We then apply Proposition
3.7
to get
{\bf P}
-a.s.

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{T},X_{r_{n}}}\!\Big[\exp\Big(i\tfrac{\overline{\lambda}}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\big(S_{X^{\prime}_{a_{n}m(\mathcal{C}^{(n)})}}\!\!-\!S_{X^{\prime}_{0}}\big)\Big)\Big]\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!{\bf E}\Big[\exp\Big(i\tfrac{\overline{\lambda}}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\big(S_{X_{r_{n}+a_{n}m(\mathcal{C}^{(n)})}}\!\!-\!S_{X_{r_{n}}}\Big)\Big|\mathscr{F}_{r_{n}}\Big]

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}

\displaystyle\!\!\exp\big(\!-\!\tfrac{1}{2}m(\mathcal{C})\overline{\lambda}^{2}\big).

Therefore, we get that
{\bf P}
-a.s.

\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\,\mathbf{T},X_{r_{n}}}(a_{n}\,\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{\frac{\cdot}{{}^{a_{n}}}}\,;\,\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\lambda_{1},\ldots,\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\lambda_{p})\;\xrightarrow[n\to\infty,\,k_{n}=k]{}\;e^{-\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{2}}m(\mathcal{C})\overline{\lambda}^{2}}L[k]=\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{C}\,;\,\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p})

thanks to the decomposition (
3.53
). Since
\{n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}:k_{n}=k\}
,
1\!\leq\!k\!\leq\!q(\mathcal{C})
, is a finite partition of
\mathbb{N}
, this completes the proof of (
3.57
), and thus of the lemma.
\hfill\blacksquare

Proof of Proposition
3.19
.
To that end, we need to relate the previous results to the finite-dimensional
marginals of the endpoint process of the height snake in harmonic coordinates and to the finite-dimensional marginals of the endpoint process of one-dimensional Brownian snake
\widehat{W}^{\prime}
whose lifetime process is
H^{\prime}
as in (
3.41
).

Recall from (
3.48
) the definition of
F_{p}
for all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
. For all
h\!\in\!\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
and for all
s_{1}\!>\!0
such that
h(s_{1})\!>\!0
, we set
\mathcal{C}(h,s_{1})\!=\!F_{1}(h(s_{1}))
. More generally, for all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
with
p\!\geq\!2
and for all real numbers
0\!=\!s_{0}\!<\!s_{1}\!<\!\ldots\!<\!s_{p}
such that the following condition

\forall j\!\in\![\![1,p\!-\!1]\!],\quad h(s_{j})\wedge h(s_{j+1})\!>\!\min_{[s_{j},s_{j+1}]}h

(3.58)

is satisfied, we set

F_{p}\Big(h(s_{1}),\!\!\min_{\;[s_{1},s_{2}]}\!\!h,h(s_{2}),\ldots,h(s_{p-1}),\!\!\!\!\!\min_{\;\;\,[s_{p-1},s_{p}]}\!\!\!\!\!h\,,h(s_{p})\Big)=:\mathcal{C}(h;s_{1},\ldots,s_{p})\,\in\mathscr{C}_{p}.

(3.59)

As explained at the beginning of the section,
\mathcal{C}(h;s_{1},\ldots,s_{p})
is the contour process of the subtree
\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq p}[\![\mathtt{r}_{h},p_{h}(s_{j})]\!]
in
(T_{h},d_{h},\mathtt{r}_{h})
.

We know from Lemma
2.27
that
(H^{\prime};s_{1},\ldots,s_{p})

{\bf P}
-a.s. satisfies (
3.58
). As an easy consequence of Definition
2.39
of finite-dimensional marginals of Brownian snakes, we see that for all
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}
,
{\bf P}
-a.s.

{\bf E}\Big[\prod_{0\leq j\leq p}e^{i\lambda_{j}\widehat{W}^{\prime}_{s_{j}}}\Big|(H^{\prime}_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\Big]=\mathscr{L}\big(\mathcal{C}(H^{\prime};s_{1},\ldots,s_{p})\,;\,\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p})

(3.60)

where we recall from (
3.52
) the definition of the right member.

Similarly, in the discrete setting: we recall that the GW(
\mu
)-forests trees
\tau_{n,\infty}
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, are
such that the almost sure limit (
3.41
) holds true and recall from (
3.42
)
the definition of the joint law of
(\tau_{n,\infty},\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{*}(\tau_{n,\infty}))
. The almost sure
convergence (
3.41
)
{\bf P}
-a.s. implies that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}H_{\lfloor ns_{j}\rfloor}(\tau_{n,\infty})\!=\!H^{\prime}_{s_{j}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\min_{{}_{\hskip 14.72241pt[\lfloor ns_{j}\rfloor,\lfloor ns_{j+1}\rfloor]}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!H(\tau_{n,\infty})=\min_{{}_{[s_{j},s_{j+1}]}}H^{\prime}.

(3.61)

Combined with Lemma
2.27
, this
{\bf P}
-a.s. entails for all sufficiently large
n
, that (
3.58
) is satisfied by
(a_{n}^{-1}H(\tau_{n,\infty});\lfloor ns_{1}\rfloor,\ldots,\lfloor ns_{p}\rfloor)
. This allows us to define

\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}=\mathcal{C}\big(\frac{{}_{{}_{1}}}{{}^{{}^{a_{n}}}}H(\tau_{n,\infty});\lfloor ns_{1}\rfloor,\ldots,\lfloor ns_{p}\rfloor\big).

(3.62)

As a consequence of the definition of the height snake
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{*}(\tau_{n,\infty})
, we see that

\displaystyle{\bf E}\Big[\prod_{0\leq j\leq p}\exp\Big(i\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\lambda_{j}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{*}_{\!\lfloor ns_{j}\rfloor}(\tau_{n,\infty})\Big)\,\Big|\,(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o}),\tau_{n,\infty}\Big]

(3.63)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\,\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o}}\Big(a_{n}\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{\frac{\cdot}{{}^{a_{n}}}}\,;\,\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\lambda_{1},\ldots,\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\lambda_{p}\Big).

Moreover, since the function
F_{p}
is continuous, we get from (
3.61
) that
\mathcal{C}^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!\!\!\to\!\mathcal{C}(H^{\prime};s_{1},\ldots,s_{p})
uniformly on every compact interval.
Then Proposition
3.19
follows by applying Lemma
3.22
to (
3.60
) and (
3.63
).
\hfill\blacksquare

3.5
Proof of Proposition
3.14

We keep the notation and the general assumptions discussed in Section
3.2
.
In particular, we recall that
\tau_{\infty}
is a GW(
\mu
)-forest corresponding to the sequence
(\tau^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
of i.i.d. GW(
\mu
)-trees (Definition
2.6

(\textbf{b})
): namely

\theta_{(k)}\tau_{\infty}\!=\!\tau^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}}
, for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
.
We recall that conditionally given the environment
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
and the genealogy
\tau_{\infty}
, for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
,

\bm{\Theta}_{k}\!=\!\big(\tau^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\,}}\!\!\!,\,\varnothing;\overline{Y}^{{}_{(k)}}_{u}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},Y^{{}_{(k)}}_{u}),u\!\in\!\tau^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}}\big)
are independent
\mathbf{T}
-valued
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased BRWs such that
Y^{{}_{(k)}}_{\varnothing}\!=\!\mathtt{o}
. It is
convenient to define a single
\tau_{\infty}
-indexed BRW
(Y_{u})_{u\in\tau_{\infty}}
by setting
Y_{\varnothing}\!=\!\mathtt{o}
and

\forall k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*},\;\forall u\!\in\!\tau^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!,\quad Y_{(k)\ast u}:=Y^{{}_{(k)}}_{u}\;.

(3.64)

We recall that
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\}}
are the vertices of
\tau_{\infty}
in lexicographical order: namely,
u_{-1}\!=\!\varnothing
,
u_{0}\!=\!(1)
, etc.
This section is devoted to the proof of the following result which implies Proposition
3.14
. Before proving Proposition
3.23
, we show that it implies Proposition
3.14
.

Proposition 3.23
.

We keep the assumptions and the notation of Proposition
3.14
. Then, there are constants

c_{0},c^{\prime}_{0},\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{1}\!\in\!(0,\infty)
which only depend on
\mu
,
\nu
and
\mathtt{b}
, such that for all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
,

{\bf P}\Big(\!\!\!\!\max_{\hskip 8.19447ptm\in[\![0,n]\!]}\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}S_{Y_{u_{m}}}\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|\,>\,c^{\prime}_{0}n^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\sqrt{a_{n}}\,\Big)\leq c_{0}n^{-\varepsilon_{0}}.

(3.65)

Proof.
The proof of Proposition
3.23
is given below, right after the proof of Proposition
3.23
.
\hfill\blacksquare

Proof of Proposition
3.14
.
We admit Proposition
3.23
and we prove that
it implies Proposition
3.14
. By (
3.65
), we first see that

{\bf E}\Big[\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}{\bf P}\Big(\!\!\!\!\!\max_{\hskip 8.19447ptm\in[\![0,2^{j}]\!]}\!\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}S_{Y_{u_{m}}}\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|>c^{\prime}_{0}2^{-\varepsilon_{1}j}\sqrt{a_{2^{j}}}\,\,\Big|\,(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})\Big)\;\Big]<\infty

which entails
{\bf P}
-a.s. that

\lim_{j\to\infty}{\bf P}\big(\max_{m\in[\![0,2^{j}]\!]}\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}S_{Y_{u_{m}}}\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|\!>\!c^{\prime}_{0}2^{-\varepsilon_{1}j}\sqrt{a_{2^{j}}}\big|(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})\big)\!=\!0
.

We next fix
s_{0}\!\in\!(1,\infty)
.
Since
n^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\sqrt{a_{n}}
is a regularly varying sequence, there is
c\!\in\!(0,\infty)
(which only depends on
\mu
,
\nu
and
s_{0}
) such that
cn^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\sqrt{a_{n}}\!\geq\!c^{\prime}_{0}2^{-\varepsilon_{1}j}\sqrt{a_{2^{j}}}
for all integers
n
such that

2^{j-1}\!\leq\!\lceil ns_{0}\rceil\leq 2^{j}
. Since

\displaystyle{\bf P}\big(\!\!\!\!\!\max_{\hskip 8.19447ptm\in[\![0,\lceil ns_{0}\rceil]\!]}\!\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}S_{Y_{u_{m}}}\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!cn^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\sqrt{a_{n}}\,\big|\,(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\bf P}\big(\!\!\!\!\!\max_{\hskip 8.19447ptm\in[\![0,2^{j}]\!]}\!\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}S_{Y_{u_{m}}}\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|>c^{\prime}_{0}2^{-\varepsilon_{1}j}\sqrt{a_{2^{j}}}\,\big|\,(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})\big),

we
{\bf P}
-a.s. get

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\bf P}\big(\max_{m\in[\![0,\lceil ns_{0}\rceil]\!]}\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}S_{Y_{u_{m}}}\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|\!>\!cn^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\sqrt{a_{n}}\big|(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})\big)\!=\!0
. Thanks to the specific interpolation
defining the contour and height snakes, we next get

\max_{\hskip 8.19447pts\in[0,s_{0}]}\!\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{*}_{ns}(\tau_{\infty})\!-\!\widehat{W}^{*}_{ns}(\tau_{\infty})\big|\leq\!\!\!\!\!\max_{\hskip 8.19447ptm\in[\![0,\lceil ns_{0}\rceil]\!]}\!\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}S_{Y_{u_{m}}}\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|

which completes the proof of Proposition
3.14
.
\hfill\blacksquare

Proof of Proposition
3.23
.
To simplify notation, we set

\Gamma_{\!n}\!=\!\max_{s\in[0,n]}H_{s}(\tau_{\infty})\!=\!\max_{m\in[\![0,n]\!]}(|u_{m}|\!-\!1)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\!n}=\!\max_{m\in[\![0,n]\!]}\big|\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|\;.

We first prove for all
p,q\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
that

{\bf P}\big(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\!n}\geq q\big)\leq{\bf P}\big(\Gamma_{\!n}\geq p\big)+8np^{3}\exp\Big(\!\!-\!\tfrac{(q-1)^{2}}{2p}\Big)\;.

(3.66)

Proof.
Let
\overline{X}_{\!n}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},X_{n})
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, be a
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased RW on
\mathbf{T}
such that
X_{0}\!=\!\mathtt{o}
. By definition of BRWs, we get

{\bf P}\big(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\!n}\geq q\big|(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o}),\tau_{\infty}\big)\leq\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}P_{\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o}}\big(\big|\bm{|}\!\!|X_{|u_{m}|-1}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|\!\geq\!q\big)\leq nP_{\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o}}\big(\max_{0\leq k\leq\Gamma_{\!n}}\big|\bm{|}\!\!|X_{k}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|\!\geq\!q\,\big|\,\Gamma_{\!n}\big).

By Lemma
3.8
, we get
{\bf 1}_{\{\Gamma_{\!n}\leq p\}}{\bf P}\big(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\!n}\!\geq\!q\big|\tau_{\infty}\big)\!\leq\!8np^{3}\exp\big(\!\!-\!\tfrac{(q-1)^{2}}{2p}\big)
, which entails (
3.66
).
\hfill\square

As in Lemma
3.2

(ii)
, we next fix
\beta\!\in\!(\tfrac{1}{2}\!+\!\tfrac{1}{2\mathtt{b}},1)
, which is specified later and we defined the r.v.
R_{\beta}
as in (
3.7
). We then have

\forall p\!\in\!\mathbb{N},\quad\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}^{2}}S_{\mathtt{o}(p)}\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}(p)\bm{|}\!\!|\big|\leq R_{\beta}(1+p^{\beta})\quad\textrm{and}\quad{\bf P}(R_{\beta}\!\geq\!\lambda)\leq c_{1}\lambda^{-2\mathtt{b}},\quad\lambda\!\in\!(0,\infty)

(3.67)

where
c_{1}\!\in\!(0,\infty)
is a constant that only depends on
\nu
,
\mathtt{b}
and
\beta
. We then prove that if

2\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\!n}\leq q
and if
R_{\beta}\!\leq\!\lambda
, then

\Big(\!\!\!\!\max_{\hskip 8.19447ptm\in[\![0,n]\!]}\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}S_{Y_{u_{m}}}\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|>2\lambda(1+q^{\beta})\Big)\Longrightarrow\Big(\exists m\!\in\![\![0,n]\!]:u_{m}\!\in\!B_{q,\lambda,\beta}\Big)

(3.68)

where
B_{q,\lambda,\beta}
is the set of vertices of
\mathbf{T}\backslash L_{\mathtt{o}}
defined in Lemma
3.3
.

Proof.
Suppose that
m\!\in\![\![0,n]\!]
is such that

\alpha_{n}:=\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}S_{Y_{u_{m}}}\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|\!>\!2\lambda(1+q^{\beta})
. Since
R_{\beta}\!\leq\!\lambda
, we get
\alpha_{n}\!\geq\!\lambda(1+q^{\beta})+R_{\beta}(1+q^{\beta})
and thus

\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}(S_{Y_{u_{m}}}\!\!-\!S_{\mathtt{o}\wedge Y_{u_{m}}}\big)\!-\!\big(\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\wedge Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big)\big|+\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}S_{\mathtt{o}\wedge Y_{u_{m}}}\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\wedge Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|\!\geq\!\alpha_{n}\!>\!\lambda(1+q^{\beta})+R_{\beta}(1+q^{\beta}).

Note that there exists
u_{l}\!\in\![\![0,n]\!]
such that
\mathtt{o}\wedge Y_{u_{m}}\!=\!Y_{u_{l}}
. Since
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\!n}\!\leq\!q/2\!\leq\!q
, there exists
j\!\in\![\![0,q]\!]
such that

\mathtt{o}\wedge Y_{u_{m}}\!=\!\mathtt{o}(j)
and by definition of
R_{\beta}
, we get

\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}S_{\mathtt{o}\wedge Y_{u_{m}}}\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\wedge Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|\leq R_{\beta}(1+j^{\beta})\leq R_{\beta}(1+q^{\beta})

and thus

\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}(S_{Y_{u_{m}}}\!\!-\!S_{\mathtt{o}\wedge Y_{u_{m}}}\big)\!-\!\big(\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\wedge Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big)\big|\!>\!\lambda(1+q^{\beta})\;.

(3.69)

Now observe that
\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|\mathtt{o}\wedge Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|=\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\!-\!\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{l}}\bm{|}\!\!|\leq 2\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\!n}\leq q
. Thus (
3.69
) implies that
u_{m}\!\in\!B_{q,\lambda,\beta}
, which completes the proof of (
3.68
).
\hfill\square

We next set
V_{m}\!=\!V_{m}(\tau_{\infty})
and
I_{m}\!=\!\min_{l\in[\![0,m]\!]}V_{l}
, where
V(\tau_{\infty})
is the Lukasiewicz path of
\tau_{\infty}
. As an easy consequence of the concatenation (
2.3
) in Definition
2.2
, the vertex
u_{n}
‘belongs’ to
\tau^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}}
where
k\!=\!-I_{n}+1
(here we mean that
u_{n}\!=\!(k)\ast v
for some

v\!\in\!\tau^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}}
). We next prove that there is a constant
c_{2}\!\in\!(0,\infty)
, which only depends on

\nu
,
\mathtt{b}
and
\beta
such that for all
p,q,r\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
and all
\lambda\!\in\!(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle{\bf P}\Big(\!\!\!\!\!\!\max_{\hskip 8.19447ptm\in[\![0,n]\!]}\big|\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{\nu}}S_{Y_{u_{m}}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u_{m}}\bm{|}\!\!|\big|\!>\!2\lambda(1+q^{\beta})\Big)

(3.70)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\bf P}\big(\Gamma_{\!n}\geq p\big)+8np^{3}e^{-\tfrac{(q-1)^{2}}{8p}}+{\bf P}(-I_{n}\!\geq\!r)+c_{2}qr\lambda^{-2\mathtt{b}}.

Proof.
Observe that

\displaystyle{\bf P}\big(\exists m\!\in\![\![0,n]\!]:u_{m}\!\in\!B_{q,\lambda,\beta}\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\bf P}\big(\exists k\!\leq\!\!-I_{n}\!+\!1,\,\exists u\!\in\!\tau^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}}:Y_{u}\!\in\!B_{q,\lambda,\beta}\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\bf P}(-I_{n}\geq r)+\sum_{1\leq k\leq r}{\bf P}\big(\exists u\!\in\!\tau^{{}_{(k)}}_{{}^{\!}}:\!Y_{u}\!\in\!B_{q,\lambda,\beta}\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\bf P}(-I_{n}\geq r)+c_{2}qr\lambda^{-2\mathtt{b}}.

by Lemma
3.3
. Then we get (
3.70
) thanks to (
3.66
), (
3.67
), and (
3.68
).
\hfill\square

We recall from (
2.54
) the definition of the slowly varying function
L
such that
b_{n}\!=\!n^{1/\alpha}L(n)
and we also recall that
a_{n}\!=\!n/b_{n}\!=\!n^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}}/L(n)
.
Recall from (
3.32
) in Proposition
3.17
that for all
\mathtt{b}_{1}\!>\!1
, there exists
c_{3}\!\in\!(0,\infty)
that only depends on
\mu
and
\mathtt{b}_{1}
such that
{\bf E}\big[\Gamma_{n}^{\mathtt{b}_{1}}]\leq c_{3}(a_{n})^{\mathtt{b}_{1}}
. Therefore,

\forall n,p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*},\quad{\bf P}\big(\Gamma_{n}\geq p\big)\leq c_{3}(a_{n}/p)^{\mathtt{b}_{1}}\;.

(3.71)

We next prove that there exists
c_{4}\!\in\!(0,\infty)
that only depends on
\mu
such that

\forall n,r\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*},\quad{\bf P}\big(-I_{n}\geq r\big)\leq 3e^{-c_{4}ra_{n}/n}.

(3.72)

Proof.
Recall the notation
T_{-r}\!=\!\inf\{m\!\in\!\mathbb{N}:V_{m}\!=\!-r\}
. Observe that
{\bf P}(-I_{n}\geq r)\!=\!{\bf P}(T_{-r}\!\leq\!n)
. By Markov inequality,
{\bf P}(T_{-r}\!\leq\!n)\!\leq\!e{\bf E}[\exp(-\tfrac{1}{n}T_{-r})]
. We next use the fact that
T_{-r}

is a sum of
r
i.i.d. r.v.s distributed as
T_{-1}
. Then,
{\bf E}[\exp(-\tfrac{1}{n}T_{-r})]\!=\!\varphi_{\mu}(e^{-1/n})^{r}
, where we recall from Lemma
2.32
that
\varphi_{\mu}
stands for the generating function of
T_{-1}
. By (
2.68
) in the same lemma, there is
c_{4}
such that
c_{4}(n^{1/\alpha}L(n))^{-1}\!\leq\!-\log\varphi_{\mu}(e^{-1/n})
, which entails (
3.72
).
\hfill\square

Since we assume that
\mathtt{b}>\frac{2\alpha}{\alpha-1}
, then
\big(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{1}{2\mathtt{b}}\big)+\tfrac{1}{2\mathtt{b}}\tfrac{\alpha+1}{\alpha-1}\!<\!1
. Thus we can find
\beta\!\in\!\big(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{1}{2\mathtt{b}},1\big)
and
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty)
such that

\beta+\tfrac{1}{2\mathtt{b}}\tfrac{\alpha+1}{\alpha-1}(1+\varepsilon)+\varepsilon+\tfrac{3\varepsilon}{1+2\varepsilon}\!<\!1.

(3.73)

Then by Potter’s bounds (see e.g. Bingham, Goldies & Teugel
[
9
, Theorem 1.5.6]
), there exists
c_{5}\!\in\!(1,\infty)
that only depends on
\mu
and
\varepsilon
such that

\forall n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*},\quad c^{-1}_{5}n^{(1-\frac{{}_{1}}{\alpha})(1-\varepsilon)}\leq a_{n}\!=\!\tfrac{1}{L(n)}n^{1-\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{\alpha}}}\leq c_{5}n^{(1-\frac{{}_{1}}{\alpha})(1+\varepsilon)}.

(3.74)

We then set

q=n^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{{}_{1}}{\alpha})(1+2\varepsilon)},\quad p=q^{2\cdot\frac{{1+\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}}{{1+2\varepsilon}}},\quad r=q^{\frac{2}{{\alpha-1}}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lambda=q^{\frac{1}{{2\mathtt{b}}}\frac{{\alpha+1}}{{\alpha-1}}(1+\varepsilon)}\;.

Then we check that
\frac{1}{p}a_{n}\!\leq\!c_{5}n^{-\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{{}_{1}}{\alpha})\varepsilon}

and that
\frac{1}{n}ra_{n}\!\geq\!\frac{1}{c_{5}}n^{\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon}
. We fix
\mathtt{b}_{1}\!>\!1
, and we check that there are positive constants
c_{6},\ldots,c_{11}
that only depend on
\mu
,
\nu
,
\mathtt{b}
,
\beta
,
\mathtt{b}_{1}
and
\varepsilon
such that

\displaystyle\mathtt{RHS}(\ref{summarybad})\leq c_{6}n^{-\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{{}_{1}}{\alpha})\mathtt{b}_{1}\varepsilon}+c_{7}n^{c_{8}}\exp(-c_{9}n^{\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon(1-\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{\alpha}})})+3\!\exp(-c_{10}n^{\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon})+c_{11}q^{-\frac{\alpha+1}{\alpha-1}\varepsilon}.

Here we used (
3.71
) and (
3.72
). Thus there are
c_{0}\!\in\!(0,\infty)
and
\varepsilon_{0}\!\in\!(0,1)
, which only depend on
\mu
,
\nu
,
\mathtt{b}
,
\beta
,
\mathtt{b}_{1}
and
\varepsilon
, such that
\mathtt{RHS}(\ref{summarybad})\!\leq\!c_{0}n^{-\varepsilon_{0}}
.

Next, by (
3.73
) and (
3.74
),
there are positive constants
c_{12},c_{13}
, which only depend on
\mu
,
\nu
,
\mathtt{b}
,
\beta
,
\mathtt{b}_{1}
and
\varepsilon
,
such that

2\lambda(1+q^{\beta})\!\leq\!c_{12}q^{\beta+\frac{1}{{2\mathtt{b}}}\frac{{\alpha+1}}{{\alpha-1}}(1+\varepsilon)}\!\!\leq\!c_{12}q^{1-\varepsilon-\tfrac{3\varepsilon}{1+2\varepsilon}}\!\!\leq\!c_{12}q^{-\varepsilon}q^{\tfrac{1-\varepsilon}{1+2\varepsilon}}\!\!=\!c_{12}q^{-\varepsilon}n^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{{}_{1}}{\alpha})(1-\varepsilon)}\!\!\leq\!c_{13}q^{-\varepsilon}a_{n}^{\!\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{2}}}.

This proves that
2\lambda(1+q^{\beta})\!\leq\!c^{\prime}_{0}n^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\sqrt{a_{n}}
where we have set

\varepsilon_{1}\!=\!\frac{1}{2}(1\!-\!\frac{1}{\alpha})(1+2\varepsilon)\varepsilon
and
c^{\prime}_{0}\!=\!c_{13}
. This implies that
\mathtt{LHS}(\ref{quantclose})\!\leq\!\mathtt{LHS}(\ref{summarybad})\!\leq\!\mathtt{RHS}(\ref{summarybad})\!\leq\!c_{0}n^{-\varepsilon_{0}}
, which completes the proof of (
3.65
).

\hfill\blacksquare

4
Proof of Theorem
1.1

4.1
Limit of the
\tau_{n}
-indexed RW and its snakes.

Before stating the main result of the section, let us introduce some notation.
We fix
\alpha\!\in\!(1,2]
and we fix an offspring distribution
\mu
that satisfies (
1.3
).
Here
\nu
is a supercritical offspring distribution whose mean is denoted by
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
. We assume that there is
\mathtt{b}\!\in\!(\frac{2\alpha}{\alpha-1},\infty)
such that
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k^{1+2\mathtt{b}}\nu(k)\!<\!\infty
.
Let
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
be an invariant GW(
\nu
)-tree as in Definition
2.21
.

Let
\tau^{\prime}:\Omega\to\mathbb{T}
be an (auxiliary) a.s. finite random ordered rooted tree. Recall that
V(\tau^{\prime})
is its Lukasiewicz path, that

H(\tau^{\prime})
is its height process (Definition
2.2
) and that
C(\tau^{\prime})
is its contour process (Definition
2.4
).
We then denote by

\bm{\Theta}(\tau^{\prime})\!=\!\big(\tau^{\prime},\varnothing;\overline{Y}_{\!u}(\tau^{\prime})\!=\!(\mathbf{T},Y_{u}(\tau^{\prime})),u\!\in\!\tau^{\prime}\big)
a
\tau^{\prime}
-indexed and
\mathbf{T}
-valued
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased BRW such that conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
and
\tau^{\prime}
,
\bm{\Theta}(\tau^{\prime})
has the
law
Q^{(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})}_{(\tau^{\prime},\varnothing)}
as in Definition
2.25
with
\lambda\!=\!\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
.
We associate with
\bm{\Theta}(\tau^{\prime})
two
\mathbb{R}
-valued and
\tau^{\prime}
-indexed BRWs

M(\tau^{\prime}):=\big(\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{u}(\tau^{\prime})\bm{|}\!\!|\big)_{u\in\tau^{\prime}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathcal{M}(\tau^{\prime}):=\big(S_{Y_{u}(\tau^{\prime})}\big)_{u\in\tau^{\prime}}\;,

where we recall from (
3.3
) the definition of the harmonic coordinates
(S_{x})_{x\in\mathbf{T}}
.
According to Definition
2.8
, we introduce the following four
\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})
-valued continuous processes:

-

\big(W_{s}(\tau^{\prime};\cdot)\big)_{\!s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is the contour snake of the BRW

M(\tau^{\prime})
;

-

\big(W_{s}^{*}(\tau^{\prime};\cdot)\big)_{\!s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is the height snake of the BRW
M(\tau^{\prime})
;

-

\big(\mathcal{W}_{s}(\tau^{\prime};\cdot)\big)_{\!s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is the contour snake of the BRW
\mathcal{M}(\tau^{\prime})
;

-

\big(\mathcal{W}_{s}^{*}(\tau^{\prime};\cdot)\big)_{\!s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is the height snake of the BRW
\mathcal{M}(\tau^{\prime})
.

In this section, the auxiliary tree
\tau^{\prime}
shall be distributed as
\tau_{n}
and
\tau_{\geq n}
, which are defined as follows:

\tau_{\geq n}\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{=}\tau\quad\textrm{under ${\bf P}(\,\cdot\,|\,\#\tau\!\geq\!n)$}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\tau_{n}\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{=}\tau\quad\textrm{under ${\bf P}(\,\cdot\,|\,\#\tau=n)$,}

(4.1)

where
\tau
is a GW(
\mu
)-tree (Definition
2.6

(\textbf{a})
). We note that the definition of
\tau_{n}
only makes sense when
{\bf P}(\#\tau\!=\!n)\!>\!0
, which is the case for all sufficiently large
n
since
\mu
is assumed to be aperiodic.

We finally introduce the processes
(X^{\prime\prime},H^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime\prime})
and
(X^{\prime},H^{\prime},W^{\prime})
defined on
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},{\bf P})
, whose laws
are described as follows.

-

The process
(X^{\prime\prime}\!,H^{\prime\prime})
is distributed as
(X,H(X))
under
\mathbf{N}(dX\,|\,\zeta\!>\!1)
and conditionally given
H^{\prime\prime}\!
,
W^{\prime\prime}
is the one-dimensional Brownian snake with lifetime process
H^{\prime\prime}
(see Proposition
2.41
).

-

The process
(X^{\prime}\!,H^{\prime})
is distributed as
(X,H(X))
under
\mathbf{N}(dX\,|\,\zeta\!=\!1)
and conditionally given
H^{\prime}\!
,
W^{\prime}
is the one-dimensional Brownian snake with lifetime process
H^{\prime}
(see Proposition
2.41
).

The goal of this section is to derive from Theorem
3.9
the following result that is the main argument of the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 4.1
.

We fix
\alpha\!\in\!(1,2]
. Let
\mu
satisfy (
1.3
).
Let
\tau_{n}
be as in (
4.1
) (i.e., namely, a GW(
\mu
)-tree conditioned to have
n
vertices).
Let
\nu
be a supercritical offspring distribution whose mean is denoted by
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
. We assume that there is
\mathtt{b}\!\in\!(\frac{2\alpha}{\alpha-1},\infty)
such that
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k^{1+2\mathtt{b}}\nu(k)\!<\!\infty
.
Let
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
be an invariant GW(
\nu
)-tree that is independent from
\tau_{n}
.
We recall the
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
from (
3.13
) and we recall
from (
3.6
) the definition of
\sigma_{\nu}
. We also recall the notation
\mathscr{Q}_{n}^{\prime}
from (
2.64
) in Theorem
2.30

(iv)
. We keep the above notation.
Then, conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
, the following convergence

\displaystyle\Big(\mathscr{Q}_{n}^{\prime},\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\!\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2n\cdot}(\tau_{n}),\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\!\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{*}_{n\cdot}(\tau_{n})\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

,

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{W}_{2n\cdot}(\tau_{n}),\!\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{W}^{*}_{n\cdot}(\tau_{n})\Big)

(4.2)

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}

\displaystyle\big(X^{\prime},H^{\prime},H^{\prime},\widehat{W}^{\prime},\widehat{W}^{\prime},\widehat{W}^{\prime},\widehat{W}^{\prime}\big)

a.s. holds weakly on
\mathbf{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})\!\times\!\mathbf{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R})^{6}
equipped with the product topology.

Proof.
We set
Z^{\prime\prime}_{n}\!=\!\big(\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{\lfloor n\cdot\rfloor}(\tau_{\geq n})
,
\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}H_{n\cdot}(\tau_{\geq n})
,
\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{*}_{n\cdot}(\tau_{\geq n})
,
\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{W}^{*}_{n\cdot}(\tau_{\geq n})\big)
for all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
and we also set

Z^{\prime\prime}

=

(X^{\prime\prime},H^{\prime\prime},\widehat{W}^{\prime\prime},\widehat{W}^{\prime\prime})
. We first prove that
conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
, the following convergence

Z^{\prime\prime}_{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}Z^{\prime\prime}

(4.3)

holds weakly on
\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})\times\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})^{3}
.

Indeed
, by easy arguments, Theorem
3.9
entails that conditionally on
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
, the following convergence

\displaystyle\Big(\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{\lfloor(\mathfrak{g}_{n}+n\cdot)\wedge\mathfrak{d}_{n}\rfloor}(\tau_{\infty})\!\!\!\!\!

,

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}H_{(\mathfrak{g}_{n}+n\cdot)\wedge\mathfrak{d}_{n}}(\tau_{\infty}),\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{*}_{(\mathfrak{g}_{n}+n\cdot)\wedge\mathfrak{d}_{n}}(\tau_{\infty}),\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{W}^{*}_{(\mathfrak{g}_{n}+n\cdot)\wedge\mathfrak{d}_{n}}(\tau_{\infty})\Big)

(4.4)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\Big(X_{(\mathbf{g}_{1}+\cdot)\wedge\mathbf{d}_{1}},H_{(\mathbf{g}_{1}+\cdot)\wedge\mathbf{d}_{1}},\widehat{W}_{(\mathbf{g}_{1}+\cdot)\wedge\mathbf{d}_{1}},\widehat{W}_{(\mathbf{g}_{1}+\cdot)\wedge\mathbf{d}_{1}}\Big)

holds weakly on
\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})\!\times\!\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})^{3}
. Then, as a consequence of the definition of
\mathfrak{g}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{n}
, the left member of (
4.4
) has the same joint law with
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
as
(Z^{\prime\prime}_{n},(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o}))
.
Similarly, the definition of
\mathbf{g}_{1}
and
\mathbf{d}_{1}
implies that the right member of (
4.4
) is distributed as
Z^{\prime\prime}
(see Remark
2.28
). Thus, (
4.4
) is equivalent to (
4.3
).
\hfill\square

To complete the proof of the theorem, we next want to use Lemma
2.31
. To that end, we fix
s\!\in\!(0,1)
and we set

s_{n}\!=\!\lfloor ns\rfloor
. We also set

-

Z^{\prime\prime}_{n}(s)\!=\!\big(\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{\lfloor n\cdot\rfloor\wedge s_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n}),\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}H_{(n\cdot)\wedge s_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n}),\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{*}_{(n\cdot)\wedge s_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n}),\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{W}^{*}_{(n\cdot)\wedge s_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n})\big)
,

-

Z^{\prime}_{n}(s)\!=\!\big(\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{\lfloor n\cdot\rfloor\wedge s_{n}}(\tau_{n}),\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}H_{(n\cdot)\wedge s_{n}}(\tau_{n}),\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{*}_{(n\cdot)\wedge s_{n}}(\tau_{n}),\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{W}^{*}_{(n\cdot)\wedge s_{n}}(\tau_{n})\big)
,

-

Z^{\prime\prime}(s)\!=\!(X^{\prime\prime}_{\cdot\wedge s},H^{\prime\prime}_{\cdot\wedge s},\widehat{W}^{\prime\prime}_{\cdot\wedge s},\widehat{W}^{\prime\prime}_{\cdot\wedge s})
and
Z^{\prime}(s)\!=\!(X^{\prime}_{\cdot\wedge s},H^{\prime}_{\cdot\wedge s},\widehat{W}^{\prime}_{\cdot\wedge s},\widehat{W}^{\prime}_{\cdot\wedge s})
.

We easily deduce from (
2.52
) that
{\bf E}[F(Z^{\prime\prime}(s))D_{s}(X^{\prime\prime}_{s})]\!=\!{\bf E}[F(Z^{\prime}(s))]
for all measurable nonnegative functions
F
. We next deduce from (
4.3
) that conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
,
Z^{\prime\prime}_{n}(s)\!\to\!Z^{\prime\prime}(s)
weakly on the appropriate spaces.
Next denote by
(u_{k})_{0\leq l<\#\tau_{\geq n}}
the vertices of
\tau_{\geq n}
listed in lexicographical order.
We observe that the subtree
\{u_{l}\,;0\!\leq\!l\!\leq\!s_{n}\}
is measurable with respect to
V_{\cdot\wedge s_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n})
. Thus,
Z^{\prime\prime}_{n}(s)
is a measurable function of
V_{\cdot\wedge s_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n})
and of an independent r.v.
U_{n}
. Consequently Lemma
2.31
applies and conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
the joint convergence

Z^{\prime}_{n}(s)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}Z^{\prime}(s)

(4.5)

holds weakly on
\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})\times\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})^{3}
.

We next derive the joint convergence of the related contour process and contour snakes via the time-change discussed in
Remark
2.5

(\textbf{d})
. Namely, there is an increasing continuous bijection
\phi_{\tau_{n}}\!:\![0,n]\!\to\![0,2n]
such that
H_{r}(\tau_{n})\!=\!C_{\phi_{\tau_{n}}(r)}(\tau_{n})
,
r\!\in\![0,n]
.
Moreover, deduce from (
2.9
) that

\max_{r\in[0,1]}|\frac{1}{2n}\phi_{\tau_{n}}(nr)\!-\!r|\!\leq\!3\tfrac{a_{n}}{n}\max_{s\in[0,1]}\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}H_{nr}(\tau_{n})
, which tends to
0
in
{\bf P}
-probability since
a_{n}/n\!\to\!0
.
Therefore, if for all
s\!\in\![0,1]
we set
\varphi_{n}(s)\!=\!\frac{1}{n}\phi^{-1}_{\tau_{n}}(2ns)
(here,
\phi^{-1}_{\tau_{n}}
stands for the inverse of
\phi_{\tau_{n}}
), then we get

(i)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\max_{s\in[0,1]}\big|\varphi_{n}(s)\!-\!s\big|=0
in
{\bf P}
-probability,

(ii)

C_{2ns}(\tau_{n})\!=\!H_{n\varphi_{n}(s)}(\tau_{n})
,

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2ns}(\tau_{n})\!=\!\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{*}_{n\varphi_{n}(s)}(\tau_{n})
,
\widehat{W}_{2ns}(\tau_{n})\!=\!\widehat{W}^{*}_{n\varphi_{n}(s)}(\tau_{n})
for all
s\!\in\![0,1]
.

This combined with Lemma
3.13
shows that (
4.5
) implies
for all
s\!\in\!(0,1)
that conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
the joint convergence

\Big(Z^{\prime}_{n}(s),\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}C_{2n(\cdot\wedge s)}(\tau_{n}),\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2n(\cdot\wedge s)}(\tau_{n}),\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{W}_{2n(\cdot\wedge s)}(\tau_{n})\Big)\!\!\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}(Z^{\prime}(s),H^{\prime}_{\cdot\wedge s},\widehat{W}^{\prime}_{\cdot\wedge s},\widehat{W}^{\prime}_{\cdot\wedge s})

(4.6)

holds weakly on
\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})\!\times\!\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})^{6}
.

To complete the proof of the theorem, we next use a symmetry property of contour processes and contour snakes. More precisely, denote by
\widetilde{\tau}_{n}
the mirror image of
\tau_{n}
. Namely there exists a height preserving and
\leq_{\mathtt{lex}}
-increasing bijection
u\!\in\!\tau_{n}\mapsto\widetilde{u}\!\in\!\widetilde{\tau}_{n}
such that
\widetilde{(v_{k})}=\widetilde{v}_{2(n-1)-k}
, where
(v_{k})_{0\leq k\leq 2(n-1)}
and
(\widetilde{v}_{k})_{0\leq k\leq 2(n-1)}
stand for the vertices of resp.
\tau_{n}
and
\widetilde{\tau}_{n}
in increasing contour order. Then, for all
s\!\in\![0,n]
, we get

C_{2(n-s)}(\tau_{n})\!=\!C_{2(s-1)_{+}}(\widetilde{\tau}_{n}),\quad\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2(n-s)}(\tau_{n})\!=\!\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2(s-1)_{+}}(\widetilde{\tau}_{n}),\quad\widehat{W}_{2(n-s)}(\tau_{n})\!=\!\widehat{W}_{2(s-1)_{+}}(\widetilde{\tau}_{n}).

Since
\widetilde{\tau}_{n}
and
\tau_{n}
have the same law, (
4.6
) then implies for all
s\!\in\!(0,1)
that conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
, the joint convergence

\Big(\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2n((1-\cdot)\wedge s)}(\tau_{n}),\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{W}_{2n((1-\cdot)\wedge s)}(\tau_{n})\Big)\!\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}(\widehat{W}^{\prime}_{\cdot\wedge s},\widehat{W}^{\prime}_{\cdot\wedge s})\;.

holds weakly on
\mathbf{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})^{2}
.
If
s\!\in\!(\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{2}},1)
, the previous limit combined with (
4.6
) imply that conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
the laws of the processes
\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2n\cdot}(\tau_{n})
and

\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{W}_{2n\cdot}(\tau_{n})
are tight on
\mathbf{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
.
Then recall from Theorem
2.30

(iv)
that the laws of the processes
\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{\lfloor n\cdot\rfloor}(\tau_{n})
,
\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}H_{n\cdot}(\tau_{n})
and
\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}C_{2n\cdot}(\tau_{n})
are tight on resp.
\mathbf{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
and
\mathbf{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
.
Since
s
can be chosen arbitrarily close to
1
in (
4.6
), the processes that are weak limits of the previous ones have
necessarily the same finite-dimensional marginals as
(X^{\prime},H^{\prime},H^{\prime},\widehat{W}^{\prime},\widehat{W}^{\prime})
. It therefore
shows that conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
the following convergence

\Big(\mathscr{Q}_{n}^{\prime},\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\!\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2n\cdot}(\tau_{n}),\tfrac{1}{\sigma_{\nu}\!\sqrt{a_{n}}}\widehat{W}_{2n\cdot}(\tau_{n})\Big)\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\big(X^{\prime},H^{\prime},H^{\prime},\widehat{W}^{\prime},\widehat{W}^{\prime}\big)

(4.7)

holds weakly on
\mathbf{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})\!\times\!\mathbf{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R})^{4}
. Now observe that for all
s\!\in\![0,1]
,

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{*}_{ns}(\tau_{n})\!=\!\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2n\varphi^{-1}_{n}(s)}(\tau_{n})\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widehat{W}^{*}_{ns}(\tau_{n})\!=\!\widehat{W}_{2n\varphi^{-1}_{n}(s)}(\tau_{n})\;.

where
\varphi^{-1}_{n}\!:\![0,1]\!\to[0,1]
stands for the inverse of
\varphi_{n}
.
From the above-mentioned uniform convergence of the
\varphi_{n}
, we easily prove that

\lim_{n\to\infty}

\max_{s\in[0,1]}\big|\varphi^{-1}_{n}(s)\!-\!s\big|

\!=\!0
in
{\bf P}
-probability and we finally derive the convergence (
4.2
) from (
4.7
) and Lemma
3.13
.
This completes the proof of the theorem.
\hfill\blacksquare

4.2
Proof of Theorem
1.1

We keep the notation and the assumptions of Theorem
4.1
. In particular
\tau_{n}
has the same law as a GW(
\mu
)-tree
\tau
under
{\bf P}(\,\cdot\,|\,\#\tau\!=\!n)
and
\bm{\Theta}(\tau_{n})\!=\!\big(\tau_{n},\varnothing;\overline{Y}_{\!u}\!=\!(\mathbf{T},Y_{u}),u\!\in\!\tau_{n}\big)
is a
\tau_{n}
-indexed and
\mathbf{T}
-valued
\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
-biased BRW such that conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
and
\tau_{n}
,
\bm{\Theta}(\tau_{n})
has the
law
Q^{(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})}_{(\tau_{n},\varnothing)}
in Definition
2.25
with
\lambda\!=\!\mathtt{m}_{\nu}
. Recall that
\tau_{n}
is independent of
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
, which is an invariant GW(
\nu
)-tree. We set

\mathtt{m}_{n}\!=\!\sum_{v\in\tau_{n}}\delta_{v},\quad\mathcal{R}_{n}=\big\{Y_{v}\,;\,v\!\in\!\tau_{n}\big\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{occ}}^{(n)}=\sum_{v\in\tau_{n}}\delta_{Y_{v}}.

We want to prove conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})

a joint convergence of the following rescaled pointed measured metric spaces equipped with their graph distances
d_{\mathtt{gr}}
:

\bm{\tau}_{n}:=\big(\tau_{n},\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}d_{\mathtt{gr}},\varnothing,\tfrac{1}{n}\mathtt{m}_{n}\big)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\bm{\mathcal{R}}_{n}:=\big(\mathcal{R}_{n},\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}d_{\mathtt{gr}},\mathtt{o},\tfrac{1}{n}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{occ}}^{(n)}\big)

weakly with respect to the Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov convergence.

To that end, we first prove a convergence of the snake-trees associated with the contour snake of the relative heights of
\bm{\Theta}(\tau_{n})
. Namely, recall that
W(\tau_{n};\cdot)
stands for the contour snake associated with the
\mathbb{R}
-valued BRW
\big(\bm{|}\!\!|Y_{v}(\tau_{n})\bm{|}\!\!|\big)_{v\in\tau_{n}}
as in Definition
2.8
. To simplify notation, we set for all
s\!\in\![0,1]
and
r\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

C^{(n)}_{s}\!=\!\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}C_{2ns}(\tau_{n}),\quad W^{(n)}_{s}(r)=\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}W_{2ns}(\tau_{n};a_{n}r).

Then
(W^{(n)}_{s}(\cdot))_{s\in[0,1]}
is a snake with lifetime process
(C^{(n)}_{s})_{s\in[0,1]}
(as in Definition
2.37
).
We recall the notation
d_{C^{(n)},W^{(n)}}
for the snake-pseudometric associated with
(C^{(n)},W^{(n)})

(Definition
2.42
). We also recall from (
2.76
) that
d_{C^{(n)}}
stands for the tree-pseudometric associated with
C^{(n)}
. By combining Theorem
4.1
, Proposition
2.45
and Lemma
2.46
, we get
conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
the following joint convergence

\big(d_{C^{(n)}},d_{C^{(n)},W^{(n)}})\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}(d_{H},d_{H,W})

(4.8)

weakly on
\mathbf{C}([0,1]^{2},\mathbb{R})^{2}
. Here
H
is the
\alpha
-stable height process under
\mathbf{N}(\,\cdot\,|\,\zeta\!=\!1)
and
W
is a one-dimensional Brownian snake with lifetime process
H
as in Proposition
2.41
.
Then to simplify, we denote by

\widetilde{\bm{\tau}}_{n}\!=\!(T_{C^{(n)}},d_{C^{(n)}},\mathtt{r}_{C^{(n)}},\mathtt{m}_{C^{(n)}})
the real tree coded by the function
C^{(n)}
as in Example
2.36
. By Remark
2.47
, we get that

{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}\big(\bm{\tau}_{n},\widetilde{\bm{\tau}}_{n}\big)\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}0\;.

(4.9)

To simplify, we also denote the snake-tree coded by the snake-pseudometric
d_{C^{(n)},W^{(n)}}
by

\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{(n)}_{\!\!\mathtt{sna}}=\big(\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{\mathtt{sna}},d^{(n)}_{\mathtt{sna}},\mathtt{r}^{(n)}_{\mathtt{sna}},\mathtt{m}^{(n)}_{\mathtt{sna}}\big).

(See Definition
2.44
).
By Proposition
2.35
, the limits (
4.8
) and (
4.9
) imply that conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
the joint convergence

\big(\bm{\tau}_{n}\,,\,\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{(n)}_{\!\!\mathtt{sna}}\big)\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\big((T_{H},d_{H},\mathtt{r}_{H},\mathtt{m}_{H}),(T_{H,W},d_{H,W},\mathtt{r}_{H,W},\mathtt{m}_{H,W})\big)

(4.10)

holds weakly with respect to Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov convergence. Here
(T_{H},d_{H},\mathtt{r}_{H},\mathtt{m}_{H})
is the real tree coded by
H
which is the
\alpha
-stable tree and
(T_{H,W},d_{H,W},\mathtt{r}_{H,W},\mathtt{m}_{H,W})
is the snake-tree coded by the snake-pseudometric
d_{H,W}
(Definition
2.44
) which is the
\alpha
-stable Brownian cactus.

We next prove that
\bm{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
is close to
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{(n)}_{\!\!\mathtt{sna}}
thanks to the results discussed in Section
2.8
.
To that end, we introduce the following.

-
We denote by
(\widetilde{\tau}_{n},d_{\widetilde{\tau}_{n}})

the real tree obtained by joining adjacent vertices in
\tau_{n}
by a unit length segment (recall that we suppose here that
\tau_{n}\!\subset\!\widetilde{\tau}_{n}
so that
d_{\widetilde{\tau}_{n}}
extend the graph distance).

-
Recall from (
2.84
) that
\widetilde{v}\!:\![0,2n]\!\to\!\widetilde{\tau}_{n}
stands for the contour exploration of
\widetilde{\tau}_{n}
(namely
(\widetilde{v}(k))_{0\leq k\leq 2(n-1)}
are the vertices of
\tau_{n}
in contour order and
\widetilde{v}
is a
‘linear’ extension of it).

-
We denote by
(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}},d_{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}})
the real tree obtained by joining adjacent vertices in
\mathbf{T}
by a unit length segment. We also assume that
\mathbf{T}\!\subset\!\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}
so that
d_{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}
extends the graph distance.

-
Recall from (
2.85
) that
\widetilde{Y}\!:\!\widetilde{\tau}_{n}\!\to\!\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}
is the ‘linear’ extension of the BRW
(Y_{v})_{v\in\tau_{n}}
.

We set
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}_{n}\!=\!(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{n},\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}d_{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}},\mathtt{o},\tfrac{1}{2n}\widetilde{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathtt{o}cc})
where
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{n}\!=\!\{\widetilde{Y}_{\sigma}\,;\,\sigma\!\in\!\widetilde{\tau}_{n}\}\!=\!\{\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(s)}\,;\,s\!\in\![0,2n]\}
and
\widetilde{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathtt{occ}}
is the occupation measure of
s\mapsto\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(s)}
as defined by (
2.86
). We then set for all
s,s^{\prime}\!\in\![0,1]
:

d_{n}(s,s^{\prime})\!=\!\tfrac{\sigma_{\nu}}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}d_{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}\big(\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(2ns)},\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{v}(2ns^{\prime})}\big)\;,

which is a tree-pseudometric.
Recall from (
2.89
) that
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}_{n}
is isometric to the pointed measured compact real tree yielded by the pseudometric
d_{n}
. Then (
2.87
) implies that conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
, we have the weak convergence

{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}\big(\bm{\mathcal{R}}_{n},\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}_{n}\big)\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}0\;.

(4.11)

We next prove that
d_{n}
and the snake-pseudometric
d_{C^{(n)},W^{(n)}}
are close thanks to
Lemma
2.49
, which applies here thanks to Lemma
2.20
. We thus get the following bound:

{\bf P}\Big(\max_{s,s^{\prime}\in[0,1]}\big|d_{n}(s,s^{\prime})\!-\!d_{C^{(n)},W^{(n)}}(s,s^{\prime})\big|>2\sigma_{\nu}a_{n}^{\!-\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{4}}}\,\Big|\,(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})\Big)\leq\mathtt{m}_{\nu}^{2}n^{3}\!\exp\!\big(\!-\!a_{n}^{1/4}\log\mathtt{m}_{\nu}\big).

Since
a_{n}
is regularly varying with a positive exponent, this thus entails that conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
the following joint convergence

\big(d_{C^{(n)}},d_{C^{(n)},W^{(n)}},d_{n})\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}(d_{H},d_{H,W},d_{H,W})

(4.12)

holds weakly on
\mathbf{C}([0,1]^{2},\mathbb{R})^{3}
. By Proposition
2.35
, it entails that
conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
the following joint convergence

\big(\bm{\tau}_{n}\,,\,\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{R}}}_{n}\big)\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\big((T_{H},d_{H},\mathtt{r}_{H},\mathtt{m}_{H}),(T_{H,W},d_{H,W},\mathtt{r}_{H,W},\mathtt{m}_{H,W})\big)

(4.13)

holds weakly with respect to Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov convergence. By (
4.11
), we thus have proved that
conditionally given
(\mathbf{T},\mathtt{o})
, the following joint convergence

\big(\bm{\tau}_{n}\,,\bm{\mathcal{R}}_{n}\big)\underset{n\to\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\big((T_{H},d_{H},\mathtt{r}_{H},\mathtt{m}_{H}),(T_{H,W},d_{H,W},\mathtt{r}_{H,W},\mathtt{m}_{H,W})\big)

(4.14)

holds weakly with respect to Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov convergence, which is the desired result.
\hfill\blacksquare

Appendix A
Proof of Lemma
2.27

We fix
\alpha\!\in\!(1,2]
and we recall that
X
is an
\alpha
-stable spectrally positive Lévy process whose law is characterized by (
2.45
). Recall that
H
is the corresponding height process as defined in (
2.46
). We also introduce the notation
S_{s}\!=\!\sup_{r\in[0,s]}X_{r}
,
s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
, for the supremum process of
X
. As a Lévy process,
X
enjoys a so-called
duality property
: for all
s\!\in\!(0,\infty)
, the time-reversed process
(\widehat{X}^{(s)}_{r})_{r\in[0,s]}
defined by

\widehat{X}_{s}^{(s)}=X_{s}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widehat{X}_{r}^{(s)}=X_{s}\!-\!X_{(s-r)-},\quad r\!\in\![0,s),

(1.1)

has the same law as
(X_{r})_{r\in[0,s]}
. Note that
\widehat{X}^{(s)}
is independent of the shifted process
(X_{r}^{(s)})_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
, which is also distributed as
X
and defined by

X_{r}^{(s)}=X_{s+r}-X_{s},\quad r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}.

(1.2)

Then,
H_{s}
is
{\bf P}
-a.s. the local time at level
0
at time
s
of the process
\widehat{S}^{(s)}\!-\!\widehat{X}^{(s)}
, where
\widehat{S}^{(s)}\!=\!\sup_{[0,r]}\widehat{X}^{(s)}
for all
r\!\in\![0,s]
. Our proof of Lemma
2.27
relies on this connection, which we explain in greater detail below.

Elementary results of fluctuation theory (see e.g. Bertoin
[
6
]
Chapter VI.1 and VII.1) yield that
S\!-\!X
is a strong Markov process in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and that
0
is regular for itself and
(0,\infty)
with respect to this Markov process. Thus, there exists a Markovian local time at
0
for
S\!-\!X
, which is a continuous and nondecreasing process
L\!=\!(L_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
. Moreover, as proved by D. & Le Gall
[
21
, Lemma 1.1.3]
, we can choose
L
such that the following limit holds in
{\bf P}
-probability for any
s\!\in\!\mathbb{R}_{+}
:

L_{s}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{s}{\bf 1}_{\{S_{r}<\varepsilon+X_{r}\}}\,dr.

(1.3)

Now, for
s\!\geq\!0
, let us denote by
\widehat{L}^{(s)}=(\widehat{L}_{r}^{(s)})_{r\in[0,s]}
the local time at
0
of
\widehat{S}^{(s)}\!-\!\widehat{X}^{(s)}
, where
\widehat{X}^{(s)}
is the time-reversed process as in (
1.1
). Comparing (
1.3
) with (
2.46
)
{\bf P}
-a.s. entails that
H_{s}\!=\!\widehat{L}_{s}^{(s)}
, so
H_{s}
and
L_{s}
have the same law by the duality property. We also denote by
H^{(s)}\!=\!(H_{r}^{(s)})_{r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
the height process of the shifted process
X^{(s)}
as in (
1.2
). Then, for all
s,r\!\in\!(0,\infty)
, D. & Le Gall
[
21
, Lemma 1.4.5]
provide the two following identities that we will use to prove Lemma
2.27
:

H_{s+r}-\inf_{[s,s+r]}H=H_{r}^{(s)}\quad\textrm{ and }\quad H_{s}-\inf_{[s,s+r]}H=\widehat{L}_{s\wedge\widehat{T}}^{(s)}\quad\textrm{${\bf P}$-a.s.},

(1.4)

where
\widehat{T}\!=\!\inf\{t\!\in\![0,s]:\widehat{X}_{t}^{(s)}\!>\!-\inf_{[0,r]}X^{(s)}\}
(with the convention
\inf\varnothing\!=\!\infty
).

Proof of Lemma
2.27
.
Let
s,r\!\in\!(0,\infty)
and let
J
be a r.v. that is distributed as
-\inf_{[0,r]}X
and independent of
X
. We set
T\!=\!\inf\{t\!\in\![0,s]:X_{t}\!>\!J\}
. Thanks to (
1.4
), we exactly need to show that
{\bf P}
-a.s.
L_{r}\!>\!0
and
L_{s\wedge T}\!>\!0
. Since
0
is regular for itself with respect to
S\!-\!X
, standard results about local times ensure that
{\bf P}
-a.s,
L_{t}\!>\!0
for all
t\!\in\!(0,\infty)
. Thus, we only have to prove that
{\bf P}
-a.s
T\!>\!0
, which holds if and only if
J\!>\!0
. But it is well-known that
{\bf P}
-a.s.
\inf_{[0,r]}X\!<\!0
(see e.g. Bertoin
[
6
, Theorem VII.1 and Corollary VII.5]
), which concludes the proof.
\hfill\blacksquare

Appendix B
Proof of Lemma
2.31

We keep the notation and the assumptions of Theorem
2.30
and of Lemma
2.31
.
To simplify notation,
denote by
\mathbf{D}_{1}
the space
\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})
. Product spaces are equipped with product topology and if
E^{\prime\prime}
is a Polish space, we denote by
\mathcal{M}_{1}(E^{\prime\prime})
the space of its Borel probability measures equipped with the topology of weak convergence.

We denote by
q_{n}(\cdot,d\mathtt{u}):\Omega\!\to\!\mathcal{M}_{1}(E)
a regular version of the conditional law of
U_{n}
given
\mathscr{G}
, We also denote by
\mathtt{Q}_{\geq n}(d\mathtt{v})
and
\mathtt{Q}_{n}(d\mathtt{v})
the laws of resp.
\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{\lfloor n\cdot\rfloor\wedge r_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n})
and
\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{\lfloor n\cdot\rfloor\wedge r_{n}}(\tau_{n})
. Note that
\mathtt{Q}_{n}(d\mathtt{v})\!=\!D^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{r_{n}}}(b_{n}\mathtt{v}(r^{\prime}_{n}))\,\mathtt{Q}_{\geq n}(d\mathtt{v})
by Theorem
2.30

(ii)
, where we have set here
r^{\prime}_{n}\!=\!r_{n}/n
. For all
\omega\!\in\!\Omega
, we next
denote by
Q_{\geq n}(\omega,d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z})
and
Q_{n}(\omega,d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z})
the law of
(\mathtt{v},Z_{n}(\mathtt{v},\mathtt{u}))
under
resp.
\mathtt{Q}_{\geq n}(d\mathtt{v})\otimes q_{n}(\omega,d\mathtt{u})
and
\mathtt{Q}_{n}(d\mathtt{v})\otimes q_{n}(\omega,d\mathtt{u})
.
Since
U_{n}
and
\mathscr{G}
are independent from

\tau_{\geq n}
and
\tau_{n}
, we easily check that

Q_{\geq n}(\cdot,d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z}):\Omega\!\to\!\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbf{D}_{1}\!\times\!E^{\prime})
and
Q_{n}(\cdot,d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z}):\Omega\!\to\!\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbf{D}_{1}\!\times\!E^{\prime})
are regular versions of the conditional law of resp.
M_{\geq n}
in (
2.65
) and
M_{n}
in (
2.66
) given
\mathscr{G}
.
We also denote by
Q(d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z})
the law of
(X^{{}_{(r)}}_{{}^{\!}},Z)
and Assumption (
2.65
) means that there is
\Omega_{0}\!\in\!\mathscr{G}
such that
{\bf P}(\Omega_{0})\!=\!1
and such that for all
\omega\!\in\!\Omega_{0}
,
Q_{\geq n}(\omega,d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z})\!\to\!Q(d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z})
in
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbf{D}_{1}\!\times\!E^{\prime})
.
Let
F
be a real-valued continuous function on
\mathbf{D}_{1}\!\times\!E^{\prime}
that is bounded by
\lVert F\rVert_{\infty}
. We fix
\omega\!\in\!\Omega_{0}
. By the construction above, we first get

u_{n}:=\int_{\mathbf{D}_{1}\times E^{\prime}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!Q_{n}(\omega,d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z})\,F(\mathtt{v},\mathtt{z})\!=\!\int_{\mathbf{D}_{1}\times E^{\prime}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!Q_{\geq n}(\omega,d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z})\,F(\mathtt{v},\mathtt{z})D^{{(n)}}_{{r_{n}}}\big(b_{n}\mathtt{v}(r^{\prime}_{n})\big)\;.

Then for all
c\!\in\!(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\Big|u_{n}-\int_{\mathbf{D}_{1}\times E^{\prime}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!Q_{\geq n}(\omega,d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z})\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!F(\mathtt{v},\mathtt{z})\big(c\!\wedge\!D^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{r_{n}}}\big(b_{n}\mathtt{v}(r^{\prime}_{n})\big)\big)\Big|

(2.1)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert F\rVert_{\infty}\,{\bf E}\Big[D^{{}_{(n)}}_{r_{n}}\!(V_{r_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n}))\,{\bf 1}_{\{D^{{}_{(n)}}_{r_{n}}(V_{r_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n}))\geq c\}}\Big]=:\eta_{n}(c)\;.

We next fix
x,y\!\in\!(0,\infty)
with
x\!\leq\!y
and we set
\varepsilon_{n}(x,y)\!:=\!\max_{p\in[\![\lfloor b_{n}x\rfloor,\lfloor b_{n}y\rfloor]\!]}\big|D^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{r_{n}}}(p)\!-\!D_{r}(p/b_{n})\big|
. We recall from (
2.62
) in Theorem
2.30

(iii)
that

\varepsilon_{n}(x,y)\to 0
as
n\!\to\!\infty
. Then we get

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbf{D}_{1}\times E^{\prime}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!Q_{\geq n}(\omega,d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z})F(\mathtt{v},\mathtt{z})\big(c\,\wedge\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!D^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{r_{n}}}\big(b_{n}\mathtt{v}(r^{\prime}_{n})\big)\big)-\int_{\mathbf{D}_{1}\times E^{\prime}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!Q_{\geq n}(\omega,d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z})F(\mathtt{v},\mathtt{z})\big(c\!\wedge\!D_{r}\big(\mathtt{v}(r^{\prime}_{n})\big)\big)\Big|

(2.2)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert F\rVert_{{}_{\infty}}\varepsilon_{n}(x,y)+2c\lVert F\rVert_{{}_{\infty}}{\bf P}\big(\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{r_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n})\notin\!(x,y)\big).

Since
c\wedge D_{r}(\cdot)
is bounded and since
X^{{}_{(r)}}_{{}^{\!}}
is almost surely continuous at
r
, we get

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{D}_{1}\times E^{\prime}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!Q_{\geq n}(\omega,d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z})F(\mathtt{v},\mathtt{z})\big(c\!\wedge\!D_{r}\big(\mathtt{v}(r^{\prime}_{n})\big)\big)=\int_{\mathbf{D}_{1}\times E^{\prime}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!Q(d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z})F(\mathtt{v},\mathtt{z})\big(c\!\wedge\!D_{r}\big(\mathtt{v}(r)\big)\big).

If we set
u_{\infty}\!=\!\int_{\mathbf{D}_{1}\times E^{\prime}}Q(d\mathtt{v}d\mathtt{z})F(\mathtt{v},\mathtt{z})D_{r}(\mathtt{v}(r))
, then we have proved for all
c,x,y\!\in\!(0,\infty)
that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}|u_{n}\!-\!u_{\infty}|\leq\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\eta_{n}(c)+2c\lVert F\rVert_{{}_{\infty}}\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\bf P}\big(\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{r_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n})\notin\!(x,y)\big)\\
+\lVert F\rVert_{{}_{\infty}}{\bf E}\Big[D_{r}\big(X^{{}_{(r)}}_{{}^{r}}\!\big){\bf 1}_{\big\{D_{r}\big(X^{{}_{(r)}}_{{}^{r}}\!\big)\geq c\big\}}\Big].

Note that
\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\bf P}\big(\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{r_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n})\notin\!(x,y)\big)\to 0
as
x\!\to\!0
and
y\!\to\!\infty

since
\tfrac{1}{b_{n}}V_{r_{n}}(\tau_{\geq n})\!\to\!X^{{}_{(r)}}_{{}^{r}}\!\in\!(0,\infty)
by (
2.65
) and an elementary argument.
It then implies that
u_{n}\!\to\!u_{\infty}
because that
\lim_{c\to\infty}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\eta_{n}(c)\!=\!0
by (
2.63
) Theorem
2.30

(iii)
.
\hfill\blacksquare

Appendix C
Proof of Proposition
3.5

We first prove the following lemma that is used in the proof of Proposition
3.5
and also
in Section
3.4
in the proof of Proposition
3.10
.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{G}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a filtration on
(\Omega,\mathscr{F})
. Let
c\!\in\!(0,\infty)
be a constant and let

(R_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of nonnegative r.v.s such that for all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
,

{\bf P}
-a.s.
R_{n}\leq c
and
{\bf P}
-a.s.
R_{n}\!\to\!0
. Let
(r_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be any sequence of integers.
Then
{\bf P}
-a.s.
{\bf E}[R_{n}|\mathcal{G}_{r_{n}}]\!\to\!0
.

Proof.
For all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, we set
\overline{R}_{p}\!=\!\sup_{n\geq p}R_{n}
. We observe that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\bf E}[R_{n}|\mathcal{G}_{r_{n}}]\!\leq\!\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p}|\mathcal{G}_{k}]
a.s. for all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
. So we only need to prove that a.s.
\lim_{p\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p}|\mathcal{G}_{k}]\!=\!0
. To that end denote by

\mathcal{G}_{\infty}
the
\sigma
-field generated by
\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{G}_{k}
and observe, by Doob’s
L^{1}
-martingale convergence, that a.s.
\lim_{k\to\infty}{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p}|\mathcal{G}_{k}]\!=\!{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p}|\mathcal{G}_{\infty}]
. Moreover by conditional dominated convergence we also get a.s.
\lim_{p\to\infty}{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p}|\mathcal{G}_{k}]\!=\!0
for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. Then there exists
\Omega_{0}\!\in\!\mathscr{F}
such that
{\bf P}(\Omega_{0})\!=\!1
and such that for all integers

p^{\prime}\geq p
and
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
and all
\omega\!\in\!\Omega_{0}
,

{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p^{\prime}}|\mathcal{G}_{k}](\omega)\!\leq\!{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p}|\mathcal{G}_{k}](\omega)
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p}|\mathcal{G}_{k}](\omega)\!=\!{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p}|\mathcal{G}_{\infty}](\omega)
and
\lim_{p\to\infty}{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p}|\mathcal{G}_{k}](\omega)\!=\!0
.
We fix
\omega\!\in\!\Omega_{0}
,
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,1)
. There is
k_{p,\varepsilon}(\omega)\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
such that

\sup_{k\geq k_{p,\varepsilon}(\omega)}{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p}|\mathcal{G}_{k}](\omega)\!\leq\!\varepsilon+{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p}|\mathcal{G}_{\infty}](\omega)
. Thus for all
p^{\prime}\!\geq\!p
,

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p^{\prime}}|\mathcal{G}_{k}](\omega)\leq\max_{k\leq k_{p,\varepsilon}(\omega)}{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p^{\prime}}|\mathcal{G}_{k}](\omega)+\varepsilon+{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p}|\mathcal{G}_{\infty}](\omega)
. Therefore,

\limsup_{p^{\prime}\to\infty}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p^{\prime}}|\mathcal{G}_{k}](\omega)\leq\varepsilon+{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p}|\mathcal{G}_{\infty}](\omega)\to\varepsilon
as
p\!\to\!\infty
. Since
\varepsilon
can be chosen arbitrarily small, this shows that
\lim_{p^{\prime}\to\infty}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\bf E}[\overline{R}_{p^{\prime}}|\mathcal{G}_{k}](\omega)\!=\!0
for all
\omega\!\in\!\Omega_{0}
, which entails the desired result.
\hfill\blacksquare

We now prove Proposition
3.5
. To that end, we recall the following: for all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
,
(\mathcal{F}_{n,k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a filtration on
(\Omega,\mathscr{F})
;

(D_{n,k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is a sequence of real valued r.v.s;
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of integers and
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We introduce the following notation

M_{n,k}\!=\!D_{n,1}+\ldots+D_{n,k},\quad Q_{n,k}\!=\!{\bf E}[D_{n,k}^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{n,k-1}],\quad V_{n,k}\!=\!Q_{n,1}+\ldots+Q_{n,k},

with
M_{n,0}\!=\!V_{n,0}\!=\!0
, and we make the following assumptions.

(a)

For all
n,k
,
D_{n,k}
is
\mathcal{F}_{n,k}
-measurable,
{\bf E}[D_{n,k}^{2}]\!<\!\infty
and
{\bf P}
-a.s.
{\bf E}[D_{n,k}|\mathcal{F}_{n,k-1}]\!=\!0
.

(b)

There exist a filtration
(\mathcal{G}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
on
(\Omega,\mathscr{F})
and a sequence of integers
(r_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\mathcal{F}_{n,0}\!=\!\mathcal{G}_{r_{n}}
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
.

(c)

For all
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,1)
,
{\bf P}
-a.s.
\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{1\leq k\leq s_{n}}{\bf E}[D_{n,k}^{2}{\bf 1}_{\{|D_{n,k}|>\varepsilon\}}|\mathcal{F}_{n,k-1}]=0
.

(d^{\prime})

For all
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,1)
,
{\bf P}
-a.s.
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\bf E}\big[{\bf 1}_{\{|V_{n,s_{n}}-s|>\varepsilon\}}\big|\mathcal{F}_{n,0}\big]\!=\!0
.

Remark C.2
.

First observe that

Assumptions
(a
-
d)
in Proposition
3.5
imply
(d^{\prime})
.

Indeed, Assumption
(d)
in Proposition
3.5
implies for all
